## INTRODUCTION {#cuaderno.3.0}

May you seek Christ: may you find Christ: may you love Christ. Three perfectly clear stages. Have you tried, at least, to live the first?1 We came to the Work because Christ came to meet us with those very attractive words: Tu, me sequeris,2 Follow me! Since then, each one of us will have gone through those three perfectly clear stages, though not necessarily in the same way. We can seek, find and love Christ with the innocence of John, with the sorrowful tears of Peter or with that serene depth that Paul acquired through many victorious battles fought for the Lord. In any case, whether we are at the beginning of the race, or already near the end with the faith kept,3 the love of God is what stirs us. Conversion is the task of a moment; sanctification is the work of a lifetime. The divine seed of charity, which God has sown in our souls, wants to grow, to express itself in action, to yield results which continually coincide with what God wants. Therefore, we must be ready to begin again, to find again – in new situations – the light and the stimulus of our first conversion.4

1 The Way, 382 2 Jn 21:22 3 cf 2 Tim 4:7 4 Christ is passing by, 58 10 Living in Christ

This Notebook has been prepared with the hope that it will help us in this very personal task of seeking, again and again, of finding once again – we have found him of whom Moses in the law and also the prophets wrote, Jesus of Nazareth5 – and of falling in love more deeply with our Lord. It contains a number of editorials from our internal publications. They are old words and new encouragement that, with the intercession of our Lady and St Joseph, will help us on the journey along the clear path we started in order to live in Christ.

5 Jn 1:45

## LIVING IN CHRIST {#cuaderno.3.1}

Beloved, it is God who establishes us with you in Christ, and has commissioned us; he has put his seal upon us and given us his Spirit in our hearts as a guarantee.1 St Thomas says, “We are confirmed in Christ in two ways: through grace and through glory. Through grace, in having been anointed with the grace of the Holy Spirit, who transforms us into members of Christ, into his anointed ones… While the union with Christ reached through glory, we only have at the moment in our hope. But this is certain hope because we firmly trust we shall reach eternal life.”2 Interior life, the growth of supernatural life in the soul, is a process that begins with that first identification with Christ through grace and which is consummated with the supreme, definitive configuration in the glory of heaven. It is in this that the dynamics of the life of grace consist: a spring of water welling up to eternal life.3 To promote this growth the Holy Spirit is given to us in our hearts, the spirit of charity that continually imprints on us the image of Christ, stamping us with his seal – the seal of the Cross. The Cross is the distinctive sign of the elect, the password that opens the gates of heaven to us, the sign of the children and servants of God. Do no harm… till

1 2 Cor 1:21-22 2 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad 2 Cor. lectura 1, 5 3 Jn 4:14 12 Living in Christ

we have sealed the servants of our God upon their foreheads.4 Great and inexpressible is the mystery of the divine action in the soul; we only manage to see it imperfectly. Nevertheless, from contemplating it we can get much energy, that will push us to follow the way of identification with Christ unwaveringly. “If fire, penetrating through the thickness of a bar of iron into its core, converts it into fire and transforms what was cold into a blaze and makes it glow brightly…, what is so strange about the transformation that the Holy Spirit works in the depths of the soul?”5

The foundation of our interior life: Divine Filiation Truly, truly, I say to you, unless one is born anew, he cannot see the kingdom of God.6 It is necessary to be born to the supernatural life, to be transformed into a new creature, the image of Christ, his member, a child of God and it is necessary to grow until we reach mature manhood, to the measure of the stature of the fullness of Christ.7 To obtain this regeneration for us, God assumed our nature. “Through Christ we ascend to a supernatural dignity. Nevertheless, we are not children of God in the same way he is, with no kind of distinction, but, rather, we are made similar to him through grace. He is the true co-eternal Son of the Father. We, through his goodness, are adopted sons.”8 Grace, in making us like Christ, the natural Son of God, makes us adopted sons, members of the society of the children of God, that is the Church. “No one is an adopted son unless he is united to and adheres to the natural Son.”9 This is a real, intimate union that shapes our whole

4 Rev 7:3 5 St Cyril of Jerusalem, Catecheses 17, 14 6 Jn 3:3 7 Eph 4:18 8 St Cyril of Alexandria, In Ioannem Ev. commentarium 1, 9 9 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Gal. lectura 3, 9

Living in Christ 13

ascetical struggle, to the point that the foundation of the spiritual life of the members of Opus Dei is the sense of their divine filiation.10 And if children, then heirs, heirs of God and fellow heirs with Christ, provided we suffer with him.11 It is purely out of goodness that God granted us the title and the reality of being his children. It gives us the right to inherit heaven. But this right depends on our suffering with him, on our being ready to use the means, so that the seed grace planted in our soul may develop. Is there anyone who, having a rich inheritance within his reach – a treasure, a precious pearl – would not use the means to possess it? Our divine filiation is the title for the inheritance, and the complete and total imitation of Christ is the way to come into its possession. That is why the soul who has received the impression of the image of Christ, the soul who lives on divine filiation, feels itself drawn along by an ardent and sincere, tender and at the same time profound desire to imitate Jesus Christ.12 The soul begins to live supernatural life fully, to respond truly to that calling of Christ, come, follow me,13 which for us has been made more specific – a divine vocation to dedicate ourselves to the service of God in the midst of the world seeking holiness in our own state. If the soul fights to brush aside the obstacles, if it knows how to make good use of the means God offers it, if it opens itself to grace, especially in the Holy Eucharist, the proper fruit of which is to effect our transformation in Christ, then, even in spite of its imperfections, the soul begins to live genuine supernatural life, reproducing in itself the life of Christ. It loves what Christ loves, and rejects what he reproves. It laughs or cries with our Lord. It works with love because Christ spent thirty years as a humble carpenter in Nazareth. It stops to converse with

10 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 8 11 Rom 8:17 12 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 8 13 Lk 18:22 14 Living in Christ

God, following the example of Christ who withdrew to pray. It practises poverty because he did not have a place to rest his head; humility, meekness and purity of heart; obedience, because our Lord erat subditus illis;14 sincerity… in a word, all the virtues of Jesus. It is not happy except when imitating him in everything, when following him everywhere. Now it can say with the Apostle: for to me to live is Christ;15 at the same time it longs to attract other souls to its way, as Christ did: I came to cast fire upon the earth, and would that it were already kindled!16

Need for union with Christ on the Cross “Life entails a certain movement. For it is said that self-movement means life. Therefore, life is precisely the root cause of movement in man. Now, man always moves towards something which is an end. Hence we also call life that which moves a man to act. In this sense Christ is also our life, in so far as the reason for our life and operations is Christ.”17 Our Lord is the source of the grace that impresses his image ever more deeply on our soul and he is also our only end. All we want is to conform ourselves to him. This is why we came to the Work. The apostles followed Jesus, they had seen his miracles, they had been witnesses to his power over the multitudes. They had shared in the power of their Master: even the demons are subject to us in your name.18 They felt strong with the strength of Christ and they loved him in spite of their wretchedness. Their affection for Jesus even led them to approach death stoutly: Let us also go, that we may die with him.19 They were truly determined, without second thoughts, to give their

14 Lk 2:51 15 Phil 1:21 16 Lk 12:49 17 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Phil. lectura 1, 3 18 Lk 10:17 19 Jn 11:16

Living in Christ 15

lives for their Master. And yet, what happened on the night of Maundy Thursday? The prophet had foretold: strike the shepherd, that the sheep may be scattered.20 Peter himself, the firm rock on which the Church would be built, trembled before a mere maid-servant and denied the Lord to whom, just before, he had promised to follow to death. What happened to Peter? A short time later would he not be crucified as his master? He had yet to be completely identified with Christ. The disciples had followed Jesus the Master, Jesus the miracle worker, but not Jesus on the holy Cross: the redeeming Cross. And what they lacked was no small thing. If the mission of Christ reached its fullness in the supreme sacrifice of the Cross to which his whole life was ordained, the soul too reaches the fullness of its identification with Christ, the maturity of supernatural life, when it is united to him on the Cross, when with Christ it becomes oboediens usque ad mortem, mortem autem crucis.21 This is what St Paul said to the Galatians: I have been crucified with Christ; it is now no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me. And the life I now live in the flesh, I live by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me.22 Christ gave himself up for Paul and he gave himself up for every single one of us. This way is open to all souls who are ready to follow him: to let him work. It is not the privilege of a few. The soul who perseveres in love finds the Cross on its way, and on the Cross, Christ. If you seek Christ as you should, could you ask for a clearer sign than the Cross to know that you have found him?23 And that is why we, who love Jesus Christ with a special love, also love his Cross, the true cross, not the one which we invent for ourselves with our wretchedness. And we try to unite ourselves with him on the Cross, nailing ourselves there

20 Zech 13:7 21 Phil 2:8 22 Gal 2:19-20 23 The Way, 710 16 Living in Christ

with love, with joy, with faith and hope. Jesus, I cannot get along without your Cross, or without these nails, with which my children and I have wanted to fasten ourselves spiritually to your holy Cross.24

Encounter with the Cross This way a moment comes – how well the soul understands it!, how clear are those times when our Lord asks us for more!25 – in which, without the noise of words, our Lord makes us share more fully in his Passion and in his Death, by accepting the sincere and profound offering we made to him at the dawn of our new life of dedication. Carissimi: Christus passus est pro nobis, vobis relinquens exemplum ut sequamini vestigia eius (1 Pet 2:21) – Beloved: Christ suffered for us, leaving us an example so that we might follow in his footsteps. To follow his example. How often you will have reflected on it, and you will have spoken about it to others! How many times I have repeated it to people, individually, in groups, in spiritual conversations, in talks – I was going to say: more or less solemn, but we do not do solemn things. How many times I have said it, even without having this text in mind! And amidst my personal errors, I have always tried to practise this: to follow his example, to follow Jesus Christ. And in this forge of sorrow, where our Lord has put me to carry the Work forward – Lord, you have given me abundant sorrows, thank you! – in this forge of suffering, which my life has been, our Lord has taught me that whoever steps where Christ stepped, finds joy.26 There is a deep, immense joy, which is the first sign of an encounter with the Cross of Christ; a sweet encounter, a happy find, full of incalculable joys, but marked in its very roots by sorrow. A sorrow

24 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 10 25 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 11 26 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 11

Living in Christ 17

that does not overwhelm a person, does not frighten anyone, does not defeat a person, but which purifies, cleans, ennobles and inflames the soul with desires of loving more. It may be that this encounter with the Cross happens suddenly, unexpectedly; that it comes with such force and intensity that the soul, overcome by love, bursts out in a yes, a wholehearted acceptance, consummated in a unique and definitive act of the will. But this is not the usual thing and much less so in the Work. The particular nature of our vocation leads us to conform to the Cross of Christ in a thousand and one, ordinary, everyday details, part of the normal run of things, without show, and which lead us up an inclined plane so that even we ourselves do not realise it. But in any case, be it an unexpected encounter or a growing familiarity with the Cross, we are speaking of a smiling asceticism. For some, this joy in the Cross could give reason for false scandal. But that is because they do not know that when the soul walks where Christ walks, when there is no longer resignation, but when the soul conforms itself with the Cross – it takes on the form of the Cross – when the soul loves the will of God, when one wants the Cross, then the Cross no longer weighs down. Then the Cross is no longer my cross, but his, and he carries it with me.27

The fullness of life in Christ From the first moment we have insisted that sanctity is not possible without the Cross. And we will always have to insist, with the testimony of our joy because to find the Cross is to find Christ. And with him there is always joy, even if there is injustice, if there is misunderstanding, if there is physical suffering. This is why I am displeased – even though I understand that it is an ordinary way of speaking – when I hear people calling crosses those contradictions 27 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 12 18 Living in Christ

which often spring from the person’s own pride. They are not the Cross. They are not the true Cross because they are not the Cross of Christ. I have never felt sorry for myself and our Lord has sent me plenty of sorrows. Thank you Lord! Thank you, Lord, because you have given me an asceticism that is yours, because you have made me understand that to have the Cross is to have joy. It is to possess you.28 It is then that the soul, in the maturity of its interior life, feels itself Ipse Christus, Christ himself, and consequently, it feels the fullness of the spirit of adoption as sons, by virtue of which we cry: Abba, Pater!29 Abba, Papa!, the familiar name the Hebrew children used to call their father. To live this identity with Christ is to feel ourselves to be completely children of God. When God dealt me those blows around 1931, I could not understand it… And then suddenly, in the midst of all that immense bitterness, came those words: You are my son (Ps 2:7), you are Christ. And all I could answer was: Abba, Pater! Abba, Pater! Abba! Abba! Abba! And now I see it all in a new light, like a new discovery, as, with the passage of the years, one sees the hand of God, divine Wisdom, divine power. You, Lord, have helped me understand that having the Cross means finding happiness and joy. And the reason, that I now see more clearly than ever, is this: that having the Cross means being identified with Christ, means being Christ and so being a son of God… Lord, I ask your Mother, our Patron St Joseph and my ministerial Archangel, that they ask for this spirit for myself and for my children for ever. Ne respicias peccata mea, sed fidem! (Ordo Missae) That faith, that light, that love for the Cross, unto death! That divine light, which will always make us understand that it is worthwhile to be

28 From our Father, Crónica, December 1963, p. 12 29 Rom 8:15

Living in Christ 19

nailed to the Cross, because it means entering into Life, being carried by the Life of Christ. The Cross: that is where Christ is, and you have to lose yourself in Him! Then there will be no more sorrows, no more hardships. You mustn’t say: Lord, I can’t cope with any more, I’m useless… No! It’s not true! On the Cross, you will be Christ, and you will feel that you are a son of God, and you will cry out: Abba, Pater! What a joy it is to find you, Lord!30 In this coming of age – paradox of Christian life – which is the maturity of divine filiation, the soul abandons itself in God as a small child does in the arms of its father. It sees things of the world as they are, at their true worth, and has no other concern than that of pleasing God. Christ, crucified, is all that matters, as it did for St Paul: henceforth let no man trouble me, for I bear on my body the marks of Jesus.31 What else could matter to him? Those who did not like him, said that he was short, slow of speech, cross-eyed… And he felt so great! With those invisible wounds, he felt like alter Christus, ipse Christus. Yes, Paul, great Paul! Thank you for this doctrine you have left us because the Holy Spirit inspired it in you! You are Christ! Paul, rejoice because we Christians love you, because we are grateful to you for this treasure of doctrine!32 Even when someone has reached this identification with Christ on the Cross, he cannot think that all struggle, all progress is over. Not that I have already obtained this or am already perfect, warns the Apostle; but I press on to make it my own, because Jesus Christ has made me his own.33 A new advance will be possible while it is day; night comes, when no one can work.34 Toil ends only with the night of death, and

30 From our Father, Meditation, 28 April 1963 31 Gal 6:17 32 From our Father, Meditation, 28 April 1963 33 Phil 3:12 34 Jn 9:4 20 Living in Christ

then the day of the Lord begins, the eternal Sunday, the glory of heaven for those who were faithful on earth. Then there will be delight, joy, the vision of God. There, the final identification through glory with Christ will take place and the possession of the inheritance of the children of God. Beloved, we are God’s children now; it does not yet appear what we shall be, but we know that when he appears we shall be like him, for we shall see him as he is.35 May our Lady, Mother of Christ and our Mother, lead us along this road of growth in Christ, of full imitation of her beloved son, of complete configuration with him, sharers in his divine filiation.

35 1 Jn 3:2

## IDENTIFYING ONESELF WITH CHRIST {#cuaderno.3.2}

May you identify yourself with Jesus Christ, who is Life. May you be Ipse Christus. Our whole life can be summed up as a continuous process that starts with an identification with Christ through grace, makes us more and more like him, and thus we come to share more fully in divine filiation, until we reach that final and definitive identification through glory in our heavenly home. This process, which is the essence of supernatural life, of real interior life, culminates on earth as the life of Christ culminated: on the Cross. It is an encounter that usually takes place little by little, up an inclined plane, as it were, almost without our realising it; but which, in uniting us to our crucified Jesus, sanctifies us and makes us co-redeemers who are apostolic and effective. We did not choose this road ourselves. It was our Lord who said to us: follow me1 and, as the Apostles did, continuo, relictis retibus,2 we immediately left everything and followed him. My son, you are in the Work because he called you. And the same one who called you now gives you all the supernatural means for you to become Ipse Christus. The means which can be perceived by the senses, which can be seen, which prevent us from going astray, are our Norms and Customs.

1 Mt 4:19 2 Mt 4:20 22 Living in Christ

Fulfil them, and the time will come when you can say, from the very depths of your soul: it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me (Gal 2:20).3 This transformation is the work of grace and grace alone, which, reaches us essentially through the sacraments ex opere operato, as theology teaches. It comes to us continually through the Eucharist – our chief Norm and the centre of our interior life – and through the sacrament of Penance. It is by means of the sacraments and, in another way, through the fulfilment of the other Norms and Customs that the Will of God has appointed for us, that the Holy Spirit goes on moulding the image of Christ in our soul: “the Spirit makes us Christlike by means of his sanctifying force.”4 Lovingly fulfilling our Norms and Customs, we facilitate the action of the Holy Spirit. The spirit of the Work of God is so rooted in the gospels that it makes us retrace the path of Christ on earth. In fulfilling the Norms we reproduce in ourselves the life of the firstborn among many brethren.5 Our Norms are the practical way of fulfilling that command of St Paul: Have this mind among yourselves which was in Christ Jesus.6 Each Norm is also a drawing closer to our Lord, a step forward in our identification with him, and a manifestation of his life in us. My children, the life of Jesus Christ is repeated in the life of each one of us in some way, both in its internal development, in sanctification, and in external life.7

A life of work We begin the day with the morning offering which is like a new

3 From our Father, Meditation, 12 April 1954 4 St Cyril of Alexandria, Sermo Pasch. 5 Rom 8:29 6 Phil 2:5 7 cf The Forge, 418

Identifying oneself with Christ 23

birth for the struggle, for the divine combat. Like Christ newly born in Bethlehem, we too place our trust in our Lady, in St Joseph, and in the angels. And we do the only thing a child can do: to promise to offer all that we are and all that we can do: thoughts, affections, words and actions. It is an offering of love and of service, following the example of Christ who loved us and gave himself up for us, a fragrant offering and sacrifice to God.8 Jesus increased in wisdom and in stature, and in favour with God and man.9 Step by step our Lord’s growth during the long years of his hidden life, when he learned the knowledge of men and worked with Joseph, is reproduced in our life. In study, in work, we go to Nazareth with our heart. We work hard and seriously. We use the means as well to grow in ascetical and in doctrinal formation because we want to know our Father God who is in heaven. We pay great attention to our professional, human and apostolic training because we want many children to share in the divine inheritance. Work: our Lord spent thirty years working as a humble craftsman. And the love he put into his work, the eager desire to serve, the effort, the responsibility of helping St Joseph meet the financial needs of the home of Nazareth, will live again in our soul as we take up our professional work. In our work, and always, we have to take care of little things because we want to imitate Christ, of whom was said: bene omnia fecit,10 he has done all things well. We have to pay attention to small details, because, as our Father tells us: Before God, who is eternal, you are much more a child than, before you, a two-year-old toddler. And besides being a child, you are a child of God. Don’t forget it.11 A father does not demand extraordinary things from his children,

8 Eph 5:2 9 Lk 2:52 10 Mk 7:37 11 The Way, 860 24 Living in Christ

especially if they are small. He asks for love in little things. That is why we, who are children of God, seek our Father in heaven by sanctifying our ordinary work, making great, through Love, the little duties of each day. And, as when we are working we are only concerned for the service of our Father God, in our professional calling we shall imitate the apostolic desire of Christ who could say: ignem veni mittere in terram.12 Our Lord wanted to set fire to the world with the fire of charity. And it is the obligation of every apostle to spread the blaze through the profession we have chosen, or with whatever other work that occupies us, trying to put Christ at the summit of all human activities.

A life of prayer My children, our vocation is to follow Christ. Venite post me et faciam vos fieri piscatores hominum (Mt 4:19). And to follow him so closely that we live with him, like the first Twelve; so close to him that we identify with him, that we live his Life.13 Look at Jesus Christ. He is our model. What does he do on the great occasions? What does the Holy Gospel tell us about him? Before he begins his public life, he retires forty days and forty nights (Mt 4:2) to the desert, to pray. Later, when he is about to make a definitive selection of the first Twelve, St Luke tells us that he continued all night in prayer to God (Lk 6:12). And when the tomb of Lazarus was opened, raising his eyes, he said, Father, I give you thanks for having heard me (Jn 11:41). And what does he do in those intimate moments of the Last Supper, in the agony of Gethsemane, in the solitude of the Cross? With his arms outstretched, he also speaks with his Father.14

12 Lk 12:49 13 From our Father, Meditation Living for the glory of God, 21 November 1954 14 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955

Identifying oneself with Christ 25

The life of Christ is continuous prayer, a dialogue with God the Father. In our lives it is renewed with presence of God, with vocal prayers and, especially, with those two daily periods devoted to prayer, talking only to our Lord. Because this is what prayer is for us: We go and speak to Jesus, who asks us: What’s the matter? Well, it’s like this... And immediately, light.15 Continuous, constant dialogue: When Christ speaks to us in parables, as he did to his first listeners, we often don’t understand him. Then we have to make our own the Apostles’ entreaty: edissere nobis parabolam! (Mt 13:36). Lord, explain the parable to us.16 And then comes all the clarity, all the understanding. Presence of God, consideration of our divine filiation. Norms of always: acts of thanksgiving – Father, I thank you that you have heard me;17 aspirations: I thank you, Father, Lord of heaven and earth;18 acts of accepting the divine Will: not what I will but what you will…19 As Jesus, the Son of the living God, was continuously contemplating the Father, the sense of our divine filiation, too, leads us to seek the presence of God. And the fruit of this presence of God is a constant, supernatural joy, which is not simply physiological but which is that of one who knows he is a child of God, Ipse Christus. And knowing it and recalling it, fills us with joy and peace. But when the time had fully come, God sent forth his Son, born of woman.20 Christ is the son of Mary, and all of us in Opus Dei feel ourselves to be children of the Blessed Virgin. That is why our whole life has a deep Marian meaning expressed in many details of piety and customs, that are like a replay of conversations between the Mother and

15 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955 16 From our Father, Meditation Living for the glory of God, 21 November 1954 17 Jn 11:41 18 Mt 11:25 19 Mk 14:36 20 Gal 4:4 26 Living in Christ

Child in the home of Nazareth. If you and I have to be other Christs, Ipse Christus, we need to humble ourselves, to deny ourselves, to forget about ourselves, in novitate sensus (Rom 12:2), having a new life, exchanging this wretchedness of ours for all that hidden greatness of God, so as to be co-redeemers with him. With this spirit we resort to mortification so that our self decreases and the life of Christ increases. And the love the Holy Spirit has infused in our soul moves us to look for self-giving with smiling asceticism. We will try to practise charity, like Christ, first with God; then towards men, beginning with our brothers and our families, because charity ought to be well ordered. “Nothing can make you such an imitator of Christ as concern for the others. Even though you fast, even though you sleep on the floor, even though – so to speak – you kill yourself, if you are not concerned for your neighbour, you haven’t done much; you’re still a long way from his image.”21 We are guided by the constant example of Christ who, even on the Cross, doesn’t think of himself, but of the others: of his blessed Mother who is being left alone, of John, of that good thief, of the very people who have crucified him… My children, I think that all your personal problems are solved if, at the time of the examination, you can truly say: Jesus, I haven’t been worrying about myself, I haven’t been thinking about myself at all… If we did this, just think of all the disappointments and sufferings we would be spared! Lord, I have thought about your love and your glory, I have thought about souls… And to my children – who are so many – to those to whom this happens, I say: You are a contemplative soul, you are Ipse Christus.22

21 St John Chrysostom, In Epistolam 1 Cor. hom. 22 From our Father, Meditation, 13 October 1963

Identifying oneself with Christ 27

Union with Christ in the sacrifice of the Mass Christ is always present, in the whole of our lives as children of God in his Work. He is the goal and the centre of all our eager desires. But where can we find Christ in the fullest sense? Where can we unite ourselves to him in the most intimate way? In the Mass which makes us sharers in the fruits of the Passion of Christ.23 The proper and main effect of the Mass is to produce identification with Christ, with the Son of God. The only reason why we do not obtain all at once, in their fullness, all the effects of this sacrament which perfects a man by uniting him to the suffering of Christ,24 is because of our defective dispositions, because we go to the Eucharist insufficiently prepared. For the sacrifice of the Mass only operates in those who are associated to the sacrament through faith and love… and each one gets more or less out of it according to his devotion.25 That is why divine filiation leads us not only to attend Mass but to take part in the Mass, intimately uniting ourselves to Christ’s oblation, offering him to God the Father and offering ourselves, too, each day with greater affection, with all that we are and all we are worth. Moreover, as Christ ordained his whole life to the supreme sacrifice of the Cross, we, too, must direct each day to the sacrifice of the Mass which thus becomes the centre of our interior life. All the Norms of piety of the day, when they inspire sorrow and penance, supplication, adoration and thanksgiving, living faith and love and hope, are like a preparation for the offering of Christ the victim, and of us with him. So we see that the desire to identify ourselves with Christ, and to share ever more fully in his divine filiation, fills our whole life; and it moves us to seek his presence, the dialogue that gives order and

23 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.83, a.1 c 24 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.73, a.3 ad 3 25 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.79, a.7 ad 2 28 Living in Christ

meaning to our practices of piety, our work, our apostolate, our rest… The contemplative life, in a word, is the result of our living in Christ. Normae vitae. There is a perfect continuity in the Norms. They are related to one another; they are perfectly arranged. But do you know what the thread is that ties them together? Our contemplative vocation. A man who tries to live this and who reaches a stage when, for a long time, he lives it almost without effort (even though it seems that there is no struggle, there is), this, then, is a man who lives the Life of God, who can say what I like to repeat so much: vivo autem iam non ego: vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20) – it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me. And to be Christ is to know yourself to be a child of God.26

26 From our Father, Brief Circle, 27 October 1963

## SHARING IN THE MISSION OF CHRIST {#cuaderno.3.3}

My children, the life of Jesus Christ is repeated in the life of each one of us in some way, both in its internal development, in sanctification, and in external life.1 Supernatural life took root in our souls when the Holy Spirit infused his grace into our hearts; and it grows stronger, continually, through that ‘supernatural breathing’ which is the practice of our Norms. This reproduces in us the image of Christ, the features of the children of God. It makes us other Christs, Ipse Christus. We have to dedicate ourselves to this task of identifying ourselves with Christ, with all our energy. “Christ has given you the power of being like him, according to your capacity. Do not be surprised to hear this. What ought to frighten you is not to be like him.”2

The Christian’s mission What did Christ come to earth for? Why did he want to become man, to take mortal flesh like ours? Ignem veni mittere in terram, et quid volo nisi ut accendatur?3 Christ came to enkindle the world with the fire of his charity. He came to redeem man. But when the time had fully come, God sent forth his Son, born of woman, born under the law,

1 cf The Forge, 418 2 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae 78, 4 3 Lk 12:49 30 Living in Christ

to redeem those who were under the law, so that we might receive adoption as sons.4 And we, who are other Christs and, consequently, children of God, have also been sent to the world with a special divine command to spread the blaze, to be co-redeemers. As you have sent me into the world, so I have sent them into the world.5 We are apostles, men with a mission, without calling ourselves missionaries, sent by God because he made us sharers in the mission of his Son, first-born among many brethren.6 And he gave us the power of being his children just like Christ, so everything that we do in his name is attributed to Christ. He who receives you receives me, and he who receives me receives him who sent me.7 To be identified with Christ is the indispensable condition for carrying out our mission with supernatural effectiveness. He himself told us so: Abide in me, and I in you. As the branch cannot bear fruit by itself, unless it abides by the vine, neither can you, unless you abide in me. I am the vine, you are the branches. He who abides in me, and I in him, he it is that bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.8 We are co-redeemers in so far as we resemble Christ, in so far as we share in his divine filiation. When one has something good, when a soul is happy, when one feels this interior joy and desires this happiness, it tries to pass on this good and this happiness to others. That is why we have the imperative duty to do proselytism; we have the duty to transmit this divine gift, and to try to get other souls to serve our Lord in Opus Dei.9 A person who is filled with Christ, who feels within himself the joy of children of

4 Gal 4:4-5 5 Jn 17:18 6 Rom 8:29 7 Mt 10:40 8 Jn 15:4-5 9 From our Father, Crónica, February 1964, p. 7

Sharing in the mission of Christ 31

God, burns with the desire of communicating his happiness to those around him – bonum est diffusivum sui, good tends naturally to spread itself. He feels the zeal for souls that burned in Christ’s heart,10 that consumed St Paul when he found himself on his own in Athens.11 And this love for God and men enables him to overcome all kinds of obstacles to drawing everyone closer to the warmth of Christ. Then his apostolate becomes, like all true apostolate, an overflowing of his life within.12 For the same reason, when a soul does not feel this zeal, we must infer that the life of Christ in it is weak, languid, almost dead. If a man does not abide in me, he is cast forth as a branch and withers; and the branches are gathered, thrown into the fire and burned.13 When a person does not have zeal to do proselytism, it is because his heart is not beating, because he is dead. And those words of Scripture can be applied to him: iam foetet, quatriduanus est enim (Jn 11:39) – he is already stinking, for he has been dead four days. These souls, even though they were in the Work, would be dead, rotten, iam foetet. And I cannot go anywhere with corpses. Corpses I bury.14 Our founder summarised for us in a few words the effectiveness of a soul that lives the life of our Lord in the Work: If we are other Christs, if we behave as children of God, wherever we are we spread the blaze: Christ sets everything alight.

All souls matter to us Our vocation – Christ’s call – brings us to identify ourselves with him, and he came into the world ut omnes homines salvi fiant (1 Tim 2:4), to redeem all mankind. There isn’t a single soul that

10 Cf Lk 12:50 11 Cf Acts 17:16 12 Cf The Way, 961 13 Jn 15:6 14 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 9 32 Living in Christ

doesn’t interest him. Every one of them has cost him all the price of his Blood (cf 1 Pet 1:18-19).15 We, too, are interested in all souls; we want to reach every corner of the world. And that is why, following the example of our Lord, we spend more time with some friends, as Christ did with the twelve Apostles, because there is a good chance they may have a vocation. And we have another broader circle of friends, like the seventy-two disciples, to whom, in some way, we try to give our formation as well. And finally, we try to get to know all the souls that our Lord places in our path, without leaving out anyone. We must not let any soul who comes close to us be lost with respect to the apostolate and possibly be deprived of a vocation. By making all the people we know apostolic, through doctrine and charity, we make the influence of our apostolate reach every corner. We want to act as a measure of divine leaven to reach so many people. Compared with the multitudes of people, even though there are thousands of us, we are few in number. So we must see ourselves as a tiny measure of yeast, prepared and ready to do good to the whole of mankind, for the whole mass, not forgetting what the Apostle says: Modicum fermentum totam massam corrumpit (1 Cor 5:6) – a little leaven is enough to leaven all the dough. We have to be this yeast, this leaven, and to know how to change and improve the mass.16 Jesus was yeast, salt and light among the Jews; his disciples, too, were leaven, salt and light, knowing how to transform the whole world. They carried this out by starting with the people nearest them, attracting the souls that were closest to them. In our apostolate, too, our brothers or sisters have to be the most important: The first expression of proselytism is to help one another to persevere and to become saints.17

15 From our Father, Meditation, 16 April 1954 16 From our Father, Meditation, 16 April 1954 17 Alone with God, 153

Sharing in the mission of Christ 33

Similarly in the apostolate: first our relatives, our companions… Among the apostles who followed Christ were James the Less, Simon and Jude, the sons of Alphaeus, relatives of our Lord. And Andrew, after answering the call of Jesus, first found his brother Simon.18 Then came Philip, who was from Bethsaida, the city of Andrew and Peter.19 Apostolate with one’s relatives is clearly expected in the order of charity.

The apostolate of doctrine and example Our Lord came to teach. We all remember St Luke’s introduction to the Acts of the Apostles: In the first book, O Theophilus, I have dealt with all that Jesus began to do and teach (Acts 1:1). Jesus came to teach us, but by doing things. He came to teach, to instruct us, but by being the model – at one and the same time being the teacher and the text. Our apostolate, because it is that of Christ, that of the children of God, is summed up in giving doctrine. Among all the means that we use, we count especially on example, on deeds, on the bonus odor Christi20 of the Christian who tries to live the teachings of our Lord. Are you and I really determined to live a life that serves as a model and an example for the others? our Father asks us. Are we determined to be other Christs, to behave like children of God? It is not enough to say with our lips that we are. We have to affirm it with deeds… Let us see then: Are you satisfied with the way you have behaved up until now? Could it be said of you, who are another Christ, a child of God, that you have come facere et docere (Acts 1:1) to act in a way that you can teach others to do good, noble things, the things of the Redemption? We have to live the apostolate of example, which opens the door to every other apostolate. The nature of our vocation demands it. We live

18 Jn 1:41 19 Jn 1:44 20 2 Cor 2:15 34 Living in Christ

in the middle of the world and use it to sanctify ourselves. We do not distance ourselves from people. It is natural, then, that we have a special obligation to give example, to be light, salt, fire and model. But how can we give example? How can we bear witness to Christ?… Perhaps with something that would arouse attention, something unusual, something extraordinary, dazzling or brilliant? Do you think that this is the way proper to the children of God in his Opus Dei? On the contrary, is not the lesson of Jesus Christ that we should pass unnoticed among others of our social condition and profession, as just one among many? Not that your name or your work will be unknown. Not that you will not be outstanding for your talent. What will not be known, for there is no need for others to know it, is that you are a soul dedicated to the service of God determined on imitating Jesus Christ, that you are salt of the earth, another Christ. Let them experience it for themselves; let them feel clean and noble and strong in their conduct. But you should pass unnoticed, as Christ did in Nazareth. I have been able to observe my children’s way of living so often in these years. They have known so well how to do it! Wherever they are there is peace, there is devotion, there is joy. And those who live with them hardly realise it. Only as time passes by, and your brother is no longer there, do they say, Ah! What was it about that person? But while they are together, they pass unnoticed. In his public life too, our Lord gave us an example of this discreet way of acting, so effective that at times the person benefiting did not realise what he had received. This part of Christ’s life was public, not because it was given over to great manifestations (though he did not avoid them when his mission required them), but because he addressed himself personally to souls. He asked the blind man whose sight he had Sharing in the mission of Christ 35

restored: Tell it to no one.21 Later on, when he was told: Leave here and go to Judea, that your disciples may see the works you are doing. For no man works in secret if he seeks to be known openly…. Jesus said to them, My time has not yet come.22 And still later, he started out on the road non manifeste, sed quasi in occulto,23 not publicising it, but passing unnoticed.

Apostolate of friendship and confidence The apostolate of example is complemented and strengthened through the apostolate of friendship and confidence. For, the souls who feel the warmth of our Life in Christ, who sense the joy of children of God in us, come in search of light. Then is the moment for the discreet indiscretion24 that opens up horizons of self-giving. Something similar happens in the scene of Jesus with the two disciples who were going to Emmaus. The whole life of Christ is a divine model which we should imitate. But what the evangelist tells us in the scene of Emmaus belongs to us in a very special way. The two disciples open their hearts to Jesus and our Lord comforts them. He restores light to their blind eyes and he wins them back for the apostolate. Nonne cor nostrum ardens erat in nobis, dum loqueretur in via? Did our hearts not burn within us as he talked to us on the road? If you are an apostle, these words of the disciples of Emmaus should rise spontaneously to the lips of your professional companions when they meet you along the ways of their lives.25 Be other Christs, and thus children of God. Then you will have this superabundance, expressed in peace and in joy that you can give to the

21 Mk 8:26 22 Jn 7:3-6 23 Jn 7:10 24 The Way, 973 25 The Way, 917 36 Living in Christ

others. You will attract people and you will work real miracles. That is why, in the midst of the most intense activity, we must recollect ourselves in God, consider our divine filiation, the moving force of our action, and feel this life of Christ in our soul moving us to work with renewed vigour. Don’t you realise that at times it seems that our Lord wants to withdraw from the world with his own, just as we do in our family life, in our Annual Courses, in our retreats, in our days of recollection…, seeking those blessed shelters we have in the Work? With these means we try to get to know our Lord better, and then we have greater strength to launch out into the midst of the crowd.26 If we act like this, truly living our identification with Christ, of our divine filiation, the fruits will not be long in coming. In Opus Dei, as each one seeks sanctity in his own state of life, in the exercise of his own profession, in his ordinary life, I dare to say that God has given us the gift of performing miracles, and big ones. We give light to the blind… Who could not relate thousands of cases? How a person, blind almost from birth, recovers his sight and receives all the splendour of Christ’s light. And another was deaf, another dumb, unable to utter a single word befitting a child of God… And his tongue is cleansed, and now he speaks like a man, not an animal. In nomine Iesu (Acts 3:6) we have restored the power of speech to the dumb man. And that maimed creature, unable to do a single good deed, and that comfort-lover, who saw things but did not do them… You told him, Surge et ambula (Mt 9:5). And that other person, dead, rotten, rotten! so that he stank like a corpse, who heard as in the miracle of the son of the widow at Naim: Adolescens tibi dico, surge (Lk 7:14). My child, miracles like Christ’s, miracles like those of the first Christians. You will work them too, if you are alter Christus, even

26 From our Father, Meditation, 28 January 1962

Sharing in the mission of Christ 37

more, ipse Christus, if you know how to live this complete identification with our Lord, that is the contemplative life. I have spoken to you in parables, as our Lord spoke in the Gospel. But what is not a parable is to say that you are ipse Christus, and I too.27 The most Blessed Virgin, the Mother of God and Co-redemptrix, will help us learn to live the life of her Son, to act as children of God and to co-redeem with Christ. Mother, Oh Mother! With that word of yours – fiat, ‘be it done’ – you have made us brothers of God and heirs to his Glory. Blessed are you!28

27 From our Father, Meditation, 16 April 1954 28 The Way, 512

## THE DIVINE DIALOGUE {#cuaderno.3.4}

For the Apostles, life with Jesus was a time for a natural, continuous and friendly relationship with the very Son of God. One day they learned through revelation that Jesus was the Christ, the Son of the living God,1 but nothing changed in their familiar conversations. The continual presence of Jesus and his attitude towards them taught them, in a practical way, that love had bridged all distances. It was he, God himself, speaking lovingly to each one of them, who had invited them to follow him. In that divine companionship it was often Jesus himself who initiated the conversation with a question, an exclamation or a comment. The Apostles addressed our Lord with great simplicity, as a friend – I have called you friends2 – or even more, as a father, for Jesus spoke to them as his sons: Little children, yet a little while I am with you.3 The veneration that they felt for Jesus in no way impeded the confident and intimate character of their filial friendship.

The relationship between the Apostles and Jesus God himself was there at their side, talking familiarly with them, person to person, in the most divine and normal of human conversations. From these discussions between men and God, the Gospels have

1 Mt 16:16 2 Jn 15:15 3 Jn 13:33

The divine dialogue 39

preserved for us some ordinary sentences, direct and simple questions; exclamations of admiration and surprise, of indignation, of joy: Look, 4 Ordinary Teacher, what wonderful stones and what wonderful buildings! sentences that sometimes referred to insignificant and unimportant facts, and at other times to the deep aspirations that Jesus’ words provoked in his followers, as when he asked Peter: “Simon, son of John, do you love me more than these?”… “Yes, Lord; you know that I love you.”5 Twice more Jesus repeated the same direct, precise question, and twice Peter answered, talking familiarly to God. And afterwards, seeing John near them, Peter took the initiative: “Lord, what about this man?”6 The conversation that we find throughout the Gospel is astonishingly simple: “And you know the way where I am going.” Thomas said to him, “Lord, we do not know where you are going; how can we know the way?” Jesus said to him, “I am the way, and the truth, and the life; no one comes to the Father, but by me. If you had known me, you would have known my Father also; henceforth you know him and have seen him.” Philip said to him, “Lord, show us the Father and we shall be satisfied.” Jesus said to him, “Have I been with you so long, and yet you do not 7 know me, Philip? He who has seen me has seen the Father.” All Jesus’ conversations with his disciples have this same tone. It was not only in moments of special significance. The conversation was continuous and it was provoked by the most commonplace events: work, rest, the road, waverings. The habitual presence of Jesus was a constant invitation to talk. And that frankness, that marvellous spontaneity of the disciples, often gave our Lord the opportunity to correct them, to give

4 Mk 13:1 5 Jn 21:15 6 Jn 21:21 7 Jn 14:4-9 40 Living in Christ

them criteria, to make them rectify, to help them. Lord, do you want us to bid fire come down from heaven and consume them? But he turned and rebuked them, and he said, “You do not know what manner of spirit you are of.”8 The disciples listened with humility, and consulted him when they did not understand. They loved our Lord more each day. He was their master, their friend, their father. They spent the whole day in prayer and they did not even realise it. One day, one of the disciples said to our Lord: “Lord, teach us to pray, as John taught his disciples.” And he said to them, “When you pray, say: Father, hallowed be thy name.”9 The Lord taught them that they had to pray just as they talked to him

– with a child’s simplicity: natural, confiding conversation. He himself had set them an example, so often. The relation of Jesus with the Father also had the character of an intimate conversation, of loving confidence. In the face of good, or before evil; to ask, or to give thanks

– for everything Jesus lifted up his heart to his Father in heaven. The interior, contemplative conversation of Jesus was uninterrupted. Nevertheless, many times he wanted to show it outwardly, in front of his followers, so that they had an example to fortify their faith: Father, I thank you for having heard me. I knew that you always hear me, but I have said this for the sake of the people standing here.10 This word, this loving and spontaneous invocation, Father, was constantly on our Lord’s lips. With it he began his thanksgiving, his petitions, his praises, his desires. Our Lord commanded his followers to pray constantly and he gave them the simple model of his own continuous prayer.

8 Lk 9:54-55 9 Lk 11:1-2 10 Jn 11:41-42

The divine dialogue 41

In search of conversation with God Our habitual dealings with God should not differ from those the Apostles had with Jesus. God is everywhere. The Blessed Trinity dwells in our souls in grace. We live in Christ as members of his body… And as if all this were not enough, he wanted to stay with us, in the Eucharist, under the humble species of bread. When you approach the Tabernacle remember that he has been awaiting you for twenty centuries.11 Everything around us and in us proclaims this presence, this nearness, this closeness of God who invites us to a continuous conversation. For we are the temple of the living God; as God said, “I will live in them and move among them, and I will be their God, and they shall be my people.”12 It is not necessary to go far away, or even move at all, to seek God. It is enough to look at him, to direct our heart to him. We’ve got to be convinced that God is always near us.13 He is continually awaiting a word from us, a smile, a gesture of love, a little conversation. Often – always – it is our Lord who provokes the conversation by little inspirations, in a thousand different incidents, sometimes directing at us this loving reproach: I have spoken to them and they have not listened, I have called to them and they have not answered.14 Man tends by nature to converse, to communicate. It is a basic need; and when this conversation is not with another then it is with himself: remembering problems, impressions of the day, worries, wishes, fears, doubts… In the interior life, a man starts to talk to God. He converses with Him in the depths of his soul. It is a conversation that is generous and open. Directing himself habitually to God in interior conversation,

11 The Way, 537 12 2 Cor 6:16 13 The Way, 267 14 Jer 35:17 42 Living in Christ

a man ceases to be egocentric and becomes theocentric. At the same time he acquires depth, firmness and serenity. This continual conversation strengthens divine friendship, enkindles love, increases our knowledge of God and of ourselves, rectifies our intention, makes our judgment more objective, promotes good works and improves our charity. Love for God is a love of friendship. This friendship, like all friendships on this earth, becomes firm by getting to know one another. It is shown, as all friendships are, in doing things together and in conversation. The first requirement for this intimacy is faith. We believe that God is really at our side; and that he is really interested in us, attentive to our thoughts, to our affections, to our words and to our works. A man of God is never alone. He never has reason to be bored. He is always in the presence of the one he loves. Our Lord is waiting for us at every moment. He is interested in everything that happens to us. God is at our side, with his fatherly and motherly care, ready to listen to our words, corresponding eternally to our love. He watches over us and wants us to approach him confidently, asking him for help, knowing that he will always listen to us.15 Faith, love, confidence, generosity, right intention, recollection… are all indispensable conditions for us to start a continuous conversation with our Lord. Perhaps at first this is done with many words but, after a while, with ever fewer words; until we arrive in heaven where the conversation will be eternal, with only one word, in mutual and eternal harmony, that will say everything. While we are on earth and are learning to have interior life, we have to re-start the conversation often, spontaneously and simply, as happens in all friendly conversations of people among themselves. Our Father has described it thus: It is the eternal conversation, that all people who have loved one another have had on earth. God has a right to ask us: Do you

15 From our Father, Crónica, August 1961, p. 10

The divine dialogue 43

think of me? Are you aware of my presence? Do you keep me present? Do you look for me as your support? Do you seek me as the light of your life, as your strength, your protection, your all? In the hours that the people of the earth call good: Lord! In the hours they call bad: Lord! And then comes peace and the loving conversation of affection.16 The custom of invoking God at every moment, taking advantage of the most unimportant happenings, has remained among Christian peoples as an inheritance from times when the faith was very strong. My God! Lord help us!… are expressions that one hears in any conversation. It is just that, quite often, they have completely lost their original meaning. But this is the meaning that ought to break out again in the Christian soul, an inner, almost instinctive movement, part of that hidden conversation with God.

Prayer, conversation with God All our Norms aim to lead us to establish a loving dialogue with our Lord, to building a bridge, to giving rise to an ebb and flow of love, to a conversation that carries our heart and mind to God. But this happens in a special way in our mental prayer, in the meditation, in those short periods each day that we dedicate exclusively to talking quietly to our Father God, in the seclusion of our heart. It is then, above all, in the prayer, that we really have to be careful to establish this conversation. You write: ‘To pray is to talk with God. But about what?’ About what? About him, about yourself: joys, sorrows, successes and failures, noble ambitions, daily worries, weaknesses! And acts of thanksgiving and petitions: and Love and reparation. In a word: to get to know him and to get to know yourself: ‘to get acquainted!’17

16 From our Father, Crónica, August 1961, p. 11 17 The Way, 91 44 Living in Christ

Conversation is so essential in our prayer that it is practically the whole of it. And so our Father was to write: You say that you do not know how to pray? Put yourself in the presence of God, and once you have said, ‘Lord, I do not know how to pray!’ rest assured that you have begun to do so.18 A good part of the course our prayer takes depends on this initial encounter: it brings peace, it makes us forget our difficulties and completely shapes the interior attitude of the soul. We usually begin by saying: My Lord and my God, I firmly believe that you are here, that you see me, that you hear me… And from here, from this faith, from this conviction, from this personal encounter with God, prayer springs forth in a broad and peaceful flow.19 Thus, the Father has also taught us: How many foolish notions and how many difficulties disappear at once when we go to God in prayer! We go and speak to Jesus, who asks us: What’s the matter? Well, it’s like this... And immediately, light.20 Prayer should never be a monologue, just an intellectual reflection; for the rest of the day depends in good part on the reality of this encounter with our Lord. Oportet semper orare et non deficere,21 we must always pray and not loose heart. All our Norms will lead us to this continual prayer. Without realising it, from morning till night and from night till morning, you will be praying: acts of love, acts of reparation, acts of thanksgiving; with your heart, with your lips, with little mortifications.22 The daily Norms and the Norms of always are specific calls to talk to our Lord. A living, personal, and tense conversation that springs from the heart, with a rhythm and tone that is always new, always spontaneous, with the pleasant surprise of an encounter.

18 The Way, 90 19 The Way, 145 20 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955 21 Lk 18:1 22 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955

The divine dialogue 45

The Norms need this conversation and they also give to the soul an attitude of openness to God, almost an instinct. The soul gets a habit of referring everything to God, who is always at our side, always ready to listen to us, always speaking to us, taking advantage of everything that happens. And so it goes on, in our work, in our social relations, in our apostolate, in moments of recreation, when going down a corridor or crossing the street. I want our whole life to be prayer: when faced with pleasant things and with what is unpleasant, when faced with consolation... and when faced with the pain of losing a loved one, our Father has told us. No matter what you face, you must go immediately to speak with your Father God, seeking him in the depths of your soul.23 There are no activities so absorbing, no circumstances so physical, that would make prayer impossible – with a word, an invocation, a glance, a smile, with the refinement of one who knows he is not alone. All this, done in a habitual, instinctive, constant and spontaneous way, is contemplation. It is a life of continual prayer, of prayer in the strictest sense, because, even though everything we do for God is prayer, it is this intimate conversation which is usually called prayer in the strict sense. Besides, if on the one hand this conversation, this encounter with God, demands rectitude of intention and of life, on the other it helps to rectify the intention and to really direct all that we do to God. It is a little more difficult at the beginning, needing more effort and the use of human devices and reminders. Later on, it comes more spontaneously and more continuously, but it is always necessary to seek the conversation, to desire it, look for it, taking advantage of every occasion. We are contemplative souls, our Father tells us. I practise this conversation with our Lord, and you have to practise it at every moment. An example? A very clear one: you catch your finger in a door. An exclamation, and afterwards: Lord! This is nothing. I would

23 From our Father, Meditation The prayer of the children of God, 4 April 1955 46 Living in Christ

merit perhaps to be in hell. I offer you this…24 Our Mother Mary and St Joseph, our Father and Lord, know a lot about this familiar, simple and constant conversation with Jesus: they lived so intimately with our Lord for so many years! We have to ask them to teach us to talk to Jesus, to find him in the thousand ordinary circumstances of our life, to say something kind and considerate to him, to keep a conversation going with him that makes our soul fall in love. It is a conversation which purifies us, which frees us from the oppressive atmosphere of the ‘self ’ and opens up for us the road towards union with God.

24 From our Father, Crónica, August 1961, p. 13

## LITTLE THINGS {#cuaderno.3.5}

For this commandment which I command you this day is not too hard for you, neither is it far off. It is not in heaven, that you should say, ‘Who will go up for us to heaven, and bring it to us, that we may hear it and do it?’ Neither is it beyond the sea, that you should say, ‘Who will go over the sea for us, and bring it to us, that we may hear it and do it?’ But the word is very near you.1 Quite close to us, in this small world in which we live, and in the work which we do, the measure of our sanctity is to be found at hand. And if we did not know about it already, this would amaze us, just as it took us by surprise the first time we heard about it. For looking around and trying to see what makes up the sanctity that is being asked of us, and which we have well within our reach, all we see are little things. Our daily lives are filled with living certain Norms and Customs, a specific apostolic task and our ordinary, very personal, daily work. And for this, besides, we have time at our disposal, that elapses little by little and from one moment to the next. So that everything, even the greatest things, are all reduced to the sum of many little things. Soul dedicated to God, to Jesus Christ, what are you doing?… Do you love with deeds, with these little deeds? For very rarely will you be able to serve him with great works. And that is precisely because, ordinarily, big things appear only in our imagination.2

1 Deut 30:11-14 2 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 10 48 Living in Christ

The importance of caring for details “In order to acquire true virtue, we must consider two things: what we do and how we do it.”3 To please God, we have to do things with perfection. If we want to become saints we must identify ourselves with Jesus Christ, who has done all things well.4 It is not enough that we do good things (such as praying, working or doing apostolate); we have to do them perfectly, finishing off each one well so that we can show our love for God. A Norm, or any other work that is well done, pleases God, and sanctifies us. It must not be done badly or carelessly, because to be accepted… you shall not offer anything that has a blemish, for it will not be acceptable for you.5 And since we love God, we have to present him quality work, done with perfection. If our work were shoddy or defective, it would be a sign of a languid spiritual life, meriting the reproach directed to the Church of Sardis: I know your works. You have the name of being alive, and you are dead. Awake, and strengthen what remains and is on the point of death, for I have not found your works perfect in the sight of my God.6 We would have to get out of this sad state at once. We would need to react urgently, and we would have to persuade ourselves, once more, of the absolute need for being concerned with details we were taught in the Work from the very beginning. Remember then what you received and heard; keep that, and repent.7 Work brought to completion. And how do we finish things well? It is always a question of details: detail in the use of the chisel or the paint brush, that final touch that turns a good work into a work of art. Commenting on the parable of the virgins, our Father taught us how the

3 St Thomas, Quodlibet 4, a.19 4 Mk 7:37 5 Lev 22:19-20 6 Rev 3:1-2 7 Rev 3:3

Little things 49

wise virgins made good use of their time. They discreetly provide themselves with oil… And when someone says: It is time!, exite obviam ei (Mt 25:6), they trim their lamps and go joyfully to receive the bridegroom. And the foolish virgins? At this moment they made all the effort: they went off to buy oil. But they were late, and while they were gone, the bridegroom came, and those who were ready went in with him to the marriage feast; and the door was shut. Afterwards the other maidens came also, saying, ‘Lord, Lord, open to us’ (Mt 25:10- 11). It is not as though they had not done anything. They had done something… but yet they had to hear the voice that told them: nescio vos (Mt 25:12), I do not know you. They just did not take the time to get ready beforehand – to take the reasonable precaution of acquiring the oil. My children, on occasions there is also no time, because the prayer gets neglected, because it is not well prepared… You must think: Why do I sometimes go to bed or get up at the wrong time? Why do I rush through work that has been entrusted for me to do? Why do I sometimes give it up after first having received it enthusiastically? Why so much disorder?… This may seem trivial, but it is our oil.8 Little things are so numerous and frequent that fulfilling them faithfully is heroic. They require attention, effort, generosity and love. One little isolated detail may not be very important. “What is little, is little, but he who is faithful in the little things, is great.”9 Steadfastness in love, in little things, is sanctity. And this is the sanctity which we try to live. Heroic sanctity. It is required by the call we have received. We have to be saints, through and through: true, authentic, canonizable saints; if not, we have failed. Authentic sanctity, without excuses, without

8 From our Father, Meditation, 9 January 1956 9 St Augustine, De doctrina christiana 14, 35 50 Living in Christ

euphemisms, to its last consequences, without half-way measures, living our vocation fully. Consequently, we must take extremely good care of even the smallest things, because our sanctification is reached precisely by sanctifying each day’s work.10 Accordingly, the sanctity which the Work asks of us does not consist in doing things that are more difficult each day, but in doing them with ever-increasing love. It is good to remember the story, of a French author, where the main character wanted to hunt lions in the corridors of his own house and, quite naturally, could not find any. Our lives are common and ordinary: for us to try to serve the Lord with big endeavours would be exactly like trying to go on a lion-hunt in the rooms of our own home. And, just like the hunter in the story, we would end up empty-handed: for big events, usually, occur only in our imagination, and seldom in reality. Throughout our life, if Love is what motivates us, how many small things will appear that we can put care into, how many opportunities for doing someone a small service, how many difficulties, unimportant, little things, that we will know how to treasure. Little things that cost us, and that are offered for a specific intention: the Church, the Pope, your brothers, all souls…11

Where to take care of the little things Life is usually a collection of small things in which God, our Father, gives us an opportunity to exercise all the virtues: to practise charity, fortitude, justice, sincerity, temperance, poverty, humility, obedience…12 We exercise charity, which is true affection, in the small services that we render others every day, with a smile, without the others realising we have done it; or, when we listen with interest to what our friends or

10 From our Father, Meditation, 19 March 1960 11 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 18 12 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930 14

Little things 51

colleagues at work have to tell us, perhaps at a moment when we are tired…; when we take care of material things, so that they last longer, or when we do repairs in time; when we don’t create necessities for ourselves; when we do without something to which we have become attached – all these are little, yet important, signs of Christian detachment. Mortifications during meals enable us to practise temperance, without show. Faithfulness to little things at work helps to finish off a job begun. And a prompt and cheerful obedience… Jesus, keep me smiling!,13 giving an account of tasks fulfilled and our permanent, personal attitude of being always available, are also little things. Little details in our Norms and Customs are the salt that preserves us from possible routine. The same thing applies to what we have been taught with regard to opening our hearts wide to speak about some apparently insignificant thing that could become big. Little things, then, in all that we do; in our life of piety also, already more personal and more intimate: small inventions of love. Our interior horizon widens and becomes richer when our spiritual childhood, made up of so many details, comes into play. So many times I have watched a little child go with its mother into the fields, and pick up one flower, and then another, and still another… He gathers flowers that have passed unnoticed to you and me. In our lives these little flowers will be aspirations to tell our Lord or the Virgin Mary that we love them more than anyone else, to make ourselves little children in their presence, to multiply our details of love. It is an interior world, rich and full of childish things that have wonderful value in God’s eyes.14 Mortification, too, consists of apparently insignificant details. I cannot understand, our Father assures us, a saintly soul who does not practise mortification. And mortification must be sought in small and

13 The Way, 626 14 The Way, 859 52 Living in Christ

ordinary matters, in intense, constant and well-ordered work. Little things that will not make your health suffer, but which will keep your love burning. Mortifications at mealtime. Heroic minutes throughout the day. Punctuality. Order. Guarding your sight, with naturalness, while walking down the street. Very many details and opportunities used well. And one very interesting mortification: interior mortification

– so that our conversations are not centred around ourselves, so that annoying things are always received with a smile, to make life pleasant for those around us. Love, my son! Love with sacrifice.15 Along with the small mortifications that the faithful fulfilment of the Norms and Customs and the duties of charity require, each day brings with it new little demands on our love for God. And though they may prove difficult, we should offer them cheerfully. You have seen a child when his father asks him for a bit of the sweet he is eating. At first, the child resists. Then, he gives the sweet and, when his father smiles, the little lad sticks his hand into his pocket and gives him all that he has been saving up: some round and shiny pebbles, an empty spool of thread, a knucklebone… And then his father hugs him with joy. Only a few things can we give to our Lord – little things, as that child did. Give them to him, my son! Give them to him! Because, if you do, your Father God will also give you a big hug! And you will be happy because mortification is joy.16 This disposition of self-giving (our love for God and for our neighbour being the same love) should be put into practice with our neighbour: through good example, by being good listeners, with a ready smile, by an offer to help… Love, with deeds: lending a helping hand, perhaps unnoticed, when someone has a lot of work; our good humour, our agreeable conversation and our sensitive fraternal corrections, so

15 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 14 16 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 14

Little things 53

charitably and kindly made, without mortifying. And when we see a defect in someone, may we learn to help that person, in an affectionate way, to overcome it before it gets out of hand. Fraternal corrections – made without being rude, without being tiresome and petty – are usually to be made about small, habitual faults. In this way we keep a fault from becoming bigger, and our behaviour becomes pleasing in God’s eyes: whoever then relaxes one of the least of these commandments and teaches men so, shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven; but he who does them and teaches them shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven.17 Affection is also shown in the material care of the house; our homes are pleasant homes. And this is achieved by paying attention to details… Your closing that door with care! – because we cannot turn our back on caring for the little things of the house. And also, when the sun shines brightly and we have to close the blinds. And later on, when we have to open them again.18 To make living together easier, and to make sanctity pleasant for the others, we always have to try to keep our home attractive and furnished in good taste – a task basically made up of little things. Our work is also made up of a multitude of details. And since we have to sanctify it, we should make a real effort to do it intensely and maintain an enthusiasm for finishing all tasks with the same eagerness with which we began them, and not abandoning them. Referring to the perfection with which sculptors had finished the carvings of a famous cathedral right down to the very last details, our Father commented: If these men were living today, they could belong to Opus Dei, because they worked before God.19

17 Mt 5:19 18 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 15 19 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 16 54 Living in Christ

A work of love We finish off things to be able to offer them to our Lord. Love is the reason for taking care of little things. It is not simply to cross off a list of things to do, or a list of instructions, a dead letter. Little things are discovered by our sensitivity, our ingenuity, our enthusiasm and our effort to find love for God and service to others on every occasion. For where there is no love, there is no virtue either; but rather manias or a Pharisaic attitude – always a danger – which would reduce everything to external perfection, something strictly formal, without spirit or love. We would pay the tithes on the mint, the anise and cumin, as the Pharisees used to do, and leave undone the more essential things of the law, of justice and of mercy.20 Little things are not intended to keep up appearances. Rather, each one is a masterpiece, that final touch, the crowning of something good which, if it were missing, would leave the work incomplete. And so, if love is what moves us to take care of details, if love is what suggests and feeds this care, then it would be good to try, as far as possible, to renew our offering; and tend to accompany each deed with an ejaculatory prayer, some detail of affection, an act of thanksgiving or of reparation, or with a petition. There is so much that we need to ask for! This way, at the close of each day, we will have offered, with the piety of children, many gifts to our Lord and to our Mother in heaven. Love in details marks all of our life with a special seal, and also creates a decisive way for us to fight in our ascetical struggle. My children, how well you know that there are small setbacks which, at times, seem very big because there was lack of love, of self-giving, of good spirit or of a spirit of sacrifice. But don’t worry. If you live the life of Opus Dei, of children of God, such a defeat will remain in the little things, on the periphery and never in something transcendental. And your small failure to love will quickly be remedied with a new act 20 Cf Mt 23:23

Little things 55

of love and with a renewed dedication. It is in little things that your struggle lies; and heaven smiles at one of these falls, so long as we continue struggling.21 Our tactic is to keep on fighting in small details, far away from dangers capable of affecting the foundations of our spiritual lives. This way we can always rectify in time. Besides, this type of ascetical struggle is splendid training. One little thing… and then another, and yet another, and a good habit is formed. A man so accustomed will not fail when big things come along; for he will have become well used to daily gymnastics, which strengthen the will.22 The acquisition and growth of good habits is an arduous task, and it is accomplished by the repetition of acts, often minute details. “First, we begin with the easiest things. And then, given this step forward, we are on our way towards perfection.”23 With such training, we are preparing ourselves to succeed at the time of trial because he who is faithful in a very little is faithful also in much.24 On the other hand, he who despises small things will fail little by little.25 Faithfulness in the details of each moment. Our conversation with our Lord develops in a climate such as this. Presence of God that teaches us to concentrate on little things: If I do not really need to go upstairs, I will not. If I do not need to go downstairs, I will not go either. And if I do not have to open that window, I will not open it; and so forth, like this, a thousand and one times. This is our sanctity. We cannot forget that when we fail to pay attention to some detail a thousand and one times, it is a disaster.26 It is a disaster because it presupposes a habitual lack of love, a state of lukewarmness, and
