21 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 17 22 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 17 23 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae 21, 4 24 Lk 16:10 25 Sir 19:1 26 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 18 56 Living in Christ

because it forms bad habits – by the repetition of acts – contrary to the virtues, thus preparing the way for big falls.

* * * * *

Little things: our way to sanctity. Little things: for us, the only way. A man who knows how to put into practice this doctrine must be happy, because he is fulfilling the will of God. If our happiness is so close at hand, let us not be foolish. Let us not stop living the spirit of our Opus Dei, which will give us heaven. We must be able to live it without fear, as something as continuous as the beating of our hearts. Beware of the day when it stops! It would mean that death has arrived. And on the day in which there is no struggle in our soul, we can also be sure that we have lost our interior life.27 May all of us, both in practising and helping everyone else to practise this characteristic which is so proper of our spirit, hear from Jesus’ lips: Euge, serve bone et fidelis, quia super pauca fuisti fidelis, super multa te constituam: intra in gaudium domini tui.28 Well done, good and faithful servant. You have been faithful over little, I will set you over much; enter into the joy of your master.

27 From our Father, Crónica, December 1961, p. 18 28 Mt 25:21

## THE AIM OF OUR VOCATION {#cuaderno.3.6}

My children, our Father told us, the children of God in Opus Dei ought to have just one exclusive concern, which is this: to become saints.1 The holiness that is asked of us in the Work brings with it a concern for others: to bring them closer to God, trying to carry out our personal, professional and social obligations with the greatest rectitude. I must remind you that we are in the Work because of a divine vocation; because God has called us. And he called us to give ourselves wholly, unstintingly, to be saints and to sanctify.2

Sanctity for the apostolate To sanctify ourselves by sanctifying. This is the aim of our vocation; the corporate aim of the Work – to sanctify ourselves and to promote Christian holiness in the middle of the world. It becomes the only personal aim of each one of us. For, if sanctity is summed up in loving God above all things, proselytism – which is nothing other than working so that other people love him – must be a necessary consequence of this charity. You have but little love if you are not zealous for the salvation of all souls. You have but poor love, if you are not eager to inspire other apostles with your craziness.3

1 Alone with God, 24 2 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 6 3 The Way, 796 58 Living in Christ

The earth is the Lord’s and the fullness thereof, the world and those who dwell therein.4 Our Lord has sent us to all men, of every social class, to remind them of the divine invitation to holiness. The world or life or death or the present or the future, all are yours; and you are Christ’s.5 There is nothing outside our mission, a mission which can be reduced to winning souls for Christ as Christ won us. Proselytism can never be a goal added on to our vocation, just as our calling does not properly add a new aim to that which we already had as Christians. But it does determine it, indicating the precise way to reach God more easily and more surely. Thus, proselytism in the Work is precisely the road, the way to reach sanctity. That is why, in Opus Dei, we cannot understand a holiness which is not zealous to win others, just as we cannot understand a proselytism which is not the expression of an interior life, one filled with the desire for holiness. This, then, is our only aim: to sanctify yourself so as sanctify. Your sanctity is not egoism. You receive the waters of God, and it is true that those waters have to fill you. But then – generously – you have to pour out from the abundance of your heart into all the hearts that live on earth.6 If proselytism is not only a condition of sanctity but a true goal, we can understand how no one under any circumstances can be dispensed from doing it. Not even the sick can be dispensed, because that would amount to dispensing them from being saints. “In that which is sought as an end, no measure can be used.”7 To limit the end is not really wanting it. The means, which receive their raison d’être (their goodness) from the end, are wanted only in as much as they are necessary. Opus Dei promotes many apostolic works throughout the world,

4 Ps 24:1 5 1 Cor 3:22-23 6 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 7 7 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Romanos lectura 13, 1

The aim of our vocation 59

and each day there will be more. It all depends on the circumstances of the country, the circumstances of the times, the circumstances of the souls and on the apostolates being appropriate. I had to name a few, since this is the habitual practice in the Church, but they are not ends: a residence, a school for domestic arts, a university… Are these ends? No, just as the spade and the hoe are not an end for the farmer, but the means for tilling the land. We use all these apostolic works, which are means. And what is the end? It is personal sanctification and the effort to see that the greatest possible number of people know and converse with Christ and, if he calls them, that they dedicate themselves to God in Opus Dei for the service of the Church.8 That is to say, it is proselytistic sanctity and proselytism that sanctifies us: two aspects of one and the same objective. One is the measure and the reason for the other: eagerness to win souls is the best sign of full correspondence to the divine calling. Only if we seek vocations will we reach sanctity; only if we are proselytistic will we live our vocation completely, will we be Opus Dei.

The danger of lukewarmness “If you are cold and indolent and are concerned about nothing other than yourself, and your happiness and even think in your heart: what have I got to do with the others? I have enough with my own soul; I hope I keep it entirely for God! Well! Do you not remember the parable of the servant who had a talent and did not want to trade with it? Was he, by chance, condemned for having lost it, or was it not because he did not want to trade with it? Think about it then, my brothers, so that it does not let you rest.”9

8 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 8 9 St Augustine, In Ioannis Evangelium tractatus 10, 9 60 Living in Christ

We all want to do proselytism. Who is not happy when new vocations come? Who does not hunger to perpetuate his apostolate?10 Only he who is not happy, he who felt burdened by the divine calling because of a lack of love, would not feel the desire to bring other souls to share what he has. When we see something as a good we want it for those whom we love. Poor interior life (love of God and for souls because of God: practical and effective love, with works) will always end up with our not evaluating our own vocation as a treasure and, at the same time, of not loving the others supernaturally. That is why when a person does not have zeal to do proselytism, it is because his heart is not beating, because he is dead. And you can apply to him those words of Scripture: iam foetet, quatriduanus est enim (Jn 11:39) – he is already stinking, for he has been dead four days. These souls, even though they were in the Work, would be dead, rotten, iam foetet. And I, says our Father, cannot go anywhere with corpses. Corpses I bury.11 It is not possible to persevere without proselytism, because it is not possible to persevere without love, without interior life, without supernatural outlook. However, it could happen that, under special circumstances, someone’s values might get turned upside down. Some goal of unquestionable importance – such as one’s profession, or any work – but which is simply a means, might then present itself as a sort of personal end, at the margin of this common goal of the Work, to sanctify ourselves by sanctifying and to sanctify by sanctifying ourselves, which ought to absorb all of our ambitions and efforts. How unhappy will be the soul that has lost this sense of direction! For after struggling hard, it will come, in the end, to realise how unfruitful those works have been. What good did it do him to sharpen the hook of his

10 The Way, 809 11 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 9

The aim of our vocation 61

profession well, prepare it with the bait of an excellent professional prestige, if all his efforts were taken up in this, if he did not want to cast it in the water and wait patiently for hours for souls to come. All his efforts will have been in vain; he was wasting his time. After someone has passed through circumstances like these and, in the end, through the mercy of God, his eyes are opened to reality, it is natural that he should be filled with repentance. But only in a renewed love for the principal goal (that of the whole Work) will he be able to recover his lost happiness. For even though there are moments of blindness, even in the midst of the most difficult contradictions, love your vocation, and it is there, in its depths, where you will find the love that you are seeking – that eager desire for proselytism.12 At other times it may happen that someone sees the end with noonday clarity; he realises perfectly well the need to do proselytism, but he does not use the means to overcome various obstacles: human respect, objective difficulties, shyness… He wants, but does not want. He sees that it would indeed be good to obtain that end, but he makes little effort to obtain it. He should be on the alert when he sees such ineffective desires filling the mind, because it is a sign that he is becoming lukewarm. He should examine himself to see whether, there in the depths of his soul, he loves his vocation as a precious good, or whether there are more or less unconscious motives which are keeping him from a complete and total dedication. It is necessary to react immediately, to talk sincerely in the Confidence and ask the help of our Lord and of our Lady so as to be able, once again, to make these words of our Father a reality in his life: That burning desire to win fellowapostles is a sure sign that you have really given yourself to God.13

12 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 10 13 The Way, 810 62 Living in Christ

Employing the means The reality of our desires for sanctity has to be shown in deeds and so we ask ourselves each week in the Circle about deeds of proselytism accomplished. Our aim is to strive so that there are many souls dedicated to the service of God in the middle of the world. Now is a good time to take stock. How many vocations have you brought?14 Our Lord is not sparing in giving us his grace, but he also wants us to use all the means for bringing many vocations. And what are these means? Those we have in the Work are very effective means: the work of St Raphael, the work of St Gabriel, and the work of the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross which has similarities with the other two works. In addition to these corporate means, and channelled through them, there is our own personal apostolate which forms the base, the motor, of everything else. It is a personal apostolate supported by three main pillars: First, prayer; then, atonement; in the third place, very much in ‘the third place’, action.15 Prayer is the most effective means of winning new apostles.16 Ours is a vocation of contemplative souls. That is why, when we want to win vocations, the first thing to be done is to ask our Lord for them constantly, while insisting without getting discouraged, knowing that our measure of time is nothing compared with God’s eternity. After praying, because prayer grows in value with sacrifice,17 we should offer up many small mortifications. Then our Lord will listen to our petitions, moving souls to give themselves. If you pray for them, sacrifice yourself, fulfil your plan of life, do your ordinary work well, add the little detail of smiling…, you fulfil the goal of Opus Dei. And

14 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 10 15 The Way, 82 16 The Way, 800 17 The Way, 81

The aim of our vocation 63

so the grace of God will reach even to the farthest corners because you are a bearer of Christ, as you carry him in your heart, in your mind, with your words and with your good example. My children, proselytism is a duty. And in the Confidence you should say: I have done this, I have thought about that, I have prayed so much, I have mortified myself, I have prepared this visit. And, if your brother or sister doesn’t ask you, you should tell him or her anyway.18 With all this preparatory work of prayer and small mortifications, action will be, as our Father says, the easiest part, because it will be the external manifestation of the interior life of each one. You know, then, St Augustine writes, “what each one of you should be doing at home with your friend, your neighbour, your subordinate, your superior, your inferior. You know also in what way God presents the occasion; in what way He opens the door with his word. I hope, then, you will not want to live in peace until you have won them for Christ, because you have been won by Christ.”19 Don’t you see how there exists in all people – in you and me, too – a sort of psychological prejudice, a sort of professional psychosis? For when a doctor sees another person passing him in the street, without even realising it, he thinks: ‘this person has liver trouble’. And if a tailor sees him, he comments to himself: ‘what a terrible suit… or what a good one, what a fine cut!’ And the shoemaker takes note of the shoes… And when you and I, children of God, dedicated through his love towards serving him in the middle of the world, loving all creatures, see people, we have to say: a soul, a soul that has to be helped; a soul that has to be understood; a soul with whom we have to live; a soul that has to be saved! 20

18 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 11 19 St Augustine, In Ioannis Evangelium tractatus 10, 9 20 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 12 64 Living in Christ

Action, personal apostolate. This means seeing in every person a soul for whom our Lord may perhaps be reserving a calling of predilection. Then looking for those souls and getting to know them, patiently and with charity, while following the advice which we have been given in the Confidence, so that our work will not be in vain and it may become an efficient part, organically grafted onto the activities of the whole Work. Our personal apostolate is directed, then, in the first place to fit our friends into the work of St Raphael or St Gabriel as soon as possible. And we do so, besides, because we realise that this is the most direct way for our Lord to give them the grace of a vocation. We have to meditate often on this reality of our self-giving, driving into the deepest part of our being: doing proselytism, winning vocations. What lights, what new energies, we will get out of this consideration! It is clearly not a case of doing our own apostolate but the apostolate of the Work, which is not an enterprise invented by men, but is rather a great supernatural enterprise,21 into which our Lord has abundantly poured his grace. The eager desire to win souls has to burn within us. We have to stir ourselves, because our Lord will not deny us his help, above all, if we know how to apply ourselves with self-denial in the works of St Raphael, St Gabriel and of the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross to widen the base, to develop these apostolates well, and to make them ever more effective. Our Father tells us that many vocations must come from these apostolates and, if they don’t come, it will be our fault, because we are not doing things as we are told: because we don’t do them cheerfully and in an organic way, in a constant way, and in a sanctified way. Cheerfulness, obedience, constancy, dedication. These are the means that we should employ always: a life of prayer, a life of sacrifice and 22 What the fulfilment of our duty in our professional and social work!

21 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 1 22 From our Father, Crónica, May 1963, p. 13

The aim of our vocation 65

a marvellous base for obtaining vocations! It is true that, once in a while, we will not be the ones to reap the fruits of our apostolic efforts

– I sent you to reap that for which you did not labour23 – but this is not what usually happens; for our Lord tends to reward generously and directly: Whoever does proselytism gets vocations. Whoever does little proselytism, gets few vocations. Whoever does a lot of proselytism gets a lot of vocations.24 Our Holy Mother Mary, Queen of the Apostles, will help us to be very proselytistic, so as to be very saintly.

23 Jn 4:38 24 From our Father, Crónica, March 1966, p. 11

## THREE DOMINANT PASSIONS {#cuaderno.3.7}

We have dedicated ourselves body and soul to God, with all the energies we possess. To serve our Lord and his Church in the Work, according to the personal circumstances of each one, has to be our absorbing and total commitment. You tell me, yes, that you want to. Very good: but do you want to as a miser longs for gold, as a mother loves her child, as a worldling craves for honours, or as a wretched sensualist seeks his pleasure? No? Then you don’t want to.1

Desiring passionately When someone truly decides to do something, when his life is centred on one goal, everything that is related to that goal – and to the extent that it is related to it – produces operative, vital and passionate inclinations. Thus the end is intensely loved and defended. Our capacity for attraction and repulsion, for interest and eagerness, is not bad in itself. On the contrary, “it belongs to the perfection of moral good that man should be moved to good, not only in respect of his will, but also in respect of the sensible appetite, as one of the Psalms expresses it: My heart and my flesh sing for joy to the living God (Ps 84:2).”2 Unloosed and uncontrolled passions would obscure judgement

1 The Way, 316 2 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.24, a.3

Three dominant passions 67

and would be a sure cause of going wrong, dragging the will along. But if it is guided by right reason by means of a well-ordered will, and prompted by faith, hope and charity, a movement of the passions is a sign of the intensity of willing. So, once we see what has to be done in the presence of God, we have to dedicate ourselves to it ardently, with all the resources that the Lord has placed in our nature. However, this passionate determination does not mean that we must always act with a physical enthusiasm. Sometimes God permits a lack of human motivation, as if testing our faith, and we have to go against the grain for a while. Then our faith in our own vocation is made ever firmer, our intention is purified, and our charity is made genuine and effective. We should not act out of enthusiasm. We should act from supernatural convictions, with love of God, which is the work of divine grace and human will. But we have to try to see that this love of our will is strong and complete, so that it draws along with it all the powers of our nature, overcoming our moods and external circumstances. Our love for Jesus has always to be vigorous and youthful, always nobly passionate. God cannot be loved moderately – as if not to exaggerate – or in a cerebral, phlegmatic, calculating manner, without heart. Diliges Dominum Deum tuum ex toto corde tuo et ex tota anima tua et ex omnibus viribus tuis et ex omni mente tua.3 Our Lord asks us to love him with our whole soul, and at the same time with our whole heart and with all our energies. Jesus isn’t satisfied “going halves”: he wants the lot.4 For him all our soul and body; for him all our powers and faculties. Our love for our Lord should never be an unfeeling love. A new heart I will give you… I will take out of your flesh the heart of stone and give you a heart of flesh.5 Our love has to be vibrant, with all

3 Lk 10:27 4 The Way, 155 5 Ezek 36:26 68 Living in Christ

the vehemence proper to souls in love. The life of self-giving – a life of love – gives rise to forms of thinking, feeling and acting, inclinations and tendencies, which enhance the operatio Dei that each one makes real with his entire life. They have been integrated into our personality in such a way that they are like supernatural instincts which move us and mobilise all our natural forces. Among these passions or living inclinations there are some which stand out above the others because they are more tightly bound up with our aims in the Work. Particularly, The children of God in Opus Dei have three dominant passions: to hand on the doctrine of the faith; to guide in one way or another the souls who draw close to the warmth of our apostolate; and to love the unity of our Work.6

Giving doctrine Man, with only natural lights incapable of knowing his own way – which is a divine way – needs Revelation, the word of God. This is why Christ came, to be the Light of the world. But in spite of the clarity that he enkindled, millions of souls are still in darkness. So many lives without direction, without joy! Blindness is a great natural misfortune; it is the privation of the sense which seems the most necessary. But a greater misfortune is spiritual darkness. It could well be said that ignorance is the greatest enemy of God, since we can love God only when we know him. Ignorance is the origin of many evils and a great obstacle to the salvation of souls.7 Every apostolate has necessarily to be based on doctrine, which is “the bread that feeds souls”,8 that nourishes intellects and lives. For this reason “he who frees a soul from ignorance deserves a greater honour

6 Alone with God, 18 7 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 47 8 St Augustine, In Ioannis Evangelium tractatus 41, 2

Three dominant passions 69

than he who gives a hungry man his fill.”9 A greater honour because the good is greater, because doctrine is necessary: This is eternal life, that they know you the only true God, and Jesus Christ whom you have sent.10 We, apostles to whom our Lord has given his own light so that we might also be the light of the world, have received this command from Jesus: Euntes ergo docete omnes gentes,11 go therefore and make disciples of all nations. We have to make the Gospel, the Good News, reach all those around us, all peoples. We learn the Word of God by studying, because we cannot expect extraordinary illuminations when we have knowledge, and doctrine at hand to teach it to others. The apostle is an intermediary, a messenger. That is why giving doctrine, accommodating it to the capacity of our listeners, is our great mission.12 Giving doctrine we become instruments, we participate in the power that the Word of Life has, to pluck up and to break down, to destroy and to overthrow, to build and to plant.13 Good doctrine is capable of freeing men from spiritual misery, of uprooting sadness, of destroying error and of laying the foundations for a new life. We have to be salt and light with our word and with our example, which is the best sermon. It follows that always and in all places we have to do good, making the truth known, and putting into practice those words: veritatem facientes in caritate (Eph 4:15).14 No one should pass by our side without our words and our actions having spoken to him of God. Awareness of the need, which souls have of the Word of Life, has to be a constant stimulus to give doctrine in season and out of season,15 taking occasion of our work, and creating

9 St John Chrysostom, Dial. hist. cum. Palladio, 12 10 Jn 17:3 11 Mt 28:19 12 From our Father, Crónica, June 1961, p. 9 13 Jer 1:10 14 From our Father, Crónica, June 1961, p. 9 15 2 Tim 4:2 70 Living in Christ

the opportunity with daring when it seems there isn’t one. We cannot wait for an ideally favourable moment, which normally will not arise. Just a small opening should be enough to put into action the deep supernatural sense of our calling, the passion of communicating the light of our Lord’s doctrine. Ignorance, the poor and human vision of things, has to cause us sorrow. We cannot remain indifferent when we see something go against the truth of God. With good manners towards persons, but always unyielding with error, we will dispel ignorance with the force and clarity of the divine word. The life of souls, the life that Christ won for them on the Cross, depends on it.

Directing souls A very effective apostolate is done with doctrine and with example. But we have to go further. We have to get into the lives of others as Jesus got into our life. Through the world still echoes that divine cry: “I have come to bring fire to the earth, and how I wish it were blazing already!” And you see: it has nearly all died out… Will you not help to spread the blaze?16 It is urgent to take souls to God, one by one. We have to have the same love and understanding, the same solicitude for those who live with us as Christ had in encouraging us, comforting us and directing us. We must follow this advice: “support others as the Lord supports you.”17 This interest in everyone – this divine craziness18 – has to move us to introduce ourselves boldly, at the same time tactfully, into the spiritual life of other people. Why are we going to get into the lives of others? Because we have an obligation, as Christians, to get into the lives

16 The Way, 801 17 St Ignatius of Antioch, Epistola ad Polycarpum 1, 2 18 Cf The Way, 934

Three dominant passions 71

of others. Because Christ got into our life! 19 We cannot be indifferent to those around us. We are not trying to ask anyone to give an account of themselves, still less do we require that they inform us of their interior dispositions. If we provoke confidences, if we tell them something which for a moment disturbs them, it is always to help them: to stimulate, to suggest, to propose, to nourish a crisis that will do good to our friends, encouraging them to get closer to God. Those well-timed words, whispered in the ear of your wavering friend; the helpful conversation you managed to start at the right moment; the ready professional advice that improves his university work; the discreet indiscretion by which you open up unexpected horizons for his zeal. This all forms part of the “apostolate of friendship”.20 The Lord wants us to spend time and effort with everyone in order to save all.21 We have to make our own the concerns and ambitions of our friends, to help them discover the need to talk to God, perhaps doing some act of piety with them, teaching them to offer up their work, encouraging them to go deeper to discover the reason for their waverings and failures, and talking to them about what we know they need. All this is compatible with really good manners, beginning if necessary with a ‘Pardon me: do not answer, if you do not want to.’ And afterwards, the question that hits the nail on the head.22 In this way one can, with time, carry out a supernatural and effective work with many people who do not even know what spiritual direction is and who perhaps would not ever want it.23 Is it not the advice that a mother gives her child, the advice of a good friend to a friend who is

19 From our Father, Crónica, June 1961, p. 10 20 The Way, 973 21 1 Cor 9:22 22 From our Father, Crónica, June 1961, p. 11 23 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 65 72 Living in Christ

wavering, spiritual direction?24 This work of direction, of help, is urgent in those environments in which, because of a wrong idea of Christian life, people shun spiritual direction. We must gain the confidence of our companions and friends, to reach the point of trust. At the beginning it is difficult for them. Afterwards, they need it. And it does so much good to souls! The apostolate of friendship and confidence will be crowned by bringing these souls to priests of the Work and by linking them up with our apostolates.

Loving unity We have to love Opus Dei passionately. And one of the clearest signs of this affection is loving its unity, which is its very life. For where there is no unity there is decomposition and death: omnis civitas vel domus, divisa contra se non stabit.25 Any city, any house, any family internally divided ends up destroying itself and disappears. Thus it has been said “care for unity, for there exists nothing better”.26 To care for the unity of the Work means being ready to defend it against any attack, if the time were to come. No external enemy, however strong it may appear, can harm either the Work or us, if we are well united, as an army in battle array.27 Thus, to love unity is above all to promote it, faithfully living the spirit of the Work, loyally fulfilling the jobs that we are given, uniting ourselves to the Father and to our brothers and sisters. Love for our spiritual unity. God wants a spirit as old and as new as the Gospel for his Work. A spirit that has to inform our life, and with which we will be able to fulfil the mission to sanctify ourselves and to sanctify others. Our Father is the deposit of this spirit. Another 24 From our Father, Crónica, June 1961, p. 11 25 Mt 12:25 26 St Ignatius of Antioch, Epistola ad Polycarpum 1, 2 27 Song 6:10

Three dominant passions 73

spirituality could be, and will be, very useful for other persons, but not for us. One same spirit for all, the same Norms and Customs, a common denominator that gives our lives a special family likeness and shapes us as children of our Father. We have to be eager to maintain the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace. There is one body and one Spirit, just as you were called to the hope that belongs to your call.28 To conserve this unity it is important to use the means of spiritual direction, which has as one of its ends to identify ourselves with the spirit of the Work. Therefore our concern for the others has to be constant, helping them with our affection, with good example, with fraternal correction, above all in points of spirit and behaviour which could go against our spirit, a spirit which is clear, one, well-defined, sculpted even in its slightest features. Loving the unity of the Work means to feel oneself forming part of this body wherever we are placed. We don’t care whether we are a hand or a foot, the tongue or the heart, because each of us is in every part of this body; we are one and the same through the charity of Christ that unites us. I would like, our Father says, to convince you of the need to see ourselves as members of one body. Unum corpus multi sumus (1 Cor 10:17). That all may be one, and that this be shown in unity of purpose, in unity of apostolate, in unity of sacrifice, in unity of hearts, in the charity with which we deal with one another, in the smile before the Cross and on the Cross. To feel, to beat all in unison! 29 And so it can be said of us, being many, a great multitude, what was said of the early Christians: multitudinis autem credentium erat cor unum et anima una,30 the multitude of the believers had the one heart and the same

28 Eph 4:3-4 29 From our Father, Meditation, 16 April 1954 30 Acts 4:32 74 Living in Christ

soul. All were perfectly united with only one will and with only one desire.31 Whenever it is appropriate, each one of us should be ready to do without those personal things which, even though legitimate, contribute more toward separation than towards union. And this, with a disposition not only of subordinating our own interest to that of the others, but even renouncing our personal things willingly and cheerfully when, as a consequence of this, a good is obtained for the Work. The intentions of the Father, the monthly intentions, the apostolates that the Work has entrusted to us, have to be in a special way the matter that occupies our mind and our imagination, that absorbs our attention and fills our work. Thus we will all beat with the same heart, united to the will of God. To obtain unity it is essential to be united to the head. Union with the Father, or with whoever represents him, is imperative for there to be fruits of sanctity and apostolate. The branch gives fruit if it is united to the vine. A branch that is not united to the vine, instead of being something alive, is a dry stick that is only good for the fire, or, at most, to drive the beasts, and to be trampled on by everyone. My children, be very united to the vine! Be very attached to our vine who is Jesus Christ through obedience to the Directors.32 Our Directors, because they have received this mission from Christ, are the vine, the source of life for us. They are the Good Shepherd who loves his sheep and gives his life for them. That is why our love for the unity of the Work, on which God hinges so much personal and apostolic effectiveness, has to be strong. An intense, passionate love which, in practice, means a constant petition for the Father and his intentions, in praying for our Directors and helping them, in loyally fulfilling our assignments, in

31 St Basil, Sermo 2, 1 32 From our Father, Crónica, June 1961, p. 13

Three dominant passions 75

obeying with promptness, faithfully, and carefully, seeing everything as a service to God and the Church.

Three strong sentiments of Christ In Jesus Christ “who possessed a true body and a true human spirit, human affection was not false”.33 After the expulsion of the dealers from the Temple, his disciples remembered that it is written, zeal for your house will consume me.34 The Lord went about all the cities and villages, teaching in their synagogues and preaching the gospel of the kingdom,35 to the point of exhausting himself each day. We contemplate him overcome by sleep, in Peter’s boat, and tired from the journey at the well of Sicar. Jesus Christ boldly introduced himself into the lives of men, into their worries. He comforted them, he enkindled them with his love. This is how he acted with the disciples of Emmaus, seeming as if to come across them accidentally: What is this conversation which you are holding with each other as you walk?36 And those discouraged men recovered their hope and found our Lord again: “Stay with us”… so he went in to stay with them.37 And their lives were filled again: “Did not our hearts burn within us while he talked to us on the road, while he opened to us the scriptures?”38 In an outpouring of love on the night of the Last Supper, after saying to the Apostles, I have earnestly desired to eat this Passover with you before I suffer,39 Jesus zealously asked the Father for unity among them: Holy Father, keep them in your name,

33 St Augustine, De civitate Dei 14, 9 34 Jn 2:17 35 Mt 9:35 36 Lk 24:17 37 Lk 24:29 38 Lk 24:32 39 Lk 22:15 76 Living in Christ

which you have given me, that they may be one, even as we are one.40 To give doctrine, to direct souls, to love unity: these are three strong sentiments of the soul of Jesus and three passions that Christ himself wanted to leave us as an inheritance.

40 Jn 17:11

## WORK OF GOD {#cuaderno.3.8}

Great and wonderful are thy deeds, O Lord God the Almighty! Just and true are thy ways.1 Our Lord, in inspiring his Work to foster sanctity among Christians living in the middle of the world while dedicated to an earthly task, has given to it a spirit and certain specific ascetical and apostolic means which are proper to the Work and suited to its aims. All of us in Opus Dei have to seek Christian holiness in our own state, in the exercise of a profession or trade. That is why a special characteristic of the spirituality proper to Opus Dei is that each one has to sanctify his profession, his ordinary work, to sanctify himself in his profession, and to sanctify others with his profession. And this is the heart of the Work, my children. For, after two thousand years, we have reminded all mankind that man was created to work: Homo nascitur ad laborem, et avis ad volatum (Job 5:7) – man was born to work and the bird to fly.2

Sanctifying one’s profession In telling the story of the creation of the world, Scripture insists on the pleasure of the Lord in the work of his hands: God saw everything that he had made and, behold, it was very good.3 But our Lord wanted

1 Rev 15:3 2 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 6 3 Gen 1:31 78 Living in Christ

one of his creatures to share in his power and so he created man, and put him in the garden of Eden to till it and keep it.4 With his work, man was to carry creatures to their earthly perfection, for the glory and honour of the God who had created them. And if, through sin, the first man changed this order, Christ, with his birth, life, death and resurrection re-established it, entrusting to his disciples, the Christians, the mission, through Him and with Him and in Him,5 of directing all creatures to God. All the things of the earth, material creatures too, including all the earthly and temporal activities of men, have to be brought to God, and now, after sin, redeemed, reconciled, each one according to its own nature, according to the immediate end that God has given it. But, at the same time, we must know how to see its ultimate supernatural destiny in Christ Jesus. For in him all the fullness of God was pleased to dwell, and through him to reconcile to himself all things, whether on earth or in heaven, making peace by the blood of his cross (Col 1:19-20). We have to place Christ at the summit of all human activities.6 And a principal part of this task is the sanctification of ordinary work; something which to many seemed unattainable and which the Work, with example and doctrine, shows to be an ideal within the reach of all. The truth is that any profession, any human task, from that of a statesman to that of a housewife, can be sanctified and brought to God. It is enough that the task be an honest one and, consequently, something that can be ordered and is, in fact, ordered to the glory of God. But besides having the right intention – Deo omnis gloria! – for someone to offer his work to God as a pleasing sacrifice, this work

4 Gen 2:15 5 Order of the Mass 6 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 7

Work of God 79

must be well done; that is to say finished and perfect as far as he can: You shall not offer anything that has a blemish, for it will not be acceptable for you.7 Work in itself has a moral value which has to be brought out. It is for this very reason, then, that our Father has so often reminded us that the essential part of this task – the sanctification of ordinary work – which God has entrusted to us, is the proper completion of the work itself, with the perfection (including human perfection) and proper fulfilment of all our professional and social obligations.8

Sanctifying oneself in a profession Professional competence, the human perfection of work with all of its consequences – of study, of practice – is only the beginning, the indispensable condition, the sine qua non. For, besides the practice of human virtues, the sanctification of daily work presupposes the application of the infused supernatural virtues: faith, hope and charity, fortitude and temperance, justice and prudence. And this is why the sanctification of one’s daily work ought to lead to the sanctification of the person who does it. Consequently, work which does not sanctify is work which has not been sanctified. While, on the other hand, a person who works in a temporal activity and seeks only God’s glory, not only keeps his work from becoming an obstacle toward living the presence of God, but converts it into a means for reaching contemplation. For, “when one of two things is the reason for the other, the soul’s occupation with the one does not impede or diminish its occupation with the other… As the saints consider God the reason for all that they do and know, their concern for sensible things, whether in contemplating them or in

7 Lev 22:20 8 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 7 80 Living in Christ

accomplishing them, in no way impedes their divine contemplation.”9 So work, whatever it may be, can be converted into true prayer. That is why we cannot say that a person who lives the spirit of Opus Dei is either active or contemplative; because action is contemplation, and contemplation is action, in unity of life.10 It is not easy however, to achieve this right intention which makes work a means of contemplation. The spirit and the asceticism which Opus Dei offers everyone, without distinction of race, language or culture, is directed precisely to this end. For, in order to live this presence of God in the practice of one’s profession, one needs the help of divine grace plus a personal effort toward making all actions really righteous and virtuous; in doing these things we do not seek ourselves, but become motivated instead only by the glory of God. Hence we make use of human devices and practices of piety – aspirations, acts of love and reparation – which are recommended in the Work so that cuncta nostra oratio et operatio a te semper incipiat, et per te coepta finiatur,11 our acts may begin and end always in God. Besides a right intention, sanctifying our ordinary work presupposes the constant exercise of the virtues which temper the soul, purify it and bring it even closer to God. Faith: that this little task, so tiny and apparently unimportant, is wanted by God as an instrument of coredemption and is very pleasing in his eyes. Charity: working only for the love of God and of all men for God. Hope – the marvellous reality of the Work is already a pledge – of our attaining our own sanctity and that of others. Justice: the fulfilment of our professional, family and social obligations. Temperance and humility: “The more a soul works with

9 St Thomas, In IV Sent., d.44, q.2, a.1, sol.3 ad 4 10 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1954, 20 11 Preces of the Work Work of God 81

rectitude of intention, the more humble the soul becomes and the more it advances in humility”,12 because one does not seek applause and personal satisfaction, but rather the fulfilment of the will of God. As our Father says: To do the work of three thousand while making the noise of three.13 Prudence: to consider at each moment what has to be done and how it is to be done. Fortitude: so as never to abandon work already begun, but rather to know how to persevere until we can place the last stone, as well as to overcome the difficulties or a possible lack of means. Ultimately, occasions and reasons for practising all virtues can be found while performing one’s professional work. And they can be found with complete naturalness, without doing anything unusual or odd, in our being men and women in the world, without being worldly men and women.14

Sanctifying through our profession If it is true that the profession is a means of sanctity for the person himself, it is also a means of sanctification for all other Christians, for we are the body of Christ and individually members of it.15 When an individual soul sanctifies himself, all the other faithful share in his spiritual goods through the reality of the mystery of the Mystical Body. Furthermore, work, in itself, can also have a directly apostolic function, which in no way destroys either its nature or its proper function but which, in fact, gives it its full meaning. This is the third aspect of the message which our Lord wants to spread in the world through his Work. In the first place, all work constitutes a contribution to the good of others, a more or less important aid for society. And this contribution to 12 St Dorotheus, Doctrinae 2, 8 13 From our Father, Meditation, 27 October 1963 14 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 9 15 1 Cor 12:27 82 Living in Christ

the temporal, secular common good is already a step toward leading souls to God. Humanly speaking, work is a source of progress, of civilisation and of well-being. And we Christians have the obligation to build the temporal city, both out of charity toward all other men and for our own personal perfection. Also, all human activities serve to give doctrine insofar as they are vehicles of ideas, and some more than others. For, in the practice of our profession, with complete naturalness, and without pretending to convert work into an instruction on morals, we can always give this doctrine. Personal apostolate rests on the basis of friendship. Without true friendship, which brings a knowledge and an understanding of the problems of others, it is very difficult to come up with the right word which a particular person needs, and difficult to reach people’s hearts and minds to bring them to God. So work, being an occasion for friendship, is also a very important occasion for personal apostolate especially in those two aspects which should always go together: that of giving good example and of giving doctrine. It is for this reason that the spirit of the Work insists so much on the need for Christians to acquire, in a noble and honest way, the greatest possible prestige among their colleagues in study or at work, whether this be intellectual or not, since this prestige will permit them to carry out an effective apostolate through their own profession.16

The importance of professional work To sanctify your profession. To sanctify yourself in your profession. To sanctify with your profession. This is the spirit of the Work which our Father summed up when he told us: Opus Dei is operatio Dei, work of God, sanctifying and sanctified work. It is an idea of sanctity which the Work has made possible throughout the world. With the

16 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 10

Work of God 83

beginning of the Work in 1928, my preaching, our Father wrote, has been that sanctity is not something for the privileged few. We have come to say that all the ways of the earth, all states in life, all professions and all honest tasks can become divine.17 It becomes clear, then, that those of us who have received a calling from God to the Work have a special obligation to seek sanctity through the practice of our profession, in sanctifying it as well as sanctifying others through it. For, if Opus Dei is operatio Dei, work of God, then each one of us should also be Opus Dei, work of God. A vocation to the Work leads us to live with a personal dedication to the service of the Lord and, for Him, to the service of all souls without any exception, in the exercise of one’s own profession or trade right in the middle of the world, which we love – each in his own state in life.18 Thus work becomes the prime matter of our sanctification. It is, in the first place, the indispensable condition to receive the call; at least a positive disposition for work should be present, if industriousness is not already a habit. Hence, being a good worker is one of the principal criteria of selection in the work of proselytism. Without work we could not possibly live our vocation, for we would be neither sanctifying ourselves nor sanctifying others. And being occupied in just any work, as something to pass the time, is not sufficient. It must be serious, stable and effective work. In a word, it must be truly professional. Its apparent human standing is of no consequence. Stand by your covenant and attend to it and grow old in your work.19 Consequently, no one in the Work can be without a profession. When, because of the apostolic needs of the Work, someone has to leave his ordinary professional work – almost always temporarily – to dedicate

17 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 10 18 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 10 19 Sir 11:20 84 Living in Christ

himself more fully to the internal work of formation, of governance or of the running of corporate works, then this work immediately becomes his profession. The same thing frequently happens in other families: financial or family circumstances, etc. lead some people to take on a professional occupation different from the one they had originally chosen. The function of governance, our Father says, becomes one’s professional work, even though you have to abandon medicine, architecture or research work. For even your periods of rest (involving a change of activity over some period of time), an illness or even old age, when the time comes, will transform itself into your professional work. In this way, in accordance with the spirit of the Work, there will be no interruption in anyone’s search for sanctity which, for us, depends upon professional work as a door rests on its hinges.20 Our piety (the sacraments, our Norms and Customs) is the traditional one in the Church – the same means that Christians have employed to reach sanctity from the beginning of Christianity. They are also pious practices suited to men and women working in the middle of the world, whatever their work. These means easily adapt themselves, as a glove to the hand, to our life of intense, serious work, and they lead us toward that unity of life which effectively turns our work into prayer, and makes our prayer the source of spiritual energy that, in turn, sanctifies our professional work. Without a life of work, we could never persevere in Opus Dei. If someone should come to the Work and does not work; if they do not remedy such an inclination to laziness, they will be out on the street in two days.21 In an ancient document of the early Church, this need for work is expressed with admirable simplicity: “a brother, if he wants to set himself up among you let him have a job; let him work and so, eat.

20 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 11 21 From our Father, Crónica, March 1965, p. 11

Work of God 85

And if he has no trade, provide for him according to your prudent judgement so that there be no lazy Christian among you. If he does not want to do this, he is a betrayer of Christ. Be alert against these.”22 With a very graphic phrase, our Father has shown us this need to work hard to attain the aims of Opus Dei – our own sanctity and that of other souls: We have a chronic illness in Opus Dei, and it is work. It is contagious, incurable and progressive: we do not know how to be idle.23 And, as a soul progresses along our way, he begins to work better, becomes more holy and more apostolic, for the love of God and for the love of others, because of God. That is why we ask our Lord for a long life, full of human and divine work, until we finish exhausted, squeezed out, and unable to give any more of ourselves because we will already have given everything, in a complete sacrifice, in a holocaust of love. We have the examples of our Lord and of the Blessed Virgin: lives of work without human brilliance, but with redeeming effectiveness.

22 Didache 12, 3-5 23 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 14

## THE WAY OF OBEDIENCE {#cuaderno.3.9}

At the end of each day of creation, Scripture says that God saw that it was good,1 that what he had made was good. God was pleased with the goodness of each of the creatures that came from his hands. And at the end of the work of the sixth day, having made man in his image and likeness,2 God saw everything that he had made and, behold, it was very good.3 Sacred Scripture tells us that this divine work had reached a special degree of goodness: valde, very good. Man is the most perfect creature on earth. God endowed him with reason and free will and left him in the power of his own inclination.4 Thanks to this freedom, man can give God a glory superior to that of irrational creatures, who fulfil his design necessarily. God speeds the lightnings of his judgement. Therefore the storehouses are opened, and the clouds fly forth like birds. In his majesty he amasses the clouds, and the hailstones are broken in pieces.5 The stars shone in their watches, and were glad; he called them, and they said: “Here we are!” They shone with gladness for him who made them.6 Man alone is associated with God in his dominion over the world and takes an active part in its government: Let us make man, God said, in our image, after our

1 Gen 1:25 2 Cf Gen 1:26 3 Gen 1:31 4 Sir 15:14 5 Sir 43:14-16 6 Bar 3:34-35

The way of obedience 87

likeness; and let them have dominion over the fish of the sea, and over the birds of the air, and the cattle, and over all the earth, and over every creeping thing that creeps upon the earth.7 In addition, God raised man to the supernatural order, and he vested him with gifts with which he could practise virtue effortlessly and live without pain or suffering. And then, after enjoying happiness in this life, he would reach the happiness of contemplating God face to face, something completely unmerited and totally exceeding the rights of human nature. But God gave man a command: You may freely eat of every tree of the garden; but of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat, for in the day that you eat of it you shall die.8

The disobedience of our first parents St Augustine says, “I have considered the matter a great deal, and I am at a loss for words to convey the joy I find in the line explaining that the tree was not harmful because of its fruit. For he who made all things exceptionally good made nothing bad in paradise. Evil came to man because he transgressed the precept. It was indeed fitting for man to be forbidden something; for placed under God the Lord, he could then, through the power of obedience, merit the possession of his Lord. I can truly say that obedience is the virtue proper to the rational creature who acts under God’s power, and that pride is the first and greatest of all vices, leading man to want to use his powers to his own ruin, and bears the name of disobedience… Hence the tree was not evil, but it was called the tree of the knowledge of good and evil because if, after it was forbidden, man ate from it, he would transgress the precept and thereby he would learn, in experiencing the penalty, what a difference there is between the good of

7 Gen 1:26 8 Gen 2:16-17 88 Living in Christ

obedience and the evil of disobedience.”9 Through the disobedience of our first parents, disorder and evil entered that world which is very good like everything from the hands of God. Adam’s disobedience has left in our nature an imprint, so to speak, of original sin, a principle of rebellion which is rooted in pride and which inclines us to evil. “Before they disobeyed, Adam and Eve were pleasing to God, and God rejoiced in them. And even though they had an animal body they felt no rebellious movements against their wills. This harmony was due to the order of justice; for, just as the body, which the soul had received, was submissive to the soul’s needs, so, the soul was submissive to the Lord. Thus, the body obeyed, offering its proper service without resistance… But after the disobedient soul rejected the law of its God in that transgression, it began to feel the rebellion of the body up to then its servant.”10 Likewise, the goods of the earth became difficult and had to be won with effort and fatigue. In the sweat of your face you shall eat bread,11 God said to Adam when he cast him out of paradise.

The obedience of Christ After the fall, it is implicitly understood in God’s promise of redemption that there is need for a reparation proportionate to the disobedience which gave rise to all sin. For as by one man’s disobedience, many were made sinners, so by one man’s obedience, many will be made righteous.12 “In obedience to the will of the Father, Jesus Christ inaugurated the kingdom of heaven on earth; he revealed this mystery to us and by his obedience brought about the redemption.”13

9 St Augustine, De Genesis ad litteram 8, 6, 12 10 St Augustine, De pecc. mer. et remis. 2, 22, 36 11 Gen 3:19 12 Rom 5:19 13 Second Vatican Council, Lumen gentium, 3

The way of obedience 89

Our Lord came into the world in fulfilment of the divine decree of redemption to satisfy by his completely free obedience, for our disobedience. For it is impossible that the blood of bulls and of goats should take away sins. Consequently, when Christ came into the world, he said, “Sacrifices and offerings you have not desired, but a body you have prepared for me; in burnt offerings and sin offerings you have taken no pleasure. Then I said, ‘Lo, I have come to do your will, O God,’ as it is written of me in the roll of the book”.14 Jesus Christ, above all, obeys the will of the Father. To obey is better than sacrifice,15 Scripture tells us. St Gregory comments, “Rightfully is obedience put before sacrifices because through victims we immolate the flesh of another, while in obedience we immolate our own will.”16 So, from his first days on earth, he whom the winds and sea obey,17 Jesus Christ, teaches us that the way of goodness and salvation lies in obedience. Today, my children, more than ever, when disobedience, backbiting, deception and intrigues are the order of the day, we must love obedience. Have you not often noticed how people obey lately? What a pity! They feel they have the right to question everything. Even among people dedicated to the service of God there are some for whom everything has to be questioned: whether a superior can command this or that; whether they have authority in these questions or in those… In Opus Dei we know this much: anything can be asked of us, with the greatest respect towards our personal freedom in political and professional matters, provided that it is not an offence against God. The need for obedience does not arise solely from reasons of organisational efficiency; it is not only a question of self-surrender: the surrender of one’s own will, the most difficult thing to give, since it is

14 Heb 10:4-7 15 1 Sam 15:22 16 St Gregory the Great, Magna Moralia, 14 17 Mt 8:27 90 Living in Christ

the most intimate part in us. It is not even a question of love, although it is out of love that we must obey. The need to obey, which is higher than any human consideration or reasons of convenience, has its roots in the very mystery of the Redemption and is a principal part of the economy of our salvation. “Causes are similar to their effects. The disobedience of our first parents, which was a matter of injustice, makes sinners and unjust men. The obedience of Christ, which is essentially justice, produces just men.”18 Whoever tried to limit his obedience would limit the possibility of his union with Jesus Christ, the Redeemer. Have this mind among yourselves, which was in Christ Jesus, who though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, taking the form of a servant.19 Therefore, whoever aspires to a fully Christian life is not content merely with fulfilling the precepts of the moral law, but like Jesus Christ hungers to obey, to go beyond the strictly obligatory, desiring to co-redeem. For though I am free from all men, I have made myself a slave to all, that I might win the more.20 This is our self-giving in the Work: whoever comes to the Work of God must be convinced that he has come to submit himself, to empty himself and not to impose his own personal viewpoints. In a word, he or she must truly decide to become a saint.21 The path to holiness cannot be different from that which Christ followed.

The fullness of obedience With this eagerness of ours to obey, we tackle at the root that pride which was the cause of original sin and which is also the cause of all our personal sins. “It is characteristic of the proud person to follow his 18 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Romanos lectura 5, 8, 5 19 Phil 2:5-7 20 1 Cor 9:19 21 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934, 17

The way of obedience 91

own will, because the proud person seeks greatness, desiring not to be governed, but to govern others. And that is why St Paul, in wanting to have us fully understand the perfect humility of Christ’s Passion, tells us that he became obedient (Phil 2:8).”22 Obedience is a basic virtue, “in a certain way, the mother and guardian of all other virtues of a rational creature.”23 Conversely, “evil is nothing other than disobedience to God.”24 He who obeys necessarily lives all the other virtues, since charity itself – the new commandment – is also a precept that has to be obeyed. “Hence, we should seek one thing alone, doing so at all costs – to fulfil everything that is asked of us and to show our obedience through deeds. Then, we will have truly achieved everything,”25 for he who keeps my commandments, he it is who loves me.26 In a certain sense obedience comprises everything. That is why our Lord could say, My food is to do the will of him who sent me.27 That was his life. With such a model, how can we keep from loving this virtue? Reflect now, on how necessary the spirit of obedience should be for a son or a daughter of God in his Opus Dei: since our Lord himself came in order to obey, spending the greater part of his life subject to two creatures, Mary our Mother, and that just man, Joseph! How are we not going to obey when we see that Jesus himself has made them write two of his biographies, in a few words, both summarising the virtue of obedience? One of them states erat subditus illis (Lk 2:51) and the other tells us obediens usque as mortem (Phil 2:8). Our Lord obeyed not only in the easy things but he “learned

22 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Phil. lectura 2, 8, 2 23 St Augustine, De civitate Dei 14, 12 24 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae 59, 3 25 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae 17, 7 26 Jn 14:21 27 Jn 4:34 92 Living in Christ

obedience through what he suffered”.28 “In other words, obedience is a serious matter: for our Lord obeyed, even unto death on the Cross in things that were important and most difficult.”29 And so, faced with the imminence of the Passion, he prayed to his Father: My Father, if it be possible, let this cup pass from me; nevertheless, not as I will, but as you will.30 “Thus the greatness and praiseworthiness of the virtue of obedience is very clear, because an act of obedience turns into something great whenever, on following the indication of another, one goes against one’s own tendencies. The human will tends toward two things: life and honour. But Christ did not refuse to suffer death: For Christ also died for sins once for all.31 Nor did he refuse ignominy: he became obedient unto death, even death on the cross,32 which is the most ignominious of all deaths.”33 St Paul continues, Therefore God has highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name which is above every name, that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, in heaven and on earth and under the earth, and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father.34 Since we desire to follow in his footsteps, we must ask him, to be able to apply it to our lives, Jesus, how did you obey? Usque ad mortem, mortem autem crucis (Phil 2:8). Even to death, and death on a cross. We have to obey, regardless of the cost, leaving our skin behind. Ordinarily, this will not happen. But if it does, don’t worry: Jesus went that far. My children, in these thirty-six years, our Father said in 1963, I have seen brothers of yours perform many heroic deeds and I have 28 Heb 5:8 29 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Hebraeos 5, 8, 2 30 Mt 26:39 31 1 Pet 3:18 32 Phil 2:8 33 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad Phil. lectura 2, 8, 2 34 Phil 2:9-11

The way of obedience 93

often had the impression of living among saints, among men capable of obeying usque ad mortem, mortem autem crucis. I have seen brothers of yours carry out calmly, and perfectly aware that their lives were at stake, marvellous acts of obedience, for the sake of the Holy Church, for the Roman Pontiff, in order to serve. We need to obey, to serve. There is no finer self-mastery than that of knowing we are serving, serving voluntarily! That is the way that great honours are won, earthly ones and heavenly ones. If through love we are dedicated to being useful in this service which is a war of peace, which is always of love and joy, at the cost of a life of self-giving, then, let us go with Christ to obey on the Cross! Otherwise it’s no good. And when we feel pride surging up inside us, making us believe that we are supermen, then will be the moment to say ‘No!’ That way, my children, perhaps people will be able to say of us that we tried to be good children of God, that we passed through life, with faults, but doing good, that we struggled to obey. And when death comes, as it must come, we will greet it joyfully, as we have seen some of your brothers die, joyfully; because like Christ we will rise. And if we have imitated him in doing good, in obedience and in his Cross, we will receive the prize of his Love: surrexit Dominus vere! (Lk 24:34). The Lord has risen indeed! Do you see? He conquered death.35

35 From our Father, Meditation, 24 December 1963

## HUMILITY AND CHARITY {#cuaderno.3.10}

“Two loves founded two cities: worldly society has flowered from a selfish love which dared to despise even God; and the heavenly kingdom is rooted in a love of God that is ready to despise self. The first city glories in itself, but the second in God.”1 We cannot stop loving, for love is a natural tendency, life’s natural impulse. Ultimately, there are only two possible loves: either we love good itself because it is worthy of being loved and then, at least implicitly, we love God, the Supreme Good, above all things, plus everything else because it shares in his goodness. Or we love what is advantageous for us, what we like, what is in accord with our interests and then we love ourselves above all things, and then we love things – and God himself – egoistically, because they bring us personal satisfaction. All possible loves can be reduced to one of these two, and depending on whether the will is directed toward one or the other, the soul is upright or it becomes proud; it tends, then, either toward God or it centres everything upon itself in a disorderly way. It constructs the city of God, the temple of the Holy Spirit, or the sad stronghold of selflove, the little shrine of self-idolatry. Because of this inevitable polarity of love, we need these two fundamental, capital virtues: humility and charity. We need humility, a stable disposition (a virtue) which avoids the disordered love of self-

1 St Augustine, De civitate Dei 14, 28

Humility and charity 95

excellence. And we need charity, a permanent force, a positive orientation towards God. Without charity, the driving force and the right order of humility would be missing. For, since love must have an object, it would fall upon itself. And without humility, there would not exist that indispensable good disposition, that essential interior void for charity to fill the soul. What can dull our charity? Pride, our Father says. On the other hand, charity takes us out of ourselves, producing humility; the habit of humility becoming firmer, charity acts more easily. “Humility is the dwelling place of charity.”2

Self-knowledge “Humility disposes the soul in such a way that it can freely approach the acquisition of both spiritual and divine goods.”3 It is the foundation of charity because, only he who knows his own nothingness seeks God’s treasures, building the spiritual edifice on solid rock. Many bad falls are the result of placing too much confidence in oneself; and, when failure demonstrates that such confidence has been in vain, there is no longer any support and everything crumbles. But with humility, we see ourselves as we really are, a filthy rag, a worm, in the face of the greatness of God, preventing then, that an initial wrong intention could cloud our mind or deform things. Humility is the indispensable basis for making a good examination of conscience. Humility leads to contrition because it enables the soul to recognise its sins and failures in love, and moves it to ask for pardon sincerely, in the same way it moved the publican in the parable, knowing himself as he was: wretched – no excuses. At the same time, it ensures that this sorrow never turns into despair, but makes it remain a sorrow full of confidence in God. Because he is infinitely good, God pardons our errors,

2 St Augustine, De sancta virginitate 51 3 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.161, a.5 ad 4 96 Living in Christ

and he appreciates our rectification, our starting again, our efforts to be like him and to get closer to him.4 All egotistical attraction, all temptation to love oneself disappears when humility shows that there is no reason for it. Because our closing the door of our heart to our own ego makes it all the more easy to raise our heart to God. St Augustine recognised this well when he wrote: “Do not go to him who does not dare lift up his eyes to heaven, oppressed by the weight of his iniquity, but to him who came from heaven, drawn by the weight of charity. Do not go to her who washed the feet of the Lord with tears, seeking the pardon of her grave sins, but to him who after granting them the pardon of their sins, washed the feet of his servants…. I will not offer you as a model the publican who humbly accused himself of all his sins, but I fear in you the pharisee who proudly boasted of his merits. I will not tell you to be like that woman of whom it is said: ‘Many sins have been forgiven her because she has loved much’; but I fear that you love too little because you feel that you have been pardoned little.”5 Humility also discovers that all the good in us is from God. It is not ours because from his fullness we have all received.6 The truly humble person knows his personal qualities, but he does not use them as a pedestal. They are gifts that he must place at the service of God and of his neighbour. The humility that the Work demands is something very much on the inside. It is something derived directly from the contemplative conversation that we maintain with our Lord sine intermissione (1 Thess 5:17). It is the profound sense that it is God our Father who is the one who does everything, making use of the poor instrument which each one of us is, servi inutiles sumus (Lk 17:10).

4 From our Father, Crónica, March 1962, p. 7 5 St Augustine, De sancta virginitate 37 6 Jn 1:16

Humility and charity 97

He plays with each one of us as with children: Ludens in orbe terrarum et deliciae meae esse cum filiis hominum (Prov 8:31).7 And if the knowledge of our personal misery leads us to ask God’s pardon, the knowledge that the gifts we have received demand selfgiving moves us to be grateful and to have a sense of responsibility because we realise just how much God loves us. I give thanks to God always for you, says St Paul, because of the grace of God which was given you in Christ Jesus, that in every way you were enriched in him with all speech and all knowledge.8 Humility gives us compunction and sorrow of love; humility also makes us grateful, enkindling more love because it makes us feel loved.

Understanding others Once the road of love for God has been opened to us by humility, love also reaches out to our neighbour. Faced with the defects of others, sometimes so obvious, humility keeps us from being scandalised. It is no longer surprised by anything: it now understands everything. There is, writes St Augustine, “no sin or crime committed by another man that I am not capable of committing, by reason of my own weakness. And if I have not yet committed it, it is because God in his mercy, has not permitted it, but has preserved me in goodness.”9 Although humility leads us to see even our own lack of right intention, it does not enable us to judge that of others. Not even the angels can see the lack of right intention in another with certainty, because only God penetrates into the heart. One can see and understand the weaknesses of a neighbour, but there is a wall which impedes our knowing his intention. The terms of comparison that we have (our own

7 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 31 8 1 Cor 1:4-5 9 St Augustine, Confessions 2, 7 98 Living in Christ

ego, even with malice in our intention) should not fail to benefit others, because they always turn out – at least because their intention is unknown to us – being probably better than we are. Humility also facilitates the positive action of charity. The right disposition toward the true good – toward God – knows how to find many good things that belong to God and come from God. When one attributes to oneself the good things that he or she has and he loves them in so far as they are his – or so he thinks – whatever belongs to others is presented to him as something foreign and which neither comes from him nor favours him, and there is no reason for loving the qualities of others. If anything, there could be reason to envy the qualities of others, because they have something which one is lacking. We shouldn’t believe that we are the centre of the universe and that everything ought to revolve around us. And the worst thing is that if you fall into this defect, when someone tells you that you’re proud you won’t believe it. For while the humble person thinks he is proud, the proud person thinks he is humble.10 How differently the truly humble soul sees things! It loves all that is good because it comes from God. Who it belongs to is unimportant, for he loves equally what is his own as what belongs to his neighbour, since everything comes from God. He feels the brotherhood that the first Christians knew how to live so well. They had all things in common; and they sold their possessions and goods and distributed them to all.11 But humility goes even further. It makes us grateful for the things, big and small, which people do for us. We feel ourselves more obliged to love them. But to the proud person nothing seems enough; he does not notice the charity of his brothers and he may even interpret it

10 From our Father, Meditation Living for the glory of God, 21 November 1954 11 Acts 2:44-45

Humility and charity 99

wrongly, because, pride can stop the charity of the others from entering into our hearts, and it makes us feel alone. Recognise it clearly, my children.12

Condition for grace and effectiveness Humility also brings with it grace and love of God; he pardons and forgets our sins. How clear this is in the parable of the publican! After a humble prayer, that man went down to his house justified,13 because God fills a humble soul, a soul that is wide open to his action, with charity. We can then, in honour of God, sing that hymn which sprang from the heart of our Mother: He has shown strength with his arm, he has scattered the proud in the imagination of their hearts, he has put down the mighty from their thrones and exalted those of low degree; he has filled the hungry with good things and the rich he has sent empty away.14 This is why our Father tells us: How effective we shall be as long as we don’t lose our humility, as long as we don’t lose this selfknowledge! I usually give the example of the dust that is lifted up by the wind until it forms a golden cloud high up in the sky because it reflects the rays of the sun. In the same way, the grace of God lifts us up high. And all this wonder of goodness, of wisdom, of effectiveness, and of beauty, that is God, reflects from us. If you and I realise that we are but dust and misery – of no value – our Lord will do the rest. It is a consideration that fills my soul.15 Humility is a necessary prerequisite for charity. For, without humility, no charity is possible, nor any good dispositions, nor the honesty needed by love. When humility is missing, there is no true love 12 From our Father, Crónica, March 1962, p. 10 13 Lk 18:14 14 Lk 1:51-53 15 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 4 100 Living in Christ

of God. Rather, there is nothing but self-love, to which everything else becomes subordinated. And, without humility, the soul is not docile to grace and, without grace, there can be no true love, either for God, for ourselves or for others. Be humble, be humble! because we know that we are in part made of clay, and we know a little about our pride and our miseries… and we don’t know it all. May we discover what is hindering our faith, our hope and our charity.16

Theological charity Through humility we do not place our love where there is no true good; we place it in God, Supreme Good and only good, in which all creatures partake. Charity, then, is needed to conserve humility, just as the best way of not going backwards is not to stand still, but to walk on ahead. Charity is a theological virtue (it has God as its direct object) and it makes us love sub ratione deitatis, God as the fullness of perfections, and most lovable, and ourselves and others because we are sharers in his goodness and beauty. First, we love God above all things. We love God for himself and because of himself, without regard for personal advantage; and in an absolute way because he absorbs all our capacity for love, he wants all. And love for God gives us all that love needs: it satisfies without satiating. True life is to be found in this absolute love for God, in selfforgetfulness and self-contempt. He who loves his life, loses it, and he who hates his life in this world will keep it for eternal life.17 Then love for others does not consist in making them subject to my personal interest or personal advantage: what I was told, what was done to me, what others care about me, whether they understand me…

16 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 6 17 Jn 12:25

Humility and charity 101

Others are from God and for God, and I should not expect them to serve me. What is important is that they serve God, that they sanctify themselves and that they are happy. Most people with personal problems have them because their egoism makes them think only about themselves. Give yourself, give yourself, give yourself! Give yourself to others, serving them for the love of God. That is the way! 18 True charity – disinterested love – is based on an indestructible foundation: God, who is above likes and affinities. God who is always in each person in some way, since every person, with the exception of the damned, is either a saint or has the possibility of becoming one. Loving others is the same as wanting the Greatest Good for them. But whoever sees his neighbour merely as a stepping-stone for his own interests or as a servant of his own whims cannot love that neighbour; he loves himself. Therefore, a person who is imbued with pride is incapable of doing apostolate, which is a true way of loving our neighbour because it is to bring him closer to God.

The order of love Much has been written about whether we ought to love ourselves more than we do others. In practice this question presents no difficulty. Our Lord, who took good care to insist on the need for loving our neighbour, for loving him as he himself loves us, even to say that this, together with the love of God, was the summary of his Law, did not ever feel the need to admonish us to love ourselves. Rather, he did just the opposite. In practice, we tend to love ourselves too much and others very little. It is true that we should love ourselves, but with order, with rectitude and for the same reasons that motivate our love for our neighbour – sub ratione deitatis – because we are of God and for God. So we ought to want for ourselves what is really good: sanctity. And we

18 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 22 102 Living in Christ

reach God – sanctity – precisely by following the way of charity, by renunciation, by self-giving, by forgetting about ourselves. Our principal true good consists precisely in living for God and for the others.
