To mortify oneself, to deny oneself certain material goods, leads to self-control on the natural plane. It maintains the supremacy of the spirit over the passions. But it can also lead to an assertion of the ego. On the other hand, on the plane of grace, embracing the Cross of our Lord, Christian mortification – that which we seek and which God sends us – leads to the renunciation of one’s ego to identify ourselves with Christ, so that Christ may live in us. This is the meaning of our mortification. He must increase, but I must decrease.26 For a child of God, mortification is not simply moderation. It is true death that gives way to true life. Not to a more balanced human life, but rather to divine, supernatural life. We supply only the pre-condition. God gives the increase and the efficacy, because he is the one who

22 Jn 11:16 23 Col 1:24 24 Jn 19:30 25 Mt 10:39 26 Jn 3:30

To die with Christ 149

sanctifies us. You need a more complete dedication so that when you have disappeared through humility and mortification, Christ will come to live in you: non ego, vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20),27 our Father says. The life of Christ in us, the life of grace is already an anticipation of the glory that is to be revealed to us.28 But until then, while we are on earth, we must remain on the Cross of Christ. Mortification, my son, mortification. I cannot understand how a soul can be holy without mortification. The need for mortification is so deeply rooted within us – the sine qua non of our effectiveness – that our spirit of mortification flowers in a multitude of details. I want it to be clear to my children through the centuries that the foundation of our life is prayer and mortification. My dear children, do not forget that the Holy Spirit is the fruit of the Cross. When moments of darkness come into the life of a Centre or of a person, if you have sought only the glory of God, if you have acted with the right intention, believe me: after the Cross, there will come the great fire, the great light, the great consolation.29

Mortification in ordinary life Paradox: to Live one must die.30 Die to live. Die to have supernatural life, to live a life of prayer. It is worthwhile; it is correspondence to the love of Christ who died for each one of us. To love the Cross is to know how to sacrifice yourself joyfully for the love of Christ, even though it is difficult and because it is difficult. For you have the experience that the two things are compatible.31 Joyfully, even though it is difficult, because the Cross with love is a cross, without a cross, which becomes

27 From our Father, Crónica, March 1963, p. 11 28 Rom 8:18 29 From our Father, Crónica, March 1963, p. 11 30 The Way, 187 31 From our Father, Crónica, March 1963, p. 12 150 Living in Christ

easy to bear. It is worthwhile mortifying ourselves for the love of souls, as St Paul did: I endure everything for the sake of the elect, that they also may obtain the salvation which in Christ Jesus goes with eternal glory.32 I protest, brethren, by my pride in you which I have in Christ Jesus our Lord, I die every day!33 It is a question of grace, generously given by God, and a question of the will, of personal effort on the part of man. If anyone would to come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me.34 It is certain that God respects our freedom. He counts on our free decision to save us. That is why he says: If anyone would. But we ought to want it seriously, because “if our will is not ready to die according to the Passion of Christ, neither will the life of Christ be the life in us.”35 And how are we going to die? How are we going to give our poor life? Once and for all? It may be that, in some exceptional case, God wants to give us the crown of martyrdom. But this is not the ordinary path our Lord wants us to follow. A person who makes great sacrifices one day but fails to mortify himself the next does not have a spirit of penance. A person who has a true spirit of penance overcomes himself every day, without fuss, offering God thousands of small things. That is the sacrificed love God expects of us.36 Mortification needs to be constant and generous in the little things of each day. Many who would willingly let themselves be nailed to a cross before the astonished gaze of a thousand onlookers cannot bear with a Christian spirit the pinpricks of each day! Think, then, which is the more heroic.37

32 2 Tim 2:10 33 1 Cor 15:31 34 Mt 16:24 35 St Ignatius of Antioch, Epist. ad Magn. 5, 1 36 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 15 37 The Way, 204

To die with Christ 151

Our Lord expects us to look for mortification in the ordinary and everyday things: in intense, constant and orderly work, knowing that the best spirit of sacrifice is perseverance in finishing with perfection the work that was started; in punctuality, filling the day with heroic minutes; in the care of the things that we have and use; in the desire to serve, that makes us carefully fulfil the smallest obligations; in small acts of charity so as to make the way to sanctity in this world pleasant for others – a smile can sometimes be the best expression of our spirit of penance.38 With desires to unite ourselves with our Redeemer Jesus Christ, with deep affection for particular works and for specific people – with names!

– we shall joyfully bear these daily pinpricks. Through these little things, because they are continuous, because we give them supernatural meaning – we really do die to ourselves, and really share in the Passion and Death of Christ; and so we fill ourselves with his Life. Thus we reproduce in our earthly passage, continuously and habitually, what Christ suffered on the Cross and what the Church applies to us in the Sacraments.

Passive mortifications To attain this life in Christ, imitating him in his death, we must make use of the unexpected, the contradictory, the difficult things that happen. That is why, among our usual mortifications, the first have to be the passive mortifications, those which we do not expect and which come anyway.39 Objective, real circumstances, not imaginary ones or those which result from a bad interior disposition which makes harmless things hurt. Our difficulties are often very subjective. Each of us takes on the

38 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 15 39 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 7 152 Living in Christ

contradictions he wants: he who lives in God has few, because when there is something objective he submits to the will of God. He asks for light to see clearly and that is enough. When the contradictions seem to abound, we should see if there isn’t a lack of mortification of the imagination, and a lack of supernatural outlook.40 It is not easy to accept difficulties lovingly. It is not easy to adhere unconditionally to the new life which God offers us and to live so remote from ourselves, so oblivious of ourselves, so lost in God, that there is no self-will, no rebellion… It is not easy, but it is essential if we want to identify ourselves with Christ. That is why we need an apprenticeship, an initial act of generosity that then goes up as if by steps: to be resigned to the will of God; to conform to the will of God; to want the will of God; to love the will of God.41

Active mortification of the senses To accept generously the purification which our Lord sends us, we must prepare with self-denial “not only in time of persecution, but in all our works, words and thoughts.”42 This active mortification helps us to accept lovingly and effectively the blows with which the divine sculptor chisels out the image of Christ in us. If we are generous in our voluntary atonement, Jesus will fill us with grace to love the trials he sends us.43 Active mortification has to be continuous, habitual; it has to take in each one of our senses and faculties because it is our whole being which must be transformed into Christ. Mortification, in the first place, of the senses which are doors of the soul. If your right eye causes you to sin, pluck it out and throw it away.44

40 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 7 41 The Way, 774 42 St Jerome, Epistola 131, 3 43 The Way, 221 44 Mt 5:29

To die with Christ 153

The soul united to the body needs bodily organs for its good and not for its ruin. That is why we have to mortify each and every one of the senses, surrendering them to love. Sight, so that it does not linger except on what leads us to God. Why look around you if you carry ‘your world’ within you?45 Hearing, to separate it from what separates us from our Lord. Taste, knowing how to put among the ingredients of the meal that most tasty one of mortification. Also, smell and touch. This is not a merely negative task. Mortification is not on the frontline with sin. It is to be found completely within the territory of the love of God, because it means depriving oneself of something which one could have had without offending our Lord. A mortified soul is not the one that does not offend, but rather one that loves. To live thus, as Christ, is folly to those who are perishing, but to us who are being saved it is the power of God.46 It is for us to mortify our feelings, the tendency to ‘have a good time’ as the prime reason of life. Mortification of the heart: detach it from exclusively human ties so as to tie it only to God. If your right eye scandalizes you, pluck it out and cast it away from you. Poor heart… that’s what scandalizes you! Press it, squeeze it tight in your hands: give it no consolation. And when it asks for them, say to it slowly and with a noble compassion – in confidence, as it were: ‘Heart…, heart on the Cross, heart on the Cross!’47 Interior mortification: fantasies, thousands of images that are not from God, that slow us down on the way to sanctity. The fantasy is a temptation which can coexist with work, with the time of prayer… Temptations which, although they bark a lot, do not bite if we do not let

45 The Way, 184 46 1 Cor 1:18 47 The Way, 163 154 Living in Christ

our heart follow the fantasy. Without great violence, but generously – which means promptness – we have to throw out these images and think about the things of God, yet fully involving ourselves in the task which occupies us, and use our memory to remember the blessings of God, the grace of our vocation: I will be mindful of your wonders from the beginning.48 A mortified memory is simply the memory of God. Mortification of the senses, mortification of our feelings, interior mortification. “The continuous separation of the body from its affections is a pleasing sacrifice to God. It is true worship of God,”49 since mortification is like the prayer of the senses. May those which are so often the occasion of impurity, help us to serve God.50

Mortifying the intelligence and will The two faculties proper to man which raise him above the animals, his intellect and will, also need constant mortification. The intellect is limited and it is difficult for it to know the truth. Nevertheless it dares to judge everything, to be the rule and ruler of the world. “Woe to those who do not attribute to God the cause and the origin of knowledge and wisdom! Because Christ is wisdom itself, we say that a wise person is one who shares in Christ.”51 Mortification of the intelligence, of our judgment, of the critical attitudes to the things of God, so as to maintain an alert, pure, clean intellect, humbly open to the clarity of God. If your eye is sound, your whole body will be full of light.52 Mortification of our curiosity, of precipitation in judgments, of superficiality. If our intelligence is mortified, our will follows it at every step,

48 Ps 76:12 49 Clement of Alexandria, Stromata 5, 11, 67, 1 50 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 9 51 St Basil, Comm. in Isai. 5, 176 52 Mt 6:22

To die with Christ 155

tending directly and openly toward the good. There are, however, many obstacles: diverse and multiple forms of the disordered love of oneself. And we must deny ourselves once and again, to order our loves, to place our will first on God, then in the others for God. Get used to saying No.53 No, to this haughty and proud self which tends to become the centre of the universe. No, to the non serviam! which means a ‘yes’ to the Love of God. Will-power. Energy. Example. What has to be done is done… without hesitation, without worrying about the consequences.54 And when this is done, once and again, even though it may be very difficult at times, so that our conversations do not centre around us, so that we always have a smile to greet annoying details, so as to make life more pleasant for the others.

Making life pleasant for the others If the matter for our mortification are the acts of each and every one of our faculties, its form is always charity. Sorrow and suffering voluntarily accepted are a sorrow of Love. It is to die in order to live. It is to let Christ live in us. And this charity is shown above all in the common things of everyday living: Little things that will not harm your health but that will keep you on fire. This mortification in little things is difficult but it is possible. Mortification like this, in a thousand details, is not something to be afraid of. It is as natural as the beating of our heart. I do not notice the beat of my heart now, but it moves, it beats. And woe the day it stops! I say the same thing to you: In our spiritual life, the life of our heart, which is that beat, is that effort to mortify ourselves at every moment.55

53 The Way, 5 54 Ibid, 11 55 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 10 156 Living in Christ

The desire – the continuous, daily desire – to convert the spirit of the Work into our own spirit, the root of all our thoughts and actions, ought to be based on mortification expressed in all kinds of details and specific norms. On our day on guard, that day when we are especially watchful and attentive to the sanctity of the others, there ought to be among other things the offering of an extraordinary mortification to our Lord. And there is a daily mortification for the Father. And that offered to our Lady. And we advise the sick to offer their sufferings with love. And we point out the importance of mortification so as to guard holy purity and for the apostolate…56 The love that inspires us to do mortification also determines the kind of mortifications we do. Choose mortifications that don’t mortify others,57 our Father advised us. And the desire to make the way of sanctity pleasant for the others is an important chapter. That witty remark, the joke held on the tip of your tongue; the cheerful smile for those who annoy you; that silence when you’re unjustly accused; your friendly conversation with people whom you find boring and tactless; the daily effort to overlook one irritating detail or another in the persons who live with you… this, with perseverance, is indeed solid interior mortification.58 In this way, we can practise mortification for the others in a wide field, delicately and gracefully. May you know how to sacrifice yourself cheerfully and discreetly in order to make life pleasant for the others, to make the way of God on earth pleasant. This way of acting is the true charity of Christ. Here mortification is shown in all its clarity with the cheerful and sacrificed character our Father recommended to us. Bear one another’s burdens, and so fulfil the law of Christ (Gal 6:2). You

56 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 11 57 The Way, 179 58 Ibid, 173

To die with Christ 157

ought to have an earnest desire, a very special determination to make life pleasant for the others without ever mortifying one another. Saying: I am going to put myself to a little trouble so as to make the divine way of the others more pleasant.59

Motives for mortification A mortified soul is ready to accept and love the will of God in any circumstance. All his sentiments are for God and through God for his brothers, his fellow men; because in dying through love so as to live in love, he recognises his dependence on God. He sees how much he has offended Him and he feels moved to make reparation for himself and, following the example of Christ, for the rest. Motives for penance? Atonement, reparation, petition, thanksgiving; as a means of progress: for you, for me, for all the rest, for your family, for your country, for the Church… And a thousand motives more.60 Atonement, reparation, petition, thanksgiving. These are the aims of our mortification. They are the same as those of the Passion of Christ and of the Mass, his perpetually renewed sacrifice. Reparation. Union with the redeeming sacrifice of Jesus. What does suffering matter if we suffer to console, to please God our Lord, in a spirit of reparation, united to him on his Cross; in a word: if we suffer for Love?…61 Sacrifice of thanksgiving with all our senses, with all our faculties, for so many blessings we have received and that we continue to receive from our Lord. Deo omnis gloria! – all the glory for God. The glory which men ought to give to God with their body and with their soul, with their thoughts, words and actions.

59 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 12 60 The Way, 232 61 Ibid, 182 158 Living in Christ

Sacrifice of petition. Our Lord awaits our prayer and our mortification to grant us all that we ask of him. Our Lord listens especially to mortified and penitent souls. That is why he listens to my children in Opus Dei who know that the contemplative life is founded on penance.62 Mortification, death in Christ, is life in Christ, holiness. And for Christ and in Christ, it is also life for the others, sanctity for all souls. Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit (Jn 12:24). Like the grain of wheat, my children, we have to die so as to give fruit. You and I don’t want to be alone. We want to multiply our family, leave a deep and luminous furrow. And to accomplish this we have to leave the poor animal-man and launch out into the fields of the spirit, raising all human things and at the same time the men who work in them.63 Look how the promise of our Lord becomes a reality! He who loses his life for my sake, will find it.64 And he will also find life for many other souls, so as to convert them to our Lord, as a proof of a great, clean, diffusive love. Pro eis sanctifico meipsum,65 for them I sanctify myself. To lead a mortified life, with serious, deep, habitual, profound, cheerful mortification we need the grace of God and personal generosity. Our Mother will teach us. Marvel at Mary’s courage: at the foot of the Cross, with the greatest of human sorrow – there is no sorrow like hers – filled with fortitude. And ask her for that same strength, so that you too can remain beside the Cross.66

62 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 13 63 From our Father, Crónica, April 1963, p. 11 64 Mt 10:39 65 Jn 17:19 66 The Way, 508

## CONFESSION {#cuaderno.3.16}

On the Cross, in the Passion and Death of our Lord Jesus Christ, the mercy and justice of God are united in a generously divine harmony. There we were redeemed and there God received abundant reparation for our offences. From the wounded Heart of Jesus, shedding its blood in a holocaust of love, springs forth the grace that takes away the sins of the world. Agnus Dei, we repeat each day at Mass, qui tollis peccata mundi, miserere nobis.1 Christ, who came to earth to offer the Father the only sacrifice that could be received in a sweet fragrance, spends his life in an effusion of mercy. I have not come to call the righteous, but sinners to repentance.2 He effectively comes to seek those who were lost, to change us, to kill death in us, to give us life in him. He is the Good Shepherd, who gave his life for his sheep,3 and he is the true Lamb who has taken away the sins of the world;4 by dying he has destroyed our death, and by rising, restored our life.5 The sacraments, guarded and administered by his Holy Church, are the channels through which this renewed life, this opus recreationis, this new creation that Jesus made from the Cross, will flow abundantly.

1 Order of the Mass 2 Lk 5:32 3 Cf Jn 10:11 4 Cf Jn 1:29 5 Preface of Easter I 160 Living in Christ

Baptism will lead us from the beginning of this life until the transition to never-ending happiness with the help of the Anointing of the Sick, passing through the plenitude of the Eucharist. The economy of our salvation is sacramental: the sacraments apply the Redemption of Christ to us and, in addition, each one of them gives us help for a specific need.

The sacrament of forgiveness Among the sacraments, as a prodigy of the inexhaustible mercy of God, is the sacrament of Penance. Christ on the Cross not only wanted to offer God a satisfaction of infinite value; he also wanted to make us capable of offering an acceptable reparation to God and of recovering divine life each time we lose it. Receive the Holy Spirit, he said to the Apostles, If you forgive the sins of any, they are forgiven; if you retain the sins of any, they are retained.6 Some time before, in Capernaum, he had told them: The Son of Man came to seek and to save the lost.7 What do you think? If a man has a hundred sheep, and one of them has gone astray, does he not leave the ninety-nine on the hills and go in search of the one that went astray? And if he finds it, truly, I say to you, he rejoices over it more than over the ninety-nine that never went astray.8 And immediately afterwards he promised to confer upon them the power of jurisdiction that the Apostolic College has today: Truly, I say to you, whatever you bind on earth shall be bound in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed in heaven.9 In instituting the sacrament of Penance, Jesus has his merciful eyes, those of the Good Shepherd, fixed on our weakness. With heavy heart until he sees us safe, he thinks about each one of us who is to come 6 Jn 20:22-23 7 Lk 19:10 8 Mt 18:11-13 9 Mt 18:18

Confession 161

afterwards. He thinks about the way we shall have to travel, about our sins, our deviation. For his love, each one of us is the sole sheep among the hundred. This sacrament of the mercy of God will allow St John to write: I am writing this to you so that you may not sin; but if anyone does sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ, the righteous; and he is the expiation for our sins.10 It is the sacrament of divine patience; the sacrament that shows our Father God watching each day at the gates of eternity, awaiting the return of the children who went away. That is why he tarries in coming to judge us. That is why he waits. The Lord is not slow about his promise, as some count slowness, but is forbearing toward you, not wishing that any should perish, but that all should reach repentance.11 Christ exercises this power of forgiving sins because he is God. With all authority he could say to the paralytic: ‘Take heart, my son; your sins are forgiven.’ And behold, some of the scribes said to themselves, ‘This man is blaspheming.’ But Jesus, knowing their thoughts, said, ‘Why do you think evil in your hearts? For which is easier, to say, “Your sins are forgiven”, or to say, “Rise and walk”? But that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins’ – he then said to the paralytic – ‘Rise, take up your bed and go home.’ And he rose and went home. When the crowds saw it, they were afraid, and they glorified God, who had given such authority to men.12 Christ, true God and true man, has this power by personal right. But in addition, he is going to let men share in it too: the Apostles, and their successors in the priesthood, to whom he confers the double power of order and of jurisdiction.

10 1 Jn 2:1-2 11 2 Pet 3:9 12 Mt 9:2-8 162 Living in Christ

“If we sin, we have to say: I acknowledged my sin to you, and I did not hide my iniquity; I will confess my transgressions to the Lord (Ps 32:5). For if we would do this and reveal our sins not only to God, but also to those who can heal our sins and wounds, these sins of ours will be erased by him who said: I have swept away your transgressions like a cloud, and your sins like a mist (Is 44:22).”13 The priest – good shepherd, judge, doctor, teacher, friend – will daily receive this wave of the sins of the world in his heart. Minister of the mercy of God, he shall also feel in his heart the weight of these sins; and if he is not always like Christ in the agony of the Garden, it is not because he does not have cause, but because of his very human smallness. But this very littleness will be an element of salvation, a step that makes the elevation of man to the things of God easier. In the tribunal of penance, the priest – untiring minister of the inexhaustible mercy of God – will make the divine dialogue, imbued with mercy, not only possible but easy: ‘Woman, where are they? Has no one condemned you?’ She said, ‘No one, Lord.’ And Jesus said, ‘Neither do I condemn you; go and do not sin again.’14 To make the reconciliation of the sinner easier, God has placed a man, a sinner too, enclosed in weakness, as the minister of his pardon. He can deal gently with the ignorant and wayward, since he himself is beset with weakness. Because of this he is bound to offer sacrifice for his own sins as well as for those of the people. And he does not take the honour upon himself, but he is called by God, just as Aaron was.15

The Good Shepherd ‘Who can forgive sins but God alone?’16 It is true: only God and

13 Origen, In Lucam homiliae 17 14 Jn 8:10-11 15 Heb 5:2-4 16 Mk 2:7

Confession 163

those to whom God wanted to give such power to be exercised in his name. An expression of this divine delegation to men, the sacrament of Penance, like every sacrament is a sensible rite, of a judicial character, in which the priest in the name of God grants pardon to a person who, sincerely repentant of his sins, has confessed them, and accepts the due penance. The power of Orders, received with priestly ordination, is not enough to exercise this divine power. The priest also needs the authority of jurisdiction, which is the faculty given to him to exercise the power of absolution in practice. This authority is indispensable because the absolution is a judicial act – to judge in order to retain or to forgive – and the judge who is not legitimately constituted as such, or who does not have jurisdiction, cannot judge. But confession, besides the strict judicial function, has a curative mission, a mission of teaching, a paternal mission of being a good shepherd. Therefore, to obtain all the fruits of confession, it is traditional in the Church to recommend the faithful always try to go to confession with the same priest. Thus he can judge with greater depth, cure with greater effectiveness, teach with greater clarity, guide with greater sureness. The better the confessor knows the penitent the greater will be the effectiveness of the confession. “For if the sick person is ashamed of telling the doctor about his wound, what medicine is unaware of will not be cured.”17 “In the same way that men do not make known their bodily illnesses to everyone, nor to just anyone, but to those who have the skill to cure them, so too confession of sins should be made to those who can cure.”18 Our Father has repeated this traditional doctrine to us many times using the same graphic image. Tell me: What does a sick person

17 St Jerome, In Eccle. comm. 10, 11 18 St Basil, Reg. brev. tract. 229 164 Living in Christ

do when he wants to be cured? He goes to a doctor who knows him: Look me over well, carry out tests, take my blood pressure and temperature. And the doctor thoroughly checks him over, and listens with his stethoscope, and looks at him with X-rays. And if the doctor acts as he should, he will make sure that the patient does not fail, through weakness or inadvertence, to tell him anything that might be relevant. And if the patient isn’t a madman, he will be quick to tell the doctor all the symptoms and circumstances that he thinks might reveal his illness, even the seemingly unimportant ones. He does not think of going to just any doctor – and then to another, and to a third, and so on – to be given an aspirin, but he hastens to the doctor who knows him well.19 It is the parable of the Good Shepherd that is fulfilled in each one of us. We have penetrated its meaning under the guidance of our Father. I wanted to point out to you once more, he has told us, what our spirit is in this marvellous means of sanctification, a means that was instituted by Christ, because it is a sacrament: Confession.20 It is a means of transcendental importance for our sanctification. Any priest who has jurisdiction and faculties can exercise the strict mission of the judge, although this mission can sometimes be obstructed by insufficient knowledge. But not the mission of the good shepherd. Do you know who is the Good Shepherd for my sheep? Whoever has the mission given by me. And I ordinarily give it to the Directors and to the priests of the Work. People who do not know Opus Dei are not prepared to be the shepherd of my sheep even though they may be good shepherds of other sheep and even though they may be saints. It is good for you to go to confession with the priests that are designated. And it has been laid down that you should go to them at least to receive a blessing. You can go to confession with any priest

19 From our Father, Meditation, 12 March 1961 20 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 11

Confession 165

who has faculties from the Ordinary. In this way, I defend freedom, but with common sense. All my children have freedom to go to confession with any priest approved by the Ordinary, and they are not obliged to tell the Directors of the Work that they have done so. Does a person who does this sin? No! Does he have good spirit? No! He is on the way to listening to the voice of the bad shepherd.21 In the light of these words, dictated by love for our sanctification, we continue to unravel the meaning of the Gospel parable. And could other shepherds not go to look for my sheep and lead them to good pastures? No. Our Lord says so positively: qui non intrat per ostium in ovile ovium, sed ascendit aliunde, ille fur est et latro (Jn 10:1)

– he who enters not by the door into the sheepfold, but climbs up another way, is a thief and a robber. Could not someone of good will perhaps go to help out, to take a little flock of sheep and lead them to good pasture and return them to the fold? No. No! And I am not the one who says so; it is our Lord himself. Those who do not have a mission given by the Directors are not the good shepherds even though they may work miracles.22 We must know how to recognise the voice of the good shepherd. We must recognise those loving, familiar whistles of the one who will be able to lead us to the fold and care for us and bring us back to the life that was perhaps lost, or improve that life, and heal us of our infirmities and weaknesses. You will go to your brothers the priests as I go. You will open wide your heart – rotten, if it were rotten! – with sincerity, with a deep desire to cure yourself. If not, that rottenness would never be cured. If we were to go to a person who could only cure our wound superficially, it would be because we were cowards, because we were not good sheep, because we would go to hide the truth to our own

21 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 11 22 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 12 166 Living in Christ

detriment. And doing this wrong, seeking a chance doctor who cannot give us more than a few seconds of his time, who cannot use the scalpel and cauterize the wound, we would also harm the Work. If you were to do this, you would have the wrong spirit; you would be unhappy. You would not sin because of this, but woe to you! You would have begun to err, to make a mistake. You would have begun to hear the voice of the bad shepherd in not wanting to cure yourself, in not wanting to use the means. And you would be doing harm to the others...23 We know well that our interior life influences the others and the whole Work. You know the doctrine of the Mystical Body, of the Communion of Saints. Well, you would be harming your brothers and those who are to come, and you yourself, and the entire body of the Work. And besides, since that bad shepherd did not come to seek you, you will be the one responsible. Because that other person who is not the Good Shepherd, non venit nisi ut furetur et mactet et perdat (Jn 10:10) – came only to steal, and slay, and destroy. We need a defined and specific spirit. Our spirit is very clear; our asceticism, our mysticism, are very clear indeed. And everything that deforms this spirit is to steal and kill.24 Steal and kill, this is what the bad shepherd does, the one who does not enter through the door, the one who does not have any, or very little, interest, in the sheep. The lesson is clear, crystal clear: omnia mihi licent, sed non omnia expediunt,25 all things are lawful for me, but not all things are helpful. And in this same doctrine, we contemplate the zeal of the good shepherd who fears for our health, who wants to give us abundant life. We are no longer in the field of the law or of strict obligation, but in another order of things, above and beyond the spirit of right and

23 From our Father, Meditation, 12 March 1961 24 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 12 25 1 Cor 6:12

Confession 167

obligation, transcending it. It is the order of the Good Shepherd. Ego sum pastor bonus. Bonus pastor animam suam dat pro ovibus suis (Jn 10:11); I am the Good Shepherd. The Good Shepherd lays down his life for his sheep. He makes all the sacrifices. And you ought to be ready to make them all too. And the first is this: not to exercise that right – because we have the right – if we can avoid it, and we can always, or almost always, avoid it. A firm resolution: the first sacrifice is not to forget, in our whole life, what is expressed in Castille in a very graphic way: Dirty linen is washed at home. The first manifestation of your dedication is not being so cowardly as to go outside the Work to wash the dirty clothes. That is if you want to be saints. If not, we can do without you.26 Cowardice, a sort of baseness that creeps into the soul that is not humble, is what can lead a person not to understand this doctrine well or perhaps to not put it into practice. So we have to prepare ourselves with humility, with the humility of knowing that we have feet of clay and of knowing too that no one will be scandalised if some day they were to find out. My children, do not be ashamed to be wretched, if sometimes you are. Do not get scared because you have the fomes peccati in your heart. Do not be afraid of anything. Be truly faithful! Sincere! Sincere! Let us have common sense and the supernatural spirit of knowing that if the Father, because he is father and mother, leaves things very much open, you, because you are steady, sure sheep, with good sense and, in order to let the Good Shepherd work, will know well enough not to use certain rights. In exchange you will experience greater effectiveness in the work of your sanctification and in the sanctification of the whole of Opus Dei, in the sanctification of your brothers and of so many souls and of the Church.27

26 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 13 27 From our Father, Meditation, 12 March 1961 168 Living in Christ

The sacramental nature of Penance The sacrament of Penance confers grace – or increases it, when the one who receives it is already in the state of grace – ex opere operato,28 with efficiency that of itself is infallible. In fact, the measure of this sanctifying effect is in proportion to the dispositions of the person who receives the sacrament. And it can happen that the lack of these dispositions, and the omission of some necessary acts, can be such as to invalidate the sacrament completely, make it ineffective and useless, with the corresponding irreverence to God. Since the tendency to sin is something continuous in us, the tendency to penance should also be continuous. For this makes us always ready to repent, to ask for pardon, to make reparation and to rectify. This habitual disposition comes from the three theological virtues: faith, hope and charity. Spirit of faith: firm adhesion to the word of God, firm belief in his power and in his mercy. And as a consequence: hope, the maximum filial confidence in our Father God. Divine filiation, then. With this marvellous belief we don’t lose our serenity, we feel secure; and we go back, if we have lost the way in some skirmish of this daily struggle – even if it were a big defeat. Because of our weakness we can go astray, and in fact sometimes we do go astray. Let us feel as children of God, to return to him with gratitude, certain of being received by our Father in heaven.29 Love: love for such a good Father who has such a heart of mercy; love for Christ the Good Shepherd who searches for us and gives his life for us. A love that is the basis for confidence: to love and to know you are loved. We should have a positive view of confession. Induimini Dominum Iesum Christum – Put on the Lord Jesus Christ, says St Paul to the Romans. It is in the Sacrament of Penance that you and

28 Cf Council of Trent, Decree, De sacramentis, can 8, Dz 851 (1608) 29 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 60

Confession 169

I put on Jesus Christ and his merits.30 To go to the sacrament of penance is to go to the Cross. It is to put oneself under the protection of Christ crucified. It is to soak oneself with his merits, with his love. It is to renew oneself, to rise again. Just as Christ (and with him, the priest) did not just wait for us, but came out to meet us, so we have to go promptly (punctually, which is delicacy of love), wanting it, feeling the need for grace: Beati qui esuriunt et sitiunt iustitiam, quoniam ipsi saturabuntur;31 Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for justice, for they shall be satisfied. We have learned the five conditions of confession since childhood: examination of conscience, sorrow for sins, firm resolution of amendment, confession of the sins and fulfilling the penance. We have to refine and improve them, see them in the light of our present circumstances. And precisely because of the frequency of our confessions, we must avoid routine – the grave of true piety32 – deepen our love and our sorrow, our self-knowledge, our humility, our spirit of penance. Go deeper as if each confession were the only one, so that this great power which God has given the sacrament is not constrained by the narrow limits of haste, superficiality, carelessness, routine.

Examination of conscience Examination of conscience is not an attitude of introspection. It is to know that we are in the presence of God, present to God. That eye we find framed in a triangle in some ancient representations of the Trinity

– and in illustrations of catechisms of Christian doctrine – is an invitation to a true examination of conscience, to a humble examination, particularly so because this look is not an accusing one but a

30 The Way, 310 31 Mt 5:6 32 The Way, 551 170 Living in Christ

merciful one. The examination of conscience demands putting oneself in the presence of God, with sincerity, with humility, with serenity, with objectivity. The time needed will vary for each soul and with the different circumstances. But having made a good examination each day, a few moments are enough for an all-embracing glance to sum up what was done and what was omitted since our last confession. We should not get lost in insignificant details. Dealing with our defects or venial sins, the important thing is not so much the exact number or a certain moral pigeonholing, as the causes. The actions pass, the habits, the inclinations and dispositions remain. This is what counts: to know our dispositions, to know where our heart is, what it tends toward. It would be something else if a person had had the misfortune to commit a mortal sin, because mortal sins must necessarily be confessed in their species and number with the greatest possible accuracy.33 But if this is not the case, and through the grace of God this is the ordinary thing for a person who has interior life, it is important to get to know the roots of those possible venial faults or even imperfections; for the disposition persists and this is the truly culpable aspect, which is worthy of sorrow and amendment. Having seen the root, we see also the need to avoid the occasion. Deliberate venial faults – to prefer some satisfaction to the will of God in a matter which is not serious but in a conscious way – attachment to our own will and judgment, to comfort, inclination to faults against charity. Ties that stop us flying and hinder us receiving from God all the help we need to grow in holiness. They are things that separate us from our Lord and make our interior life precarious and the apostolate ineffective. Semi-deliberate faults committed when we are surprised, through carelessness, through pure fragility, but we take no pleasure in; for which we are immediately sorry and we try earnestly

33 Cf Council of Trent, Decree, De poenitentia, can. 7, Dz 917 (1707)

Confession 171

not to fall into again. Imperfections: lack of generosity in the acts of love and of service to God and souls. We shall never manage to avoid our miseries completely – at least some of them – but we should struggle, we should know them, and understand their roots. “Man, as long as he is in this life, cannot be without sins even though these be slight. But do not scorn these slight sins we are talking about. Many little things make a big thing. Many drops fill a river. Many grains make a big pile. And what hope is there? Above all, confession.”34 A superficial, distracted glance – in the daily examination or in the preparation to receive the sacrament – cannot but lead us to an equally superficial confession and therefore to a superficial cure that does not tackle the cause of the illness.

Love-sorrow and resolutions Sorrow for sins, contrition, has to be deep and sincere, consequence of a deep and humble examination, and with a clear understanding of what sin is: an offence against God and not only an error of evaluation or a lack of effectiveness. An absolute lack of sorrow could invalidate the sacramental absolution, because the proximate matter of the sacrament on which the form of the priestly absolution acts, is not the sins but the acts with which they are rejected: contrition, confession and satisfaction. That is why spiritual authors never tire of insisting with those who go to confession frequently, or who through the grace of God do not usually commit grave faults, to go deeper and move themselves to repentance; because routine could lead to a lack of sorrow that would invalidate the sacrament. This sorrow is not a sensible thing – although at times its sign may be sensible – but an act of the will whereby one abhors sin and loves God with a spirit of reparation, with an eagerness to make atonement.

34 St Augustine, In epistola Ioann. ad Parthos tract. 1, 6 172 Living in Christ

In any case, sorrow is a grace of God and one must ask for it. And we should try to be motivated by supernatural considerations: the offence against God, the resistance to his most loveable will, the ingratitude toward our Father God and toward the love and redeeming suffering of Jesus Christ. And the loss of intimacy in our friendship with God that the fault has produced, that paralyses or obstructs our interior life, that increases our inclination to evil, that predisposes us to more serious faults. Filial fear is the gateway to love. A specific and firm purpose of amendment. Its absence could also invalidate the absolution. If the contrition is to be more than a slight wish, it must include the resolution of not falling again. This resolution will be the more firm the deeper the sorrow; we can certainly say that there is no sorrow where there is no purpose of amendment. Remember then from what you have fallen, repent and do the works you did at first.35 Let us remember Peter after his fall. Jesus does not scold him. He only asks for an act of love, for the decision to be faithful in the future. ‘Simon, son of John, do you love me?’36

The confession of sins and satisfaction At the moment of confessing our sins to the priest, it is clear that if there has been some grave fault, this would be the first thing we should say; and then, all the rest, with that depth that the examination of conscience has given us. Confession of sins: not just simply relating them, but a true accusation. “God forgives you in so far as you do not forgive yourself.”37 A humble accusation, without excuses. We do not go to justify ourselves, but to be justified by Jesus Christ. Understanding is for the priest; for the

35 Rev 2:5 36 Jn 21:15 37 Tertullian, De poenit. 9

Confession 173

penitent, humility. A humble confession is a personal, sincere, sorrowful confession. It avoids both insubstantial and prolific details and the anodyne and anonymous generalisation. It must be made by telling simply what has happened, relating the true state of the soul, the suggestions of the enemy, the real and personal. The spiritual authors of old used to enumerate sixteen qualities of this confession of sins: simple, humble, pure, faithful, frequent, clear, discreet, voluntary, without boasting, integral, secret, sorrowful, prompt, strong, accusing, and ready to obey.38 Our Father has summarised these conditions for a good confession in four: We must try to be concise, concrete, clear and complete. And this is good especially for confession. Preach about these four marks to everyone. Let them be concise, concrete, clear and complete. In confession, from the beginning go straight to the point. Prepare your confessions so that they are concise, concrete, clear and complete. Even though they may be things that are matter for spiritual direction and even the little things of daily life that cannot even be said to belong to spiritual direction.39 Concise confession. A few words, the precise ones, the ones necessary to say with humility what we have done and what we have omitted. An accusation without circumlocutions, without literature, without justifications, without adorning the sins. Humble conciseness, that conciseness of one who has done the examination of conscience well, who has his conscience clear. The conciseness of the soul that feels sorrow for its faults and senses its own unworthiness when relating them. Concrete confession. Without rambling, without generalities: I was not humble, I lacked charity. Why? When? How? The sum of circumstances that make the fault more personal, more culpable. Circumstances that give it all its relief so that the confessor can judge and absolve and cure.

38 Cf St Thomas, Suppl., q.9, a.4 39 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 19 174 Living in Christ

Causes, motives, important circumstances. In a brief but expressive way, as one tells a doctor the symptoms of an illness. Generalities are an evasion of responsibility for one’s own faults, a tacit excuse, a proud defence of one’s own intimacy. Clear confession. So that we are understood, not letting ourselves be overcome by the shame of declaring the precise nature of the fault, comforting ourselves that we will somehow be understood; though the necessary modesty must be observed, above all in certain matters. Clarity, humble clarity, which is already a satisfaction, a penance, a manifestation of sorrow. Clarity without pride. Clarity of one who is decided to make reparation. “If you do not declare the magnitude of your debt, you will not know the greatness of the pardon.”40 Clarity that shows our weakness clearly without any shadows, because he who conceals his transgressions will not prosper, but he who confesses and forsakes them will obtain mercy.41 Complete, integral, total confession. We know well that the integrity necessary for the validity of confession means all mortal sins. But here we consider the integrity of confession as an instrument of sanctification, as meeting with Christ the Good Shepherd, Doctor, Teacher, Friend. Don’t make any concession to yourself without mentioning it. You must say everything. Look, if not, things become complicated and what began as nothing ends up immense. Remember the story of the gipsy who went to confession and said, ‘Father, I accuse myself of stealing a halter…’ And behind it there was a mule, and then another halter and another mule, and so on, up to twenty. My children, the same can happen in many other things. As soon as you yield to the halter, everything else follows, a whole string of things, things that make us ashamed.42

40 St John Chrysostom, De Lazaro hom. 4, 4 41 Prov 28:13 42 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 39

Confession 175

It can happen, however, that in spite of saying everything and of having made a profound and careful examination, all we confess does not amount to even a venial sin. That is why it is advisable to accuse oneself always of some grave sin from one’s past life – a generic accusation is not enough to constitute sufficient matter for the absolution – or of the mortal or venial sins committed against some specific virtue or commandment. It may be useful to accuse ourselves of sins against the virtue for which we are at present struggling, because the graces that the sacrament confers helps us struggle where we confess to be weakest. But we must carefully avoid routine entering here, converting this accusation into a formality. Sorrow is also necessary for these faults that are already forgiven, because the proximate matter, as we have already said, is not the sins but the acts with which these are rejected: sorrow, the purpose of amendment, satisfaction. We have to move ourselves to sorrow for those things we confess. Love for confession. Gratitude to our Lord. Charity with those who are waiting. Charity with the priest – your brother who lives especially that: pro eis ego sanctifico meipsum (Jn 17:19), for them I sanctify myself – who habitually kills himself. Such ought to be and such is the quality and the quantity of his continuous formation, and of his habitual work. And all this is why you should make good use of confession. And to make good use of it, do not waste time or make others waste it. The preparation can be long for some people. For others, a quick look and a sincere act of contrition will be enough. Those who need it can make a longer examination, but afterwards the confession can be brief: concise, concrete, clear, complete. Do not go around in circles, entangling things. Besides this would lead to friendships that do not go with our spirit. For us, one priest should be the same as another, provided that he is a priest of the Work.43

43 From our Father, Crónica, June 1962, p. 21 176 Living in Christ

Afterwards Jesus Christ will associate us to his infinite Sacrifice of expiation when the priest imposes a penance, which is not simply a work of piety, but atonement, reparation, satisfaction for the debt contracted in offending God. It is a penalty that justifies us as well as being a medicinal and preventive one. We have to give the full meaning to the penance we fulfil, mitigated so maternally by the Church, and also because the priest imposes upon himself part of the penance so as to contribute to the reparation. We must fulfil this penance with a spirit of atonement, with humility and with sorrow. “Since the penance imposed by the priest is a part of the vis clavium, the power of pardoning, it is of more value in expiating the sin, than if the man were to do the same penance through his own choice.”44

The efficacy of penance Jesus Christ was put to death for our trespasses and raised for our justification.45 Confession makes us participate in the Passion of Christ and through his merits, in his Resurrection. In each confession a resurrection takes place, a rebirth to the life of grace. Not only are our sins forgiven, but we are reborn with a resurrection that is the more deep and complete the more profound and sorrowful our confession has been. Through this sacrament the Blood of Christ lovingly shed, purifies and sanctifies the soul. Through its own virtue it confers grace – if it had been lost – or it increases it, but in different degrees according to personal dispositions. “The intensity of the repentance of the penitent is, at times, proportioned to a greater grace than that from which he fell through sin. At times, it is the same; at times, it is less. And therefore sometimes the penitent gets up with much more grace than he had before; at times with the same; at times with less. And the same has to

44 St Thomas, Quodlibet 3, a.28 45 Rom 4:25

Confession 177

be said of the virtues that depend on and accompany grace.”46 “See how good God is and how easily he forgives sin. He not only restores what was pardoned but he gives unexpected things.”47 We receive greater lights from God and an increase of strength – special graces to combat the inclinations confessed, to avoid the occasions that are feared, for not relapsing into the faults committed. Special graces for our daily struggle, for our path toward sanctity. And as a consequence of all this, we leave the confessional flooded with peace and consolation, sure, serene, happy: a happiness that is not only compatible with sorrow, but which is born of it. “One can be sorrowful for having sinned and rejoice that this hurts, with the hope of pardon. Thus this sadness is the cause of joy.”48 This sacrament is, on God’s part, the harmony between his justice and his mercy; for us, it is the harmony between sorrow and joy. “Semper doleat poenitens, et de dolore gaudeat.”49 The penitent should always be sorrowful and rejoice in this sorrow. The joy that passes through sorrow, that is reborn of sorrow, is glory, in the same way as life born of death is resurrection. If any one is in Christ, he is a new creation; the old has passed away, behold, the new has come. All this is from God, who through Christ reconciled us to himself and gave us the ministry of reconciliation.50 In the light of this doctrine it is easy to understand that the best devotion is a contrite confession, as our Father has said. May the most Holy Virgin, our Queen and Mother, Refugium peccatorum, obtain for us the grace of always receiving very well the sacrament of the mercy of God.

46 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.89, a.2 47 St Ambrose, Super Ev. Luc. tract. 2, 73 48 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.84, a.9 ad 2 49 St Augustine, De vera et falsa poenit. 13 50 2 Cor 5:17-18

## THE FRATERNAL CHAT {#cuaderno.3.17}

With our Father’s help we have seen, interwoven among the events of our Lord’s life in the home of Lazarus, Martha and Mary, the presence of a reality perhaps only hinted at in the Gospel narrative but which can be clearly discerned: Lazarus frequently spoke with our Lord; he spoke about himself. And Peter, John, and Andrew and the other disciples would do the same: Lord…, you have the words of eternal life.1 I am the good shepherd, Jesus said, I know my own and my own know me.2 He gathered round him a small group of rough and simple men and formed them with infinite patience. Little by little – with a divine pedagogy – he revealed the most sublime mysteries to them. At the same time, as the situation required, he used both maternal gentleness and the firmness of one who has authority to refine and purify their coarse insensitivity to perceive heavenly realities.3

The need for the chat We can well appreciate the immense supernatural value of this trusting conversation with our Lord, and at the same time its human warmth. The book of Sirach tells us: Stay constantly with a godly man, whom you know to be a keeper of the commandments, whose soul is in

1 Jn 6:68 2 Jn 10:14 3 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 2

The fraternal chat 179

accord with your soul, and who will sorrow with you if you fail. And establish the counsel of your own heart, for no one is more faithful to you than it is. For a man’s soul sometimes keeps him better informed than seven watchmen sitting high on a watchtower. And besides all this, pray to the Most High that he may direct your way in truth.4 The fraternal chat, the Confidence,5 is so necessary that, even before belonging to the Work, we probably began doing it with no conscious resolution to use a specific means of formation. In this way, too, it came into being within Opus Dei, spontaneously, effortlessly, as water rises in a gentle spring, with the naturalness with which a spring flows. “All our norms and customs have been born in this way. Our Father, being the only priest in the Work at the beginning, did not want to hear the confessions of our first brothers in the normal course of events so as not to tie his hands. I did not have any teacher, he says, speaking of this and of so many other matters, and it was the Holy Spirit who taught me. Our first brothers, voluntarily and with complete freedom, began the custom of telling the Father what was on their minds by baring their conscience outside of Confession. When the Father was not available, and later because of the growth of the apostolate, our first brothers went to the Director with the same candor to do their Confidence which had shown itself to be so effective and so necessary.”6 The Confidence is not a whim. It is a necessity. In times of difficulty we are assured of victory if we have opened our hearts, because God will not let this act of sincerity go unrewarded and will give us his grace.7 We need a Director because your own judgment is a

4 Sir 37:12-15 5 This means of formation, which we have known as the Confidence from the beginning, can also be called the fraternal chat, for it is a conversation between two brothers (or sisters), and not one between a subject and his (or her) superior (Catechism of the Work, 213, 8th edition). 6 Don Alvaro, note 75 to our Father’s Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950 7 From our Father, Crónica, 1968, p. 409 180 Living in Christ

bad adviser, a poor pilot to steer your soul through the storms, squalls and reefs of the interior life.8 Left to ourselves we could easily go astray. In the words of the book of Ecclesiastes: Two are better than one, because they have a good reward for their toil. For if they fall, one will lift up his fellow; but woe to him who is alone when he falls and has not another to lift him up.9 It is bad to be alone and worse to want to be alone on the way toward God. Do you remember the vine and the branches? How fruitful is the branch united to the vine! How abundant the bunches of grapes! Separated from the vine the branch withers, losing its life; and if it already had bunches, they are eaten by worms.10 Our Lord gives us his grace when we seek that spiritual direction to identify our spirit with that of the Work. Now you are the body of Christ and individual members of it.11 The person designated to receive our Confidence has a special grace to counsel us.

Its supernatural character To the extent that supernatural outlook can be lost, spiritual direction can become proportionately hard, even difficult. Without faith that our Lord will make use of this instrument, how is it possible to look forward to this regular chat? Without the firm conviction – the certain hope – that we will find the solution to the problems of our soul, what can move us to speak about our difficulties? If we are not prepared to give ourselves completely for love, how can we gladly let ourselves be moulded and make our own the demands of sanctity that are clearly and decisively shown us in the chat? For to do it well means giving ourselves, voluntarily letting down 8 The Way, 59 9 Eccles 4:9-10 10 From our Father, Crónica, 1968, p. 409 11 1 Cor 12:27

The fraternal chat 181

our defences, surrendering our thoughts and memories, also the aspirations, perhaps illusory, and the real motives behind our actions. It means showing our life as it really is, not a protective coating of appearances. It means putting ourselves in the hands of the one who directs us, surrendering the defences of intimacy. Hence our Father reminded us that we do not go to spiritual direction for the sake of friendship, or for personal reasons, but for supernatural reasons.12 Whether the one who listens to us has been in the Work for a short or for a long time, no matter what the circumstances may be, at that moment he is the most authorised interpreter of the spirit of the Work. It is better for a man to hear the rebuke of the wise than to hear the song of fools.13 Possible human motives which could affect our sincerity, such as like-mindedness, prestige, agreeableness, would be like the flattery of fools. Stemming from purely human motives, they could be a serious obstacle to our transformation in Jesus Christ towards which grace moves us.

The way of doing it This conviction is needed for a good preparation in the presence of God. So we avoid the danger of improvising a few generalities of little or no interest, and avoid dealing only with situations which are purely incidental. We should allow ourselves enough time to prepare it adequately and foresee possible difficulties. Occasionally, they will be real, such as a shortage of time or unexpected circumstances. But they can also be just signs of plain laziness. To avoid this we should examine ourselves, as our Father has taught us, on our fulfilment of the Norms and Customs, especially on the way we do our prayer and examinations of conscience,

12 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 132 13 Sir 7:5 182 Living in Christ

and practise mortification; everything referring to faith, purity and vocation; spirit of filiation, fraternity and proselytism; worries, joys and sorrows; love for the Church and the Work; prayer of petition for the Roman Pontiff and Bishops in communion with the Holy See; prayer and mortification for the Father and all members of the Work.14 We should also talk about the way we carry out our apostolate, especially our special apostolic task, and any other jobs that have been entrusted to us; professional work, social and family relations in so far as they have a bearing on our interior life and are a source of apostolate. These matters must be dealt with supernaturally, revealing our interior dispositions, not vague and abstract sentiments but the real ways in which we react to different situations. This is shown when we talk about the things which we do gladly or reluctantly, what makes us happy or sad, what brings out our egoism or self-surrender, our pride or selfabandonment… We should avoid spending time on those things which have only a marginal bearing on our interior life and our apostolic activity; and always respect with the greatest delicacy the strictly professional area, in which each one is completely free and responsible.

Speaking with sincerity If we try to maintain a supernatural tone throughout this talk with our brother, it will be easy to stick to the point – saying things the way they are, simply and directly, without beating about the bush. This simplicity, a sure sign of good spirit is indispensable for receiving light and grace from our Lord, for a holy and disciplined spirit will free from deceit.15 “Listen again to what Scripture testifies: Yahweh… confides only in honest men (Prov 3:32). When God converses confidently with the human soul, he reveals his secrets to it, clarifying them by his

14 Cf Catechism of the Work, 214, 8th edition 15 Wis 1:5

The fraternal chat 183

presence. It is said that he speaks to simple men, because by the bright light of his visit he uncovers heavenly mysteries to those whose minds are not clouded by any trace of deceit.”16 We ought to chat with the same simplicity with which we speak to God in prayer, so that we do not confuse what we are with what we would like to be; and we should avoid making reference to one’s virtues or achievements in a self-satisfied fashion in order to be praised for them.17 We should also avoid seeking sympathy: very often this is a sign of pride.18 “Well does the prophet Isaiah say of the souls of those who do evil and excuse themselves: It will be the haunt of … the hedgehog! (Is 34:11) The hedgehog portrays exactly the double-dealing of the deceptive soul that cunningly defends itself. When the hedgehog is surprised his head and claws are visible, but when he is challenged he tucks in his head, claws and tail, rolling up into a ball, in such a way as to disappear. We say the hedgehog’s head is visible, because even before one approaches a sinner his sins are visible; his claws are visible because sin leaves its traces in the sinner. However, when his faults are pointed out to him, the sinner hides them…, and rolls up, as it were, into a tight ball in the hands of those who try to correct him.”19 Total sincerity starts from and is built on confidence in the Directors. There are two terrible signs of bad spirit: to fear those who govern in the Work and to be ashamed to speak out in the Confidence.20 That bad attitude undermines sincerity and any possible defence is lost for those souls who allow it to occur.21 Our Father has told us we should

16 St Gregory the Great, Reg. Past. 3, 11 17 Catechism of the Work, 216, 8th edition 18 Ibid 19 St Gregory the Great, Reg. Past. 3, 11 20 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 130 21 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 92 184 Living in Christ

not be ashamed to speak out in the chat, but if there is anything that is harder to say, we should talk about it at the very beginning. Especially when something happens that you would not want anyone to know about, make it known immediately – running – to the person who can help you, the Good Shepherd. This is a logical decision. Suppose a person is walking with a large rock on his shoulder and his pockets filled with pebbles that weigh, all together, about a hundred grams. If we locate this person in Madrid, let us suppose the distance he has to cover is from the Puerta del Sol to Cuatro Caminos.22 When he arrives at his destination, he is not going to empty his pockets of the pebbles – one by one – while he stands there with the rock on his shoulder. My children, the same thing applies to us. We should get rid of the burdensome matters first. Any other way of acting would be extremely foolish and the beginning of insincerity.23 That fraternal confidence, with which we tell our brother how things are going, is ultimately and most properly based on confidence in God. The sooner we understand, in its deepest sense, that the chat is not a matter of getting on well with a particular person, of a similarity of character that humanly speaking facilitates feeling understood, the less of an obstacle will be the fear of not being understood or any other barrier which, in the last analysis, is nothing more than distrust of our Lord. Confidence in our Director should be the practice and sign of our love for God.

Listening with docility But it is not enough to make ourselves known, even though we may do it deeply and with sincerity. We should also know how to listen with docility, receiving the advice as if it came from Jesus Christ our Lord

22 Translator’s note: a distance of about four kilometres. 23 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 40

The fraternal chat 185

himself. This way it will be easy for us to accept advice or correction not just to learn, which would indicate a lack of good spirit, but rather, to improve our life, so that our efforts and our actions may be effective. It is not possible to know everything, for, as the Holy Spirit says, to none has he given the power to proclaim his works, and who can search out his mighty deeds?24 Our Father has also told us that no one in the Work can consider himself to be sufficiently formed, and it is even more unthinkable for us not to be able to improve. All of us in the Work always need spiritual direction in order to improve. The talk should usually be brief, not only to avoid wasting our own time but also that of others; excessive talk is usually a sign of a lack of simplicity, and often a sign of vanity. Qui ambulat simpliciter ambulat confidenter – he who walks in simplicity walks confidently.25 We should come directly to the point, simply and humbly. Afterwards one should give thanks to God, engrave on one’s heart the advice received and try to put it into practice.26 It is necessary, then, to practise the virtue of hope in a special way so as not to get discouraged by the difficulties we see or, more or less consciously, to forget to fulfil the indications given to us. We should concentrate our efforts on the specific details indicated, certain that is the will of God and, as such, our guarantee of being fruitful and effective. The need to keep in mind the advice we have received should lead us to be careful to be punctual with the Confidence, to maintain the necessary continuity between one Confidence and the next. We should go over the matters previously dealt with so that we can talk over the difficulties encountered and obtain help anew. Habitual delay in this means of formation would be bad not only because it is in itself a

24 Sir 18:4 25 Prov 10:9 26 Catechism of the Work, 215, 8th edition 186 Living in Christ

disorder, but because it would indicate that we were not doing it well: that we were not fully docile to advice or not really determined to follow it. It would indicate an absence of true desire to struggle or of supernatural outlook. When we use this means of spiritual direction properly, eager for self-surrender and confident in God, we experience inner refreshment, joy and peace; we become stronger and more optimistic in our ascetical struggle and in our apostolate. We find the gaudium cum pace and more: each Confidence is a step closer to God. We become humbler, our love of God increases and, at the same time, we are more closely united to the Work and to our brothers. I have opened my mouth, and have spoken: buy for yourselves without silver, our Lord tells us, ...and let your soul receive discipline.27 Our Lord speaks to us. He is beside us, accompanying us. In each chat, just as happened to the disciples of Emmaus, faith and supernatural outlook make us discover the joy and encouragement of meeting Christ. Did not our hearts burn within us while he talked to us on the road, while he opened to us the Scriptures?28

27 Sir 51:34 28 Lk 24:32

## SIMPLICITY {#cuaderno.3.18}

Jesus was going to Galilee. His first followers had already begun their proselytism. Philip must have thought of Nathanael, and, as soon as he could, he told him: Come and see. Jesus saw Nathanael coming to him, and said of him: ‘Behold a true Israelite, in whom there is no guile!’1 It is always a pleasure to come across someone who is straightforward, uncomplicated and simple. That person soon wins our confidence. Simplicity is a great virtue. Perhaps even without much reflection on the praises that our Lord gives it in the Gospel, we have always understood it to be a deeply Christian virtue. Later we saw it shine like a diamond in the ensemble of virtues that the spirit of Opus Dei requires of us. The formation that the Work gives us tends to simplify our interior life, so that we are not interiorly complicated, twisted, entangled. Our Father often told us: Our asceticism has the simplicity of the Gospel. We would complicate it if we were to be complicated, if we were to leave our hearts in darkness, if our sincerity were not absolute.2 Opus Dei gives us the practical means to live that counsel of the book of Wisdom: Think of the Lord in goodness, and seek him in simplicity of heart.3 Wisdom, sincerity and simplicity always walk hand

1 Jn 1:46-47 2 From our Father, Crónica, December 1959, p. 6 3 Wis 1:1 188 Living in Christ

in hand along the paths that lead to God. Simplicity is an important element of the supernatural edifice of sanctity. You write: ‘Simplicity is the salt of perfection. And that’s what I lack. I want to acquire it, with his help and with yours.’ Neither his nor mine will fail you. Use the means.4

The virtue of simplicity To use the means we need first to know what simplicity is and the root from which it springs. “What craftiness is to prudence,” says St Thomas, “fraud and deceit are to simplicity. Deceit or fraud are primarily directed to deceiving, and sometimes, secondarily to harming. For which reason it pertains directly to simplicity to avoid deceit. And according to this, as was already said above,”5 “the virtue of simplicity is the same as that of veracity, but differs according to a consideration of the mind: for veracity is said according as signs agree with the thing signed; and simplicity is said according as it does not tend towards different things, in such a way that one thing is desired within and another shown without.”6 It might appear, however, that if we wish to practise simplicity we are pretty well obliged to get out of this world; since some people think that it’s inevitable for those who live in the world to rely on deception, to use cunning. But this is not true. The good, straightforward, upright man does not need to deceive anyone by cunning, fraud or deceit because he is not going to harm anyone. On the contrary, he seeks their good, and he can do this with perfect simplicity. Furthermore, men can be ignorant and prone to sin, and sometimes the very people who receive benefits from the upright man become his enemies. Not even

4 The Way, 305 5 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.109, a.2 ad 4 6 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.111, a.3 ad 2

Simplicity 189

then should this man lose his simplicity. Prudence will suffice to avoid and counteract the mistaken attacks that threaten him. On the other hand, someone who does not have an upright intention, who does not always and above all seek the glory of God and the good of souls, but seeks only his own good – in fact in so thinking he is mistaken – for this very reason he finds in every one else obstacles to his interests, presumed enemies, limitations to his individual possibilities. And as no one wants to serve the egoism of another, if the egoist persists in his intentions he finds himself obliged to deceive. His prudence, the choice of means, begin to be twisted and become cunning, because the aim of his actions is twisted. He will have to put on appearances, to lie, in order to advance among men whom he wishes to employ as simple means for his individual welfare. Only one who is really simple can have true prudence, an upright prudence. Qui ambulat simpliciter ambulat confidenter.7 He who walks in simplicity walks confidently, far from the unrest of cunning, from the anguish of seeing enmity on all sides. Simplicity excludes cunning, the sly defence of our selfish attitudes; but not virtuous prudence, which prevents simplicity from being too naive, simple, and ineffective in its love and work for good. According to the words of our Lord: Estote ergo prudentes sicut serpentes et simplices sicut columbae.8 You must be as prudent as serpents and as simple as doves.

The danger of being complicated It also helps in understanding the nature of simplicity to consider the vices that are most directly opposed to it: affectation and officiousness, which are also the fruit of a more or less conscious double play; pedantry, by which one speaks and listens to oneself at the same time; scruples,

7 Prov 10:9 8 Mt 10:16 190 Living in Christ

where one wishes to be the judge and the thing judged at the same time, suspecting the judgment of others; and following an increasing gravity of scale, there are irony, boasting, hypocrisy and lying. The very natural repulsion these vices inspire in us, shows the value of simplicity; not only is duplicity, the gravest case, opposed to simplicity, but so also is any complication, any kind of hidden action from behind the lines, even the most superficial, because they all do great harm to the interior life. Abominabile Domino cor pravum, et voluntas eius in iis qui simpliciter ambulant,9 those having a perverse heart are the abomination of Yahweh, but those who are straightforward in their conduct are pleasing to him. Simplicity, that is to say integrity, unity, being uncomplicated, is a necessary consequence of goodness of heart, because “what is proper to a star is the light that surrounds it; and what is proper to a pious man who fears God is simplicity and humility.”10 All complication is bad: That pose and that self-satisfied manner don’t suit you at all: they are easily seen to be affected. Try, at least, to use them neither with God, nor with your Director, nor with your brothers; and between them and you there will be one barrier fewer.11 All complication is a defence system, a protective wall that our egoism has taught us to establish around ourselves, a barrier made up of appearances to safeguard our interests. And with this we have entered into the root of simplicity.

Spiritual childhood The root of simplicity is to be unarmed, defenceless, to break down the barriers that separate us from the loving demands of God; from the

9 Prov 11:20 10 Hesiquius, De temperantia et virtute 1, 82 11 The Way, 47

Simplicity 191

unconditional obedience that we owe our Directors; from the unlimited fraternity that we must practise with our brothers; from a courageous clarity in our self-knowledge that seeks no excuses. To become simple we must stop defending ourselves, stop thinking about our own rights, forget ourselves, and leave our future in the hands of God and of our Directors who represent him. We must lose our life, be born again,12 leave behind our own judgment. We must lose our freedom so as to recover it in identification with Jesus Christ, and become as little children. We remember that scene of the Gospel, where our Lord invites us to live the simplicity of children. St Matthew writes: At that time the disciples came to Jesus, saying, ‘Who is the greatest in the kingdom of heaven?’ And calling to him a child, he put him in the midst of them, and said, ‘Truly, I say to you, unless you turn and become like children, you will never enter the kingdom of heaven.’13 And St Jerome comments on the passage with this paraphrase: “Just as this little child, whose example I propose to you… does not think one thing and say another, so you also, if you were not to have such innocence and purity of intention you would not be able to enter into the Kingdom of Heaven.”14 To become as little children: it is a whole programme of the supernatural life that Jesus recommended to Nicodemus, and that our Father asked of us insistently. Be devout as children and learned as theologians – with sincerity, through simplicity – and you will see how you come along fine. There are two very clear manifestations of a life of childhood: simplicity and naturalness. They are the fruit of disarming ourselves, of working in the presence of God, with no other interest or aim. And before God, we are what we are. There is no room

12 Cf Jn 3:5 13 Mt 18:1-3 14 St Jerome, In Ev. Matthaeum comm. 3, 17, 3 192 Living in Christ

for histrionics or putting on appearances. Simplicity cannot be simulated; it has to come from within. It is not a disguise or a cover, quite the reverse. It means doing away with every difference between what is within and what is without. And whoever would want to show simplicity without possessing it, would for this very reason end up complicated.
