Rectitude of intention The intention is what simplifies, that intention which in the Gospel is compared to a look: Si oculus tuus fuerit simplex, totum corpus tuum lucidum erit,15 if your eye is simple, all of your body will be illuminated. If our intentions are upright and simple, focused, all our life will be one, true and luminous instead of being complex like those who try to serve two masters: God and themselves. Simplicity is only possible when there is loyalty. But just as simplicity is opposed not only to duplicity but to any kind of complication, it is not just a lack of rectitude of intention but also feebleness of intention that destroys simplicity. Simplicity is an upright intention in the love of God, which must govern all our sentiments, impressions and emotions, and the confused and complex life of the senses. Our intention should be upright, but it should also be firm. It must impose itself on the intentions or natural desires of the sensory life. It has to dominate whatever is turbulent and complicated in us, by force of charity. And it must enlighten the darkness of the senses with the light of the faith. The simple soul does not have complications. It does not judge anything according to personal impressions of the moment. Opposing desires would make it waver. It judges everything enlightened by divine light and desiring all for God. To be simple all affectation and falsehood have to be

15 Mt 6:22

Simplicity 193

renounced, and perhaps in a special way that subtle falsehood, that selfish falsification of love that is called sentimentality.

The value of sincerity But what about the means to achieve simplicity? There is one basic means: sincerity. Nothing, not even our own weaknesses, can hold out against it. Look: the Apostles, for all their evident and undeniable defects, were sincere, simple… transparent. You, too, have evident and undeniable defects. May you not lack simplicity.16 Simplicity, sincerity with the Directors, naturalness with our brothers, clarity in the examination, and – as the root of everything – confidence in God. Sincerity without ambiguity or circumlocution; brutal sincerity – rough when necessary – in spiritual direction and in our dealings with the Directors. We must practise a simple, clear sincerity and be brief. Let what you say be simply ‘Yes’, or ‘No’; anything more than this comes from evil.17 A sincerity that does not seek the way of saying things, which is an affectation, but simply says the things that have to be said, nothing more, just what there is to say. It is an absolutely necessary condition to achieve simplicity. Our founder reminded us: Silly child, the day you hide some part of your soul from your Director, you will cease to be a child, for you will have lost your simplicity.18 We must never fear being known just as we are, or that the things we do be known just as we do them, as we think them and want them to be. Renounce covering up, minimising, misrepresenting. We must abandon ourselves with the fraternal abandon-

16 The Way, 932 17 Mt 5:37 18 The Way, 862 194 Living in Christ

ment asked of us in the Work. Do not hide anything from your Directors. Be sincere. Let yourself be led by the hand through obedience.19 Amiable and spontaneous naturalness in the practice of fraternity is the naturalness proper to brothers who love one another without confusing courtesy with officiousness, or dignity with affectation. Always keep up our spirit of simplicity, which is forgetfulness of self and a constant concern for the others. Authenticity with oneself is necessary, interior transparency: an examination well done, being on guard against the devil that ties your tongue,20 without letting ourselves be influenced by confused states of mind; with an objectivity that rises above the complications of feelings and uncontrolled emotions. Supreme confidence, familiarity, divine filiation in our relations with God our Father who is in heaven; a filial love that encourages us to abandonment and removes interior monologue (which is a source of duplicity), substituting the loving dialogue of a son with his Father; simplicity in praying, not looking for well-rounded phrases and elegant words; a life of continual piety; aspirations in abundance, said with a simple spirit; solid devotion to the Blessed Virgin; earnest mortification of the imagination and of the memory. The fruits of simplicity are countless. Simplicity follows upon rectitude of intention and engenders purity of heart, as St Thomas affirms: “Simplicity is said in opposition to duplicity by which a person has one thing in his heart and exteriorises something different… Simplicity rectifies the intention, not directly, because this is common to all virtue: but excluding duplicity, by which man intends one thing and gives the appearance of another.”21

19 From our Father, Crónica, December 1959, p. 12 20 The Way, 236 21 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.109, a.2 ad 4

Simplicity 195

Simplicity brings peace and joy to the heart, for our boast is this, the testimony of our conscience that we have behaved in the world, and still more toward you, with holiness and godly sincerity, not by earthly wisdom but by the grace of God.22

22 2 Cor 1:12

## CHRISTIAN FORTITUDE {#cuaderno.3.19}

From the days of John the Baptist until now, our Lord said, the Kingdom of heaven has suffered violence, and the men of violence take it by force.1 Struggle, effort – with grace, in grace and because of grace

– should be the normal thing in a Christian’s life. And being normal, we also need a habitual disposition to undertake and maintain this effort. We need a habit, a special virtue, fortitude, which keeps us firm in the struggle – firm in the Faith, faithful – in spite of everything. “Invincible in work, strong in dangers, rigorous against sensuality.”2 We need fortitude: to keep us from failing, whatever the difficulties; to keep us serene, neither rash nor timid; to reject energetically the temptation to stray even a little from the true path; never to pause, no matter how steep and long the climb may become.

The role of fortitude Be strong, and let your heart take courage.3 Sanctity is no easy task. We have to fulfil the will of God always, every day, every minute even though it may be very difficult. This is true at times of interior struggle, and when doing apostolate. A son of God must be strong to undertake the work of his sanctity, to start again at each of its stages, and to tackle

1 Mt 11:12 2 St Ambrose, De Officiis 29 3 Ps 30:24

Christian fortitude 197

apostolic tasks. But he has to be even stronger to continue them, to persevere when internal and external obstacles appear. We also need this cardinal virtue to keep on the road, to remain detached from the goods of the earth, and be able to say with St Paul: But whatever gain I had, I counted as loss for the sake of Christ. Indeed, I count everything as loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord. For his sake I have suffered the loss of all things, and count them as refuse.4 This contempt is not easy. It demands struggle, conquest of self, fortitude. A certain fortitude is needed for this general attitude regarding the goods of the earth. But even more fortitude is required to reject them when they make pull at us, when they tempt our appetites, when they try to take advantage of our weakness. The world, the flesh and the devil are a band of adventurers who take advantage of the weakness of that savage you bear within you and want you to hand over to them, in exchange for the glittering tinsel of a pleasure – which is worth nothing – the pure gold and the pearls and the diamonds and rubies, drenched in the life-blood of your God-Redeemer, which are the price and the treasure of your eternity.5 The role of fortitude is precisely to strengthen the weakness of the flesh, to make it firm, obedient to reason and to faith, giving the flesh the firmness of the spirit. We must not let ourselves be surprised, we must be on the watch, because the enemy does not rest. Be sober, be watchful. Your adversary the devil prowls around like a roaring lion, seeking someone to devour. Resist him, firm in your faith.6

4 Phil 3:7-8 5 The Way, 708 6 1 Pet 5:8 198 Living in Christ

Perseverance in the struggle This struggle is not a matter of a moment. We must persevere in it, day after day. The virtue of fortitude also makes our interior struggle constant, firm, patient and daring. It enables us to live without fear. It helps us to make sanctity the only ideal which will last until the end, until, as victors, we can say with St Paul: Bonum certamen certavi, cursum consummavi, fidem servavi.7 I have fought the good fight, I have finished the course, I have kept the Faith. There may be moments along the way to sanctity when we feel the hardness of the struggle, when the effort weighs upon us and when work hurts. “Boxers,” St Thomas says, “rejoice in the thought of the end they are fighting for, that is, because they will be crowned and filled with honours. But it is painful for them to suffer the blows. To deny this would be to deny that they are of flesh. Because they have sensible flesh, what wounds them necessarily produces pain.”8 And to stay in the fight, in addition to love for the goal for which we fight, we need fortitude. We may also suffer times of discouragement. It is then that we should put into practice this piece of advice of our Father: And, when discouragement comes, if the Lord were to permit this temptation; in the face of apparently adverse circumstances; in some cases, considering the ineffectiveness of your apostolic works of formation; if anyone should ask you, as they asked the older Tobias: ubi est spes tua? Where is your hope?… lift up your eyes above the wretchedness of this life, which is not your aim, and tell them, like that Old Testament character, who was strong and full of hope: quoniam memor fuit Domini in toto corde suo (Tob 1:13), because he always remembered God and loved Him with all his heart. Filii sanctorum sumus, et

7 2 Tim 4:7 8 St Thomas, In Ethic. ad Nicom., 587

Christian fortitude 199

vitam illam expectamus, quam Deus daturus est his, qui fidem suam nunquam mutant ab eo: we are children of saints, and we look forward to that life which God will give to those who never abandoned their faith in Him (Tob 2:18). What is certain is that we will find the fruit of our works in our Homeland.9

Supernatural fortitude In the apostolate as in the ascetical struggle, the fortitude that we should practise is not simply a human virtue that functions in the light of natural reason. It must be supernatural, infused fortitude which acts in the light of faith. We must be strong, strong in the faith: fortes in fide; and faith tells us that if we want to struggle, the victory is already ours. And so there cannot be fear of failing. Do not be grieved, for the joy of the Lord is your strength.10 Besides, to help us be strong, we have the example of Christians who knew how to give their life for the Faith. And, very close to us, we have the example of our Father, the Father and our brothers and sisters in the Work who know how to be faithful, who know how to conquer. Resist him, firm in your faith, knowing that the same experience of suffering is required of your brotherhood throughout the world.11 The fortitude that is asked of us is, above all, not that based on our poor human strength, but that which has the omnipotence of God for its support. So, when we notice the insufficiency of our own efforts – efforts which are indispensable for the road that God has traced out for us – we know enough to continue ahead strong in the faith. Omnia possibilia sunt credenti. Everything is possible for anyone who has

9 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935, 19-20 10 Neh 8:10 11 1 Pet 5:9 200 Living in Christ

faith. The words are Christ’s. How is it that you don’t say to him with the Apostles: ‘Adauge nobis fidem! – increase my faith!’?12 There are paradoxes of supernatural life: we are asked for all human effort, all possible co-operation, only to find in the end our true strength in a recognition, humble and full of faith, of our weakness. For when I am weak, then I am strong,13 because the Lord is my rock and my fortress, and my deliverer, my God, my rock, in whom I take refuge, my shield, and the horn of my salvation, my stronghold. I call upon the Lord, who is worthy to be praised, and I am saved from my enemies.14 We must be strong, yes, but strong in faith and in charity, strong in the perfection of the supernatural virtue of fortitude and in the corresponding gift of the Holy Spirit. Through faith we know that all things are possible, and we know to whom we must go to accomplish them. Through charity we undertake them with “a love that finishes everything easily for love of God.”15 Fortitude reaches its highest degree in martyrdom, in death accepted for love, because fortis est ut mors dilectio,16 for love is strong as death. There is a death that is suffered once, for which usually only one resolution is needed. But there is another that lasts a whole lifetime and is made up of little things. Many who would willingly let themselves be nailed to a cross before the astonished gaze of a thousand onlookers cannot bear with a Christian spirit the pinpricks of each day! Think, then, which is the more heroic.17 Fortitude, and that to a very high degree, is also required for this continual death.

12 The Way, 588 13 2 Cor 12:10 14 Ps 16:2-4 15 St Augustine, De moribus Ecclesiae catholicae 1, 15 16 Song 8:6 17 The Way, 204

Christian fortitude 201

Our fortitude must be constant, when faced with the foreseen or the unforeseen, when faced with the momentary or with lasting things, when faced with great things or – so as to be able to carry them out – when faced also with small and monotonous things. Will-power. A very important quality. Don’t despise little things, for by the continual practice of denying yourself again and again in such things – which are never futile or trivial – with God’s grace you will add strength and resilience to your will. In that way you will first become master of yourself, and then a guide, a chief, a leader: to compel and to urge and to inspire others, with your word, with your example, with your knowledge and with your authority.18 Thus, fortitude exercised for a long time, becomes the guardian of all the virtues and the guarantee of the apostolate. Because if the strong man, fully armed, guards his own palace, his goods are in peace.19

The serenity of the children of God Among the fruits of fortitude, one of great importance is serenity, equanimity, stability, permanence. Our founder asked us for fortitude when he said: Children of my soul, be serene in your work and in your spiritual life. For the love of God, be faithful. Don't be childish or crazy. Presence of God, that is a clear characteristic of our vocation.20 A Christian must be serene and optimistic in his interior life and in his apostolate. “Nothing can separate him from the love of God. He does not need to sedate his soul since he is convinced that everything is for good. He does not get upset; there is nothing that makes him angry since he always loves God and only strives for this.”21 This habitual serenity, this ordinary dominion of our passions – in 18 Ibid, 19 19 Lk 11:21 20 From our Father, Meditation, 19 June 1955 21 Clement of Alexandria, Stromata 6, 9, 71, 4 202 Living in Christ

danger, in suffering, in temptation – is an essential condition for a contemplative soul. Given our good will, fortitude is needed to live without doing anything crazy, without the agitation that obscures thinking and that leads a person to do things for which he will be sorry afterwards. The peace that our Lord gives does not consist in the absence of difficulties, but rather in dominion over them. And this stillness, this peace, this composure, facilitates continual presence of God because “God comes with tranquillity.”22 We cannot be like children or like crazy people. We should be strong, children of God. Serene in our work and in our profession. With a continuous presence of God, that makes us put ourselves into perfecting little things. Fortitude is a key element in the effective execution of a job. And it is fortitude, too, which helps us to understand and practise this formula that our Father has given us: There are things that can wait. There are things that are very urgent… and these can wait longer. Be calm, serene, composed.23 He who is strong inspires fortitude in his brothers. He infuses confidence and serenity, in the same way that a man without fortitude shakes and disturbs those around him. What man is there that is fearful and faint-hearted? Let him go back to his house, lest the heart of his fellows melt as his heart.24 Another reason for us to make an effort to be strong is the responsibility of those who know they are links of the same chain. We are all involved together in the sanctity of our brothers and in the progress of the apostolate. We must be firm, sure, serene, strong in the faith, out of charity with the others. Each day we are engaged in the battles of the Lord, within and

22 Barsanuphius and John, Liber utilissimus 23 From our Father, Crónica, April 1960, p. 12 24 Deut 20:8

Christian fortitude 203

outside of ourselves. Our responsibility is great; our reward also will be great. God is looking at us; at the same time he blesses us with his grace. Therefore, my beloved brethren, be steadfast, immovable, always abounding in the work of the Lord, knowing that in the Lord your labour is not in vain… stand firm in your faith, be courageous, be strong.25 In our Lady we have both a bright model and a help to reach this fortitude: Marvel at Mary’s courage: at the foot of the Cross, with the greatest of human sorrow – there is no sorrow like her sorrow – filled with fortitude. And ask her for that same strength, so that you too can remain beside the Cross.26

25 1 Cor 15:58; 16:13 26 The Way, 508

## THE DOMINION OF THE CHILDREN OF GOD {#cuaderno.3.20}

We are children of God, and from him we have received dominion over the goods of the world. Fill the earth and subdue it, God said to our first parents; and have dominion over the fish of the sea and over the birds of the air, and over every living thing that moves upon the earth.1 God created us for himself, and he placed at our service all other things, so that they might help us travel the path that he has marked for us. The things of this earth are good because they come from God. And, because they are good, they are desirable: they are sufficiently attractive for their necessary use not to be repugnant to our heart which is made for God. The goods of this earth are objectively good as means, as instruments. And in this measure they should be desired by the children of God.

The need for temperance With the disorder that sin introduced into human nature, with the tragic blindness of man’s mind and the deviation of his heart, a monstrous capacity for idolatry appeared in him. Good things turned bad through the concupiscence of men. And what ought to have been a road became a destination. Love lost its purity: goods ceased to be

1 Gen 1:28

The Dominion of the children of God 205

loved because they were good and began to be loved only because they produced pleasure and satisfaction. To enjoy oneself at all costs: this is the law of disorder. Men rushed at things without measure, without a rule, without temperance; and the result was a heart restless and sad. Thus the goods of the earth, put there for man’s service while on the way towards his goal, were changed into his tyrannical rulers. When man rebelled and did not want God to be his Lord, he found himself subjugated and enslaved by a multitude of cruel masters: the goods of the earth. Their kingly title is their capacity to put into the human heart a passing and limited joy, a joy which is unable to fill it. No man can serve two masters; for either he will hate the one and love the other, or he will be devoted to the one and despise the other. You cannot serve God and mammon.2 It was a warning of Jesus Christ, a loving warning for those who wished to preserve the peace of God in their hearts. We all have a nature disordered by original sin; but time and again we must pass in the midst of the goods of this earth, sanctifying them, using them to accomplish our supernatural mission. God, who has called us, will give us the necessary graces. But on our part, we must contribute vigilance, the exercise of the cardinal virtue of temperance, an active, real temperance, exercised every day of our journey. Our love is completely for God and temperance should guard it since, as St Augustine says, this virtue consists in “preserving love integrally and uncorrupted for God.”3 Our vocation brings us to all the crossroads of the earth, working in our own social environment. To live and to carry out our apostolic activities, we need to use human means which are usually one of the supports of many supernatural enterprises. And for all this to be a real occasion of sanctity and apostolate, we must exercise the virtue of

2 Mt 6:24 3 St Augustine, De moribus Ecclesiae catholicae 1, 15 206 Living in Christ

temperance unceasingly, often heroically. We are to use these things, 4 with the modesty of one who utentis modestia, non amantis affectu uses them, not with the affection of one who loves them.

Detachment from earthly goods Detach yourself from the goods of the world. Love and practise poverty of spirit: be content with what enables you to lead a simple and sober life. Otherwise, you will never be an apostle.5 We are asked for a habitual detachment; the habit, obtained and preserved by repetition of acts, of being always above and detached from the things we use. We must do without what is superfluous; and in what is necessary we must apply this habitual mortification which is a guarantee of right use. Do not be anxious about your life, what you shall eat… nor about your body, what you shall put on.6 What has to be avoided is excessive concern, the affection of the heart that takes away our peace. With a sense of responsibility and poverty, we have to work to obtain the necessary goods; with apostolic charity we use them effectively. But we should keep our hearts very free, properly detached. This is how we must live throughout the day. We must know how to include… among the ingredients of the meal that most tasty one of mortification. In family life, when we can choose in such a way that your choice is not noticed,7 we always try to choose the worst for ourselves. In this way we will always have just enough, the indispensable essential in clothing, in objects of personal use and work. We will know how to do without something necessary because, as our Father told us, he has most who needs least.8 Without letting ourselves

4 Ibid, 1, 21 5 The Way, 631 6 Mt 6:25 7 The Way, 635 8 Ibid, 630

The Dominion of the children of God 207

be fooled by the sophistry of the enemy, under the pretext of naturalness, we have also to practise temperance – the seal of our divine filiation – in our social, professional, and family relations… Temperance fortifies, strengthens the will and, in a certain way, confers on us once again that dominion over the things of the earth that our first parents received. Temperance is an absolutely essential condition to live a contemplative life of union with God. As St Ambrose states, “Above all we expect and seek in temperance a tranquillity of spirit.”9 Living temperately we will have a peace, a tranquillity that nothing disturbs. We will seek the goods of the earth to serve God, each one in his own state of life, with his intention well purified. And if those means do not come, if they are delayed, if they are lost, if they are less than we expected, our heart should not worry: it works and loves anchored firmly in the knowledge that we are children of God. Seek first, his kingdom and his righteousness, and all these things shall be yours as well.10 We are going to serve God with human means, with temporal goods

– those that every one uses – to which people often feel madly attracted. And God will bless us particularly, when he sees the rectitude of our intention, the temperate use that we make of these goods, with our heart detached – placed in God and in the spreading of his glory – without stealing from him even the smallest part of our affection. Undoubtedly our temperance well-lived will stand out in our ordinary social environment. And it will be an apostolic weapon, a flash of clarity, a resplendence of mastery, of supernatural outlook, besides giving a certain human tone. Men expect from us that bonus odor Christi (2 Cor 2:15) which, our Father tells us, supported by our temperance, enkindles them and moves them.11

9 St Ambrose, De Officiis 1, 43 10 Mt 6:33 11 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 65

## WORK LIKE THE BEST {#cuaderno.3.21}

Homo nascitur ad laborem… man was born to work and the bird to fly.1 Work is a natural requirement, planned from the beginning by God who placed man in the world ut operaretur,2 to work, perfecting the work of creation. After original sin man realised that the command to work had become burdensome: in the sweat of your face you shall eat bread.3 But work is still good. And Christ, our Saviour, chose to spend many years doing manual labour, to the point where it would later serve to identify him: Is not this the carpenter’s son?4

Human dimensions of work The reality of work gives shape to all human life. The goals and methods may be different, but there is no responsible person who, through his own choosing, is without work or employment. There are noble reasons for working: to support oneself, to maintain a family, to educate the children, to want them to have the possibility of a better life in the future. There are people who dedicate themselves to a task to exercise their talents: manual ability, technical and scientific capacity, or artistic creativity. Others desire to contribute to the common

1 Job 5:7 2 Gen 2:15 3 Gen 3:19 4 Mt 13:55

Work like the best 209

good, because man naturally feels responsible for his fellow men. At times, the reasons for working are not so noble. Some work for riches or power, or with the proud urge to assert their own worth, or to satisfy their passions with the product of their fatigue. There are noble reasons and selfish reasons, but always a job, which quite often is not limited to certain fixed hours. When there is a determination to get ahead, the ordinary hours are not enough. People look for possibilities of taking on two or more jobs. In human society everyone works, whether they are the head of a family or not. And people work not only for a reasonable time; many, because of their interest or their need to earn more, put even more time into their occupation.5 And since people know that normally only productive effort is well rewarded, they work intensely and seriously in the hours they have to put in, and at other times as well. And they are interested in earning the reputation of being a good worker, of someone who can be trusted because he knows how to work wholeheartedly, making use of all his knowledge. And when someone needs to know more, he studies while he continues working, attending night school, taking correspondence courses, so he can get a promotion or earn more. Of course there are idlers and people who try to work as little as possible. But it is easy and normal to find very hard-working people at university, in offices, in homes, in factories, in workshops, in the city, in the countryside. Often these people do not know Christ but have the urge to work for human reasons, noble or not so noble.

Christian industriousness If those who do not know our Lord work like this, what should Christians do? In the Gospel we have the example of Joseph and Mary, and above all of Christ’s thirty years of hidden life as a carpenter.

5 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 13 210 Living in Christ

When Jesus wants to choose his disciples, his attention is directed to hard-working men, and he calls them in the midst of their daily occupations. It is easy to imagine our Lord being pleased with the labour of the apostles when, before the miraculous catch of fish, they tell him: Master, we toiled all night…6 They had to fish to earn their living, and they had been out all night. We have St Paul’s example: We labour, working with our own hands.7 And he writes to his flock at Thessalonica: We did not eat any one’s bread without paying, but with toil and labour we worked night and day, that we might not burden any of you.8 For a Christian, the need to be always occupied is not only a natural need: it is an essential condition to be able to follow Christ’s example. “I want everyone to work,” said St John Chrysostom, “for idleness is the teacher of all vices.”9 Making good use of time has profound ascetic consequences: besides avoiding idleness, it accustoms us to sacrifice and keeps the spirit awake.

Need for a professional job We who have been called to Opus Dei – which is operatio Dei, work of God – should work for a two-fold reason. In the first place, our vocation does not take us out of the world. We have the same obligations and rights in the middle of the world as anyone else, and we ought to work as hard and as long as they do. This is a pre-condition, for it is the matter of our sanctification and therefore something indispensable, because our vocation implies precisely the sanctification of ordinary work. The need to work is at the heart of our calling. I have always said that our professional vocation, our professional job, is 6 Lk 5:5 7 1 Cor 4:12 8 2 Thess 3:8 9 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae 35, 4

Work like the best 211

part of our divine vocation. The reason for this is clear. It is a means of sanctity and a means of apostolate.10 Our work must be, to begin with, as good as that of the others, and then, because of supernatural demands, better than theirs. Our vocation preserves the noble human reasons for working and then makes them supernatural. We work because work is a path to sanctity and a hook for fishing souls.11 A particular characteristic of the spirituality of Opus Dei is that each one has to sanctify his profession – his ordinary work

– he has to sanctify himself in his profession, and he has to sanctify with his profession.12 Occasionally this professional job may be an internal task. A person exercising his profession can be transferred to some other work. And there too he ought to sanctify himself and find the means to do apostolate with souls. Our way of acting seemed like something new. Usually, even in modern apostolates, people who gave themselves to God would leave their professional job to dedicate themselves to something different from their profession, from their human vocation. Here we have said no. But we admit those wonderful exceptions which are necessary above all for the internal work of government and formation. And in the Work even these tasks are professional jobs that demand careful preparation beforehand. But the usual thing is for each one to seek sanctity, in the place where he was before coming to Opus Dei, or where he would have been if he had not come. And there he will work like his fellow-workers beside him.13 The exception refers to the type of work, but not to the work itself. Thus in the Work, every task, whether internal or external, has to be carried out with the same intensity.

10 From our Father, Crónica, July 1963, p. 9 11 Cf The Way, 372 12 Alone with God, 55 13 From our Father, Crónica, July 1963, p. 9 212 Living in Christ

Conditions for sanctifying work We must work like the best. And if possible, better than the best.14 An occupation without this characteristic would not be sanctifiable. If many hours of hard work is the norm, we cannot do less. It would be a shame. And it would not be the way to sanctify ourselves or to sanctify others. Work done seriously: to which we dedicate all our strength, all our capacity, putting ourselves completely into it with the best we have and are. We must strive to work as perfectly as we can so that our work is well-done, lasting. Our work cannot be shoddy, with unfinished elements. It is not enough for it to seem to be good: it must really be so. It matters little whether it is manual or intellectual, giving orders or carrying them out. In the service of God there are no positions of little importance. All are of great importance. The importance of a position depends on the individual carrying it out.15 Responsible work: because we support ourselves with our work, because we are poor, because we must also support a family and numerous apostolic works on which depend the spiritual good of many souls. If our work were to be habitually imperfect, it would show a lack of responsibility and substance. It would be a sign of not wanting to be saints, to be ready to be good instruments, to serve God. Intense work: though of course this demands effort. We offer our Lord the tiredness that is the result of true work, of putting our heart into what we do. Work that is completed: that can be counted in terms of hours. We are not doing God a favour when we bind ourselves to some hours of work, of labour, just as other people are bound to work for very many hours, for a very long period, always. A little bit of examination

14 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 15 15 Alone with God, 62

Work like the best 213

would not be a bad thing, to respect lovingly the hours of work, to make others respect them, to put this need in every one’s conscience.16 We cannot quit our work or culpably lessen its yield. Our intense effort has to be maintained hour after hour, yielding tangible results. We cannot have any time left over: we must administer it well to fulfil all our obligations. The Work provides times to rest for everyone, which always help us go back to our tasks with greater eagerness. At these times we rest by changing our occupation. Seriousness, responsibility, effort. We finish our work, putting in full hours. Only with an industrious life will we be able to forge our sanctity, our union with God. To work. Many hours of work each day, feeling the urgent needs, including the financial ones, of our supernatural family. This is to have a sense of responsibility, this is to want to be saints and to serve God.17

To surpass oneself Work that is well done both humanly and supernaturally is the best means of apostolate. St Augustine asks: “When was it that St Paul worked? I mean: What time did he have left that would not hinder the preaching of the gospel? Who can know? Even so, he claimed that he worked day and night. What the Apostle did was a wonderful thing, for he worked with his hands in spite of his concern for all the churches, already founded or to be founded, which were his care and responsibility.”18 He had Christ stamped on his heart. He knew that for Christians work is glory. He could have given up the occupation that wore him out. But he did not want to leave it, surpassing himself for the love of God.

16 From our Father, Crónica, July 1963, p. 10 17 From our Father, Letter 15 October 1948, 13 18 St Augustine, De op. monach. 15, 15 214 Living in Christ

Our Lord asks the same of us: In carrying out their work, the children of God in his Opus Dei try not merely to fulfil, but to love, which always means surpassing oneself gladly in duty and sacrifice.19 In facing our tasks each day we should often think of the love with which Christ surpassed himself for us. Then we, too, will know how to make demands on ourselves in our work, which is a means of sanctity and of apostolate. And we cannot forget that this is what many who do not have a commitment of Love do normally. When you have done all that is commanded you, say, ‘We are unworthy servants; we have only 20 What is new is to work only for God and done what was our duty.’ for souls. But serious, responsible, intense labour for many hours is how honest work should always be; in our case perfected, bettered, because of the demands of our vocation. The Blessed Virgin Mary, who contemplated Jesus during the years of his hidden life, through her intercession will give our work human energy and divine fruitfulness.

19 Alone with God, 66 20 Lk 17:10

## SANCTIFYING STUDY {#cuaderno.3.22}

Wisdom is radiant and unfading, and she is easily discerned by those who love her, and is found by those who seek her. She hastens to make herself known to those who desire her. He who rises early to seek her will have no difficulty, for he will find her sitting at his gates… The beginning of wisdom is the most sincere desire for instruction… so the desire for wisdom leads to a kingdom.1 Along with God’s grace, we all need constant formation to develop our ideals, our work, our abilities and our ambitions and to direct them to God, placing them at his service. Consequently, the Work is ceaselessly giving us the specific training we need to make us consistent and effective, so that God our Lord will not have to renew his lament: My people were scattered over all the face of the earth, because there was no shepherd (Ezech 34:6). Your formation can never be considered finished. All your life long you must keep on perfecting your human, spiritual, doctrinal, apostolic and professional formation, all with a marvellous spirit of humility.2 The effectiveness of this formation depends to a great extent on our personal effort to assimilate and improve it. What efforts have I made in my cultural development, which is essential to my purpose, in my

1 Wis 6:12-20 2 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 19 216 Living in Christ

study or professional formation and in making the best use of my time? Our Father commented: It is a question of having the culture necessary to carry out our job or profession with perfection and competence. Yet culture, which is acquired through personal effort and study, is a very broad term, which adapts itself very well to the variety of personal situations found in the Work. For example, a housewife’s culture does not have to be the same as a university professor’s, nor does a white collar worker’s have to be the same as a farmer’s. Culture is the sum of knowledge mankind has accumulated throughout the ages, forming an intellectual patrimony common to men of all times. But there is also a religious culture shared by all, although in varying degrees. The theologian studies revealed doctrine scientifically and in depth. A person who is not a theologian goes as far as his talents allow, and has a faith just as strong as the theologian should have. Then comes professional culture, which in our case forms part of our divine vocation. I give as much importance to the professional culture of a barber as I do to that of a person involved in research, and as much to a college student’s as to a domestic worker’s. Each person ought to have the culture of his own job, befitting his professional vocation, which we have to sanctify, in which we sanctify ourselves, and through which we help sanctify others.3

Importance of study for formation We need to study, first of all to form ourselves in the spirit of Opus Dei, to know its specific characteristics very well, and to make them part of our own life. This is the purpose of the means of formation the Work continually places within our reach: Circles, meditations, annual

3 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p. 587

Sanctifying study 217

courses... We can never exempt ourselves from making use of them, as if we already knew enough about the spirit God has given us. We can always deepen our understanding of the spirit of the Work, be more committed and discover unknown or perhaps forgotten aspects of that spirit, seeing them in a new light. Our spirit, old like the Gospel and like the Gospel new, is like that bottomless treasure from which the good head of a family brings out what is new and what is old.4 Besides, Opus Dei’s apostolic modes and apostolate are a sea without shores: we need constantly to be learning them and practising them. We should strive continually to acquire a deeper knowledge of our spirit and its specific demands. That is why we go to annual courses and other kinds of courses with the same eagerness as the first time. We try to get the most out of the Circles. Again and again, we renew our resolutions to live specific aspects of our spirit better. In short, day by day we discover its novelty and its character. Our Father frequently showed us that we also need a solid doctrinal formation to know God better and to lead an upright moral life. Ignorance is the greatest enemy of our faith, and also the greatest obstacle to carrying out the redemption of souls.5 Ignorance is found not just among the uneducated but also among people considered experts in human matters – in scientific research, history, economics, law, etc. Even those with the greatest prestige in their professional field can suffer from such ignorance, as can those who occupy important government positions in countries with an ancient tradition of Christianity.6 Especially today, religious ignorance is on the increase though often it is disguised by showy language. It is now very fashionable to speak about religion. There are people who would be hard put to say the Creed

4 Mt 13:52 5 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1951, 8 6 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1951, 7 218 Living in Christ

without making a mistake and who do not even know the catechism, yet they dare to call themselves theologians. Some even go so far as to call themselves ‘doctors’ of the Church. But the only Doctors of the Church are those who have been given this title by the Roman Pontiff after calmly studying the matter and after a very long procedure. To provide us with a solid and profound doctrinal formation, the Work gives us, to the extent and in the form that best suits each one’s circumstances, exact knowledge of the Church’s dogmatic and moral teaching, Sacred Scripture and liturgy, Church law and history.7 We have also been asked by our Father to study the catechism of Christian doctrine with effort and affection, so going ever deeper into the doctrinal treasure contained in the deposit of Revelation, and engraving in our mind the principal points of Christ’s teaching so that they are neither forgotten nor distorted. Then we will be better able to counteract the wave of religious ignorance. We cannot leave our books aside, like Friar Gerundio, and set off to preach. What I want is to have all the arguments of good doctrine fixed and clear in my mind; that is why I go over the traditional treatises of theology. And I also read literature, because words are the clothing: Fides ex auditu (Rom 10:17). We must give doctrine, good doctrine, and present it to people in an attractive way. The traditional arguments can be dressed in literary style, and brought to the common man without becoming common.8

Professional formation We need, then, to study the spirit and apostolic ways proper to the Work, as well as Christian doctrine. But you should also acquire the necessary professional formation to be able to carry out effectively an

7 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 49 8 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p. 589

Sanctifying study 219

apostolate of doctrine, through your personal activities and in your ordinary work. Each one has to acquire the professional formation befitting his place in society and his occupation, whether intellectual or manual. It will be difficult for work to be sanctified if it is not also done with human perfection. Moreover, without that human perfection a person will find it hard – not to say impossible – to attain the professional prestige he needs, that position from which to teach others to sanctify their work and to fashion their lives according to the demands of the Christian faith.9 The spirit of the Work requires each of us to have solid professional formation, with all the human knowledge he is capable of acquiring.10 And we try to improve it constantly. Since the professional activity, or ordinary trade of each one, is the instrument of our supernatural action, the members of the Work can never abandon it. They should be learned, with doctrine and an adequate knowledge of their profession; and some should even be outstanding. They should be well-versed in the teachings of Jesus Christ, and they should be cultured and have a deep knowledge of the sciences or arts or manual jobs with which they are involved.11 Besides, formerly, since human knowledge – science – was very limited, it seemed quite feasible for a single learned individual to undertake the defence and vindication of our holy Faith. Today, with the extension and intensity of modern science, the apologists have to divide the work among themselves, if they want to defend the Church scientifically in all fields. You… cannot shirk this responsibility.12

9 From our Father, Crónica, 1969, p. 589 10 The Way, 857 11 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1950, 16 12 The Way, 338 220 Living in Christ

All this requires that we know something about developments in the principal fields of knowledge, particularly in our own professional specialisation, as far as circumstances permit. So someone engaged in a rather practical type of work does not need to be as up-to-date as a professor who ought to give his students the most recent developments in his specialisation. Professional competence alone, however, is not enough. The more specialised our professional activity, the more careful we must be to acquire and develop a broad culture. This will be the basis for an open mind and for the human tone the Work expects of us so that we may be more effective supernaturally. We understand well why our Father often told us that there is no excuse, for those who could be scholars and are not. But it is not necessary for everyone to be so. On the other hand everyone must be trained, competent in his professional work, with prestige among his colleagues, known for his integrity and for his learning or skill.13

Study, a Norm of always Study. Study in earnest. If you are to be salt and light, you need knowledge, ability. Or do you imagine that an idle and lazy life will entitle you to receive infused knowledge? 14 In the Work, study is a Norm of always. Everyone has the duty whatever his profession or trade. In the Work there is room for everyone who, with a divine vocation, desires to seek holiness in ordinary work. There is room for everyone: for intellectuals, for white-collar workers, for manual and farm labourers, for all men and women striving for sanctity in the ordinary circumstances of their lives. And each in his own way must have a good grasp of his profession or job,

13 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 50 14 The Way, 340

Sanctifying study 221

so that he can carry out his daily tasks worthily.15 Although we all should study, this Norm especially obliges those having study as their profession, because then it is the immediate matter they must sanctify and convert into an instrument of apostolate. We should not deceive ourselves: You pray, you deny yourself, you work in a thousand apostolic activities, but you don’t study. You are useless then unless you change. Study – professional training of whatever type it be

– is a grave obligation for us.16 Like every other profession, study must be well-done and produce concrete results if it is to be sanctifiable. Students have to get good marks. Otherwise, how are they going to attract their classmates? Not to possess outstanding gifts is no excuse, because their lack can and should be compensated for by study habits, order and constancy; also, by asking for advice and help when necessary, and above all, by having great desires to make good use of our God-given talents, whether they be few or many. Two words are superfluous in our dictionary: tomorrow and later. Today and now! Don’t leave your work for later, and teach others not to do so. Soon you will understand how, given equal conditions and even inferior conditions as to talents, culture, etc., the one who habitually overcomes laziness ‘today, now’ is the one who always comes out on top.17 Those having an intellectual profession have also a greater obligation to study: If you are to serve God with your mind, to study is a grave obligation for you.18 It will not always mean picking up a book, but will involve every way of improving oneself professionally. Our Father gives the following advice to those who have finished the complete course of theology studies: Try to devote a little time each

15 From our Father, Letter 14 February 1950, 16 16 The Way, 334 17 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935, 46 18 The Way, 336 222 Living in Christ

day, even for only a few minutes, to studying the ecclesiastical sciences, reading the classical treatises again and again, giving more solidity to the principles.19 To improve our professional and cultural formation, in some instances it may be good to make a reading list, taking into account the Director’s advice. This could be a help to develop one’s basic culture as circumstances require. While avoiding unnecessary expenses, there are ways of getting access to journals and attending special courses when necessary. Let us also keep in mind that when we study, we should be especially concerned to make it easier for those who come after us. In 1935 our Father wrote by way of example: You should make notes of experience about all your seminar work. This will save time for your brothers in the future and make it easier for them to improve their formation.20

Making good use of time All these plans and care will be of no avail unless our desire to study is focused mainly on making good use of our time. Look carefully then how you walk, not as unwise men but as wise, making the most of the time.21 Time must be used intensely. The minutes between one activity and the next have to yield fruit. We should use to good advantage time spent travelling and waiting as well as the breaks in our work. During the Spanish civil war, our Father frequently told the members of the Work and the St Raphael fellows to use their time well: Why don’t you use the abundant free time, that would otherwise be wasted, to brush up on a foreign language? To get the most out of each day we need the virtue of order, which is

19 From our Father, Letter 8 August 1956, 15 20 From our Father, Instruction, 9 January 1935, 163 21 Eph 5:15-16

Sanctifying study 223

the best way to ensure that we will make good use of our time. This explains why we should take a quick look over our plans each day. We should establish an order because we do not have time to do everything at once. Each day you should make a list of priorities and follow it. But do not think you have to spend an hour thinking it over: that’s for people who are insane. Just a few minutes when beginning your work is enough.22 With good use made of time, and from serious spiritual, doctrinal and professional formation acquired by dint of daily effort, God will harvest abundant fruit. We find in the book of Wisdom: I called upon God, and the spirit of wisdom came to me. I preferred her to sceptres and thrones, and I accounted wealth as nothing in comparison with her… I loved her more than health and beauty, and I chose to have her rather than light, because her radiance never ceases.23 If you fulfil your professional duties well, each of you working and studying according to his circumstances, including those with manual occupations, you will have the doctrine and wisdom necessary to reach your goal… And you will draw your colleagues to the faith and love of Christ. They will understand what you teach them, and they will be able to say: audivimus eos loquentes nostris linguis magnalia Dei (Acts 2:11).24 Each one, equipped with his own professional formation, with all the human culture he can acquire, with the characteristic mentality of his milieu and status, will bring Christ’s doctrine to everyone he meets along his path through life. And he will be able to do this with the gift of tongues, in such a way that people will understand him.25

22 From our Father, Instruction, 31 May 1936, note 61 23 Wis 7:7-10 24 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1951, 31-32 25 From our Father, Letter 15 August 1953, 9

## CHEERFULNESS {#cuaderno.3.23}

Rejoice in the Lord, Oh you righteous! Praise befits the upright.1 God wants our happiness; he loves our joy. Innumerable times he has told us so. Fear not, Oh land; be glad and rejoice, for the Lord has done great things! Fear not, you beasts of the field, for the pastures of the wilderness are green; the tree bears its fruit, the fig tree and vine give their full yield. Be glad, Oh sons of Zion, and rejoice in the Lord, your God; for he has given the early rain for your vindication, he has poured down for you abundant rain, the early and the later rain, as before.2 God wants all men to be happy and glad-hearted. And the closer he calls them to himself, the happier he wants them to be. That is why joy has been an essential part of our spirit from the beginning of the Work. Good humour is, and will always be, a characteristic of the history of Opus Dei: I want you to be happy always, for cheerfulness is an essential part of your way.3 In the Work everything, including the ascetical struggle itself, is positive, affirmative, joyful. And it is noticeable. Everyone realises that in Opus Dei there is a lot of joy. Joy comes of its own accord when we feel that we are children of God. Quite often this luminous atmosphere of deep joy is the first thing that attracts people to our apostolate just as

1 Ps 33:1 2 Joel 2:21-23 3 The Way, 665

Cheerfulness 225

one day it also enchanted us, helping us to discover our vocation. A glad heart makes a cheerful countenance.4 The abundant interior joy of our divine filiation radiates outwardly. Our joy must not have exaggerated ups and downs. It must not depend on circumstances because Christian cheerfulness is not something physiological. Its foundation is supernatural, and it goes deeper than illnesses or difficulties. Cheerfulness does not mean the jingling of bells, or the gaiety of a dance at the local hall. True cheerfulness is something deeper, something within: something that keeps us peaceful and happy, though at times our face may be stern.5 Sometimes our body may tire and it will be difficult to show this inner joy. But the soul of a daughter or son of God is always serene, joyful, happy.

The temptation to sadness Nevertheless, our joy here on earth is nothing more than the beginning, an anticipation of that other joy to which we have been called. “Joy in this life cannot be complete. It will be complete in the next life, when we possess in a complete way the perfect good: Enter into the joy of your master (Mt 25:21).”6 While here on earth, joy comes with effort because the joy of us poor men, even when it has supernatural reasons, always leaves some taste of bitterness. What did you expect? Here on earth, suffering is the salt of life.7 And because of suffering, and sickness, and setbacks, and especially because of the weight of our wretchedness, we are threatened by sadness. But we are not talking here about the sadness which could be termed ‘physiological’, the result of sickness or exhaustion. Sadness that is harmful is something else. It leads the imagination to wander – stirring 4 Prov 15:13 5 The Forge, 520 6 St Thomas, Super Evangelium S. Ioannis lectura 15, 1, 2 7 The Way, 203 226 Living in Christ

up memories, building fantasies, anxiously seeking compensations. It is grumbling inside us, about our work, our struggle, our dedication. It permits one instance of negligence after another, leading to reluctance and laziness. To give way to gloominess is to surrender our weapons to the enemy. “Sadness moves one to rage and anger. Thus experience shows us that when we are sad, we easily get upset and angry about anything. Moreover, it makes a man impatient in the things he manages, it makes him suspicious and malicious. At times sadness so upsets him that it seems to take away his common sense and put him out of his wits.”8 A sad soul is predisposed to evil. Like a moth in clothing, or a worm in wood, sorrow gnaws at the human heart.9 Gloominess, depression. I am not surprised: it is the cloud of dust raised by your fall. But… that’s enough!10 We have to react at once because while it is human for sadness to touch the heart, it is bad for it to overwhelm it. We ought to reject it energetically as soon as the first symptoms appear. A son of God, a son of mine becoming sad? He may be tired because he pulls the cart like a faithful donkey, but not sad.11 Sadness is bad; it is a powerful ally of the enemy, a sure way to defeat. “The effect of sadness that is according to the world is death, because he who loves the world becomes an enemy of God.”12 This bad disposition makes the things of God produce sadness. If we do not fight against it, we end up by rejecting them. Our longing for happiness never disappears for happiness is desired necessarily. If, because of a twisted disposition, one does not find joy along the divine way, one seeks it elsewhere. First in compensations, then by going fully off the road, because one can easily believe what one wants; and since one

8 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 1, 31 9 Prov 25:20 10 The Way, 260 11 From our Father, 2 November 1958 12 St Thomas, Super Epistolam ad 2 Cor. 7, 1, 3

Cheerfulness 227

wants a life that is more pleasant, one may come to believe – if one does not rectify in time – that one does not have a vocation. Delight your soul and comfort your heart, and remove sorrow far from you, for sorrow has destroyed many.13 And just as the soul’s joy pours outward, and is a stimulus for others, this bad sadness, this sombre bitterness, darkens the atmosphere, does damage, and harms the apostolate. See to it that no one fails to obtain the grace of God; that no ‘root of bitterness’ springs up and cause trouble, and by it the many become defiled.14 You cannot do proselytism sadly. You will not convince anyone. Long face, rough manner, ridiculous appearance, unfriendly attitude. Is that how you hope to inspire others to follow Christ? 15

Humility is the remedy We cannot let sadness overcome us. We have to fight. We must know what causes it, so that we can apply the remedy at the root. “Sadness is a vice caused by the disordered love of self, which is not a special vice but the general root of all of them.”16 We will always find pride at the root of any lasting grief. Behind any unwarranted reluctance in our work, there is perhaps human disillusion, the impossibility of affirming our own personality, our ideas, our vanity. If obedience becomes difficult, perhaps it is not so much what has been commanded that is difficult as having to give way, having to accept being only an instrument. Behind any pessimistic sorrow caused by the sight of our own faults there may lie a humiliation of our ego: Gloominess, if you don’t fight it, could very well be the cloak of your pride. –Did you really

13 Sir 30:23 14 Heb 12:15 15 The Way, 661 16 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.28, a.4 ad 1 228 Living in Christ

think yourself perfect and incapable of sinning?17 Pride in any of its many manifestations is an obstacle to charity, hope and faith, which are the theological foundation of our joy. If we love ourselves in a disordered way, there are reasons to be sad: What a lot of failures! What pettiness! Seeing our wretchedness must cause sadness, discouragement.18 If the heart is emptied of all love of God, the swelling of self-love immediately begins to fill it with bitterness. Since love of God and of others for God takes us out of ourselves, the lack of love – the lack of charity – shrinks our spirit and depresses us, and sadness begins to take hold of our soul. “The soul becomes sad and anxious when it wants to have what it does not have or is afraid of losing what is has.”19 Together with a lack of love – which is a lack of generosity, a lack of dedication, and selfishness – come despair and presumption. These are a negation of supernatural hope, and because of this they cause sadness. Not hoping in God, relying on one’s own strength, is a sure road to bitterness, because experience soon destroys this human and self-sufficient confidence. Your optimism will be a necessary consequence of your faith.20 If faith is lacking (if we do not fight against our pride, faith weakens in us), our human outlook on things always presents a poor, sad, truly disheartening spectacle. We must have faith, hope and charity, which is the reason why our dedication requires steadfastness in faith, vocation, and purity. If the soul gives in to concupiscence, sadness appears. Examine yourself: slowly, courageously. Is it not true that your bad humour and your gloominess, both without cause – without apparent cause – are due to

17 The Way, 260 18 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 25 19 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 22, 14 20 The Way, 378

Cheerfulness 229

your lack of determination in breaking the subtle but real snares laid for you – cunningly and attractively – by your concupiscence?21 And this sadness that has a bad beginning has an even worse end unless it is fought off energetically.

Joy, fruit of the theological virtues Our positive, affirmative asceticism leads us not only to throw off sadness, but also to foster joy actively according to St Paul’s command: Rejoice in the Lord always; again I will say, rejoice.22 God commands us to be cheerful. Our way is one of joy, of loving fidelity to God’s service. This joy is not the peal of a silly, purely animal laughter. Our joy has very deep roots; it is something very deep. But it is compatible with physical tiredness, with sorrow – because we have a heart – with difficulties in our interior life and in our apostolic work.23 Cheerfulness is a necessary consequence of our divine filiation, of knowing that our Father God loves us with a love of predilection, that he holds us up and helps us and forgives us. Remember this and never forget it: even if it should seem at times that everything around you is collapsing, in fact nothing is collapsing at all because God doesn’t lose battles.24 We strive for the joy man can reach on earth. “Joy is full when there is nothing more to desire. But while we are in this world, the restless impulse of our desire does not cease because we have yet to get closer to God through grace.”25 Only the clear vision of God in heaven will fill us with total joy that cannot fail. Consequently, to gain the joy for which we have been destined, we must forget about ourselves through humility, and practise the three theological virtues, which lead us to

21 The Way, 237 22 Phil 4:4 23 From our Father, Crónica, December 1962, p. 11 24 The Forge, 332 25 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.28, a.3 230 Living in Christ

God, the source and foundation of all joy. “Joy is not a virtue distinct from charity, but rather a certain act and effect of it.”26 It is so necessary that the command to be cheerful is as wide as that of loving God. Hence it follows that if our vocation requires more love, it also brings more joy. That is why the good humour that accompanies the fulfilment of our daily obligations is the thermometer of the love we have in everything we do. Servite Domino in laetitia, serve the Lord with joy (Ps 100:2). Serve the Lord with gladness. Do you think people are ever grateful for a service that is rendered reluctantly? No. It would be better not to offer it at all. And are we going to serve the Lord with a long face? No. We are going to serve him cheerfully, in spite of our wretchedness, which we will in fact overcome with the grace of God.27 The important thing is to have the good will to serve, love for God and souls, and a firm determination to give ourselves and forget ourselves. Do have good humour and do things well. Well! With sanctity, in the presence of God, even when they are difficult. So, we will give great glory to God, and we will do a great good to other souls. “There are two ways of having spiritual joy in God: in so far as we enjoy the divine good considered in itself, and in so far as we participate in it. The first joy is the best, coming from charity. But the second, because we hope for the joy of the divine good, is born of hope even though this joy be perfect or imperfect according to the measure of charity.”28 Above all we must seek God’s love, but God himself wants our happiness. The joy that is asked of us is not disembodied. It has a supernatural foundation, but it is not inhuman. It also includes, duly ordered to God, the desire for our own good. God has given us the

26 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.28, a.4 27 From our Father, Meditation Living for the glory of God, 21 November 1954 28 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.28, a.4 ad 3

Cheerfulness 231

theological virtue of hope so that we might hope for this good and rejoice in this confidence. Rejoice in hope,29 in the consolation of reaching full joy, because what no eye has seen nor ear heard, nor the heart of man conceived, what God has prepared for those who love him, God has revealed to us through the Spirit.30 This supernatural hope, which is humble, lifts the heart up after a fall and gives it back the joy it has lost. Though you do not now see him, you believe in him and rejoice with unutterable and exalted joy.31 Faith in God, in his promises, in his word, in his calling. Faith in his help, in his certain victory. Faith in spite of the evidence of so much evil. Faith in the Church, faith in the Work. Faith because God has said so. And this faith is already the beginning of eternal life, happiness, unshakable joy.

Joy assured Just as pain is the symptom of illness and a warning that leads one to seek a remedy, so is sadness. Be optimistic. St Paul himself in the epistle to the Philippians tells us: Gaudete in Domino semper: iterum dico: gaudete (Phil 4:4); rejoice always in the Lord, I tell you again: be happy. My children, you should see the positive side of things. The things that appear most depressing in life are never really quite as bad as they seem. If you examine them closely enough you will not come to negative conclusions.32 Fidelity is happiness. That is why the means that assure our fidelity

– unquestioned, firm and delicate – are also the assurance of our cheerfulness: prayer, mortification, doing the Norms with order and constancy; the Eucharist, contrite and humble Confession; divine filiation; 29 Rom 12:12 30 1 Cor 2:9-10 31 1 Pet 1:8 32 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 14 232 Living in Christ

loving familiarity with our Mother; proselytism; absolute sincerity… You ask me to suggest a cure for your sadness. I’ll give you a prescription from an expert adviser, the Apostle Saint James: Tristatur aliquis vestrum? Are you sad, my son? Oret! Pray! Try it and you will see.33 It is a prayer that leads us to God, that restores our supernatural outlook which perhaps has weakened; prayer that fills us with security because we are children of God, that takes us out of ourselves, that makes us get what we need. Ask, and you will receive, that your joy may be full.34 We also need mortification, because our joy is supernatural. But far be it from me to glory except in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, by which the world has been crucified to me, and I to the world.35 For joy is the result of love, and love is tested and strengthened through suffering. Mortification makes us serene, because by it we deny ourselves, for self-love is always at the bottom of sadness. We should be sincere in examining our conscience to recognise the underlying reason for any bitterness. And we need sincerity when speaking in spiritual direction. We must be concerned for others and serve them. We should deny ourselves the right to think about ourselves when there are so many needs around us, so much to do, so much work. To be joyful there is a simple remedy and one that is always within our reach: forgetting about ourselves completely, out of love. Most of our trials are caused by forgetting about the duties we owe the rest of mankind and getting too caught up in our own ego. To forget about oneself and give oneself to the service of souls is so effective that God rewards it with a joyful humility.36

33 The Way, 663 34 Jn 16:24 35 Gal 6:14 36 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 15

Cheerfulness 233

I run the way of your commandments, for you enlarge my understanding.37 We need that joy which lightens our heart and quickens our pace. They who wait for the Lord shall renew their strength. They shall mount up with wings like eagles. They shall run and not be weary. They shall walk and not faint.38 Inner joy is the climate needed to develop all virtues and is what makes the sacrifice of our life pleasing to God. With every gift show a cheerful face.39 Joy is one of the means that God gives us to do good, because our Lord uses the joy and serenity of my children to carry his light and his peace to souls.40 Our joy has apostolic roots. Let us invoke our holy Mother Mary, causa nostrae laetitiae, the cause of our joy, if at some moment our inner joy seems to become clouded. By preserving our fidelity, she also assures our joy.

37 Ps 119:32 38 Is 40:31 39 Sir 35:9 40 From our Father, Crónica, December 1962, p. 15

## EXAMINATION OF CONSCIENCE {#cuaderno.3.24}

Teach me the way I should go, for to you I lift up my soul.1 Since coming to Opus Dei, we are firmly determined to fulfil God’s will, to be saints, because this is the will of God, your sanctification.2 In spite of this basic intention, our advance towards God is not always as straight as it should be. We have followed the Master’s advice: duc in altum,3 put out into the deep. When we began the long journey of our sanctification in the fragile boat of good desires and willingness to serve, we knew that not all the equipment was in good shape. We knew that there was a lot of ballast we would have to throw overboard. And afterwards, all along the way, in the thousand battles we have had to fight, we have acquired a clearer awareness of our frailty. In every storm, in every failure, we have seen that there was much to mend. The daily examination of conscience shows us our interior weakness and inclination to evil. And this daily light is the source of a new conversion, of a quicker pace along the pathways of God. Your interior life has to be just that: to begin… and to begin again. Improving a little each day.

Examination, a loving task Here lies the heart of the constant battle of our spiritual life. It

1 Ps 142:8 2 1 Thess 4:3 3 Lk 5:4

Examination of Conscience 235

admits no truces because we will never be free of faults and imperfections. In addition to the bad things we started with, we discover little by little, each day in God’s presence, new things for which to be sorry and to ask forgiveness. Every day, if we are truly decided to reach our goal, we should examine how the day has gone, what we have yet to clean, where we have strayed from the path to sanctity. We ought to examine ourselves to see whether we have distanced ourselves from God, whether our heart is still stuck to creatures, whether we have fulfilled the serious obligations of our apostolic mission. Examination of conscience: a daily task. Book-keeping is never neglected by anyone in business. And is there any business worth more than the business of eternal life?4 The need to examine our consciences arises from love, from sensitivity. There is no better foundation; we will not find any better reason to examine ourselves than love. Our dedication in the Work is a homage to the Blessed Trinity. And each day is a gift, a surrendering of everything offered. The concern of a soul in love is summed up in the desire to please God alone. Were you pleased with this day, Lord? In what did I displease you? What did you expect from me that I did not do? And when the soul discovers its sins, imperfections and defects, there arises an act of contrition and the resolution to improve the following day, because this is the purpose of the examination of conscience: “Clean your soul and protect it with the examination of your heart, so that all the stains that come from malice and all the uncleanness from vices disappear. Then make your soul shine and bedeck it with the splendour of the virtues. Search yourself, find out what you are; do everything possible to know yourself.”5 To do the daily examination well – during those moments we

4 The Way, 235 5 St Basil, Hom. 3 236 Living in Christ

usually dedicate to it – requires having an habitual spirit of examination, a desire to know oneself and to be always in God’s presence, and to act accordingly. As “a good banker who each evening computes his profits and losses. But this cannot be done in detail, if during the day he does not register all the accounts in the books. A look at each and every annotation shows the situation of the whole day.”6 To examine ourselves as we should, it is not enough to review the accounts at night. The examination is something that is being prepared all through the day, constantly registering in the book of our sensitive conscience – even making notes if necessary – the value of our day’s actions. In this way we shall really know ourselves and we shall be able to grasp at a glance the specific actions and dispositions of our heart; and we shall be able to plan the battle well.

Humility and courage in the personal examination To know oneself is not easy; we always encounter serious obstacles. At the time of your examination beware of the devil that ties your tongue.7 Disordered love for one’s own excellence always tries to stop us from seeing ourselves as we are, with all our wretchedness. Pride, which so easily sees the faults of others, is blind when it comes to one’s own. It leaves the field open to the dumb devil who subtly introduces himself into the soul disguised in many ways, even as virtue. Perhaps we do not deny in a general way that we are sinners, but we always resist admitting it in particular. To examine oneself without humility is like trying to see with the eyes of a blind man: their ears are hard of hearing, and they have shut their eyes; so that they might not look with their eyes and listen with their ears.8 There’s nothing! And immediately:

6 St John Climacus, Scala parad. 4 7 The Way, 236 8 Mt 13:15

Examination of Conscience 237

O God, I thank you that I am not like other men, extortioners, unjust, adulterers… I fast twice a week, I give tithes of all that I get.9 Everyone can say with the Psalmist: Behold, I was brought forth in iniquity, and in sin did my mother conceive me.10 Admitting our unworthiness, being sincere, knowing that we are really sinners, is the first step, the necessary prerequisite to make a good examination. A righteous man falls seven times, and rises again.11 My children, do not be ashamed to have miseries. Do not get frightened because you have the fomes peccati in your heart. Do you remember what a nineteenth century French writer said? I do not know what the heart of a criminal will be like, but I looked into the heart of an honest man and I was shocked. Don’t be shocked by anything. Be truly faithful! Sincere! 12 Be sincere: examine yourself: slowly, courageously.13 We must not be afraid. We have to lose the fear of discovering our own unworthiness. This will not make us lose heart because we always count on the infinite power of God, who only waits for us to recognise our littleness to come to our help. Not to be careful about our examination of conscience, to do it lightly and hurriedly out of fear to see the truth, to justify ourselves with naive pretexts, does not make us better. We would be closing our eyes to reality and abandoning the field to the enemy. The devil “has always concentrated his work and his effort in not letting us examine our heart, because he is well aware of the benefits the soul obtains from daily examination.”14 And later, when the remedy is much more difficult, the same devil will take pleasure in

9 Lk 18:11-12 10 Ps 50:5 11 Prov 24:16 12 From our Father, Crónica, November 1962, p. 9 13 The Way, 237 14 Hesiquius, De temperantia et virtute 1, 30 238 Living in Christ

opening our eyes before a disheartening reality: I have forsaken my house, I have abandoned my heritage. I have given the beloved of my soul into the hands of her enemies… Many shepherds have destroyed my vineyard, they have trampled down my portion, they have made my pleasant portion a desolate wilderness.15 We must fight to eliminate the obstacles that keep us from making a good examination of conscience every day. We should be forewarned against the dumb devil and also against laziness which, in the things of God, signals lukewarmness, the beginning of the death of the soul, the sepulchre of interior life. One of its first manifestations is precisely little effort in examining oneself, a sign that our love for God has grown cold, that our interest for divine things is dying. Then, as in a field the farmer leaves unattended for a time, the thistles of defects and the nettles of disordered passions that choke the good seed of grace do not take long to start growing in the soul. I passed by the field of a sluggard, by the vineyard of the man without sense; and lo, it was all overgrown with thorns; the ground was covered with nettles, and its stone wall was broken down.16 If we are sincere and if we have a real desire for sanctity, or if at least we fight to have it, we shall not get as far as this. For our humble effort will attract divine grace. My child, how are you doing? How well do you prepare yourself for a rigorous examination, asking our Lord for grace so that you may know him, and know yourself, and in this way make a new conversion?

Spirit of examination Sincerity with ourselves, the disposition to examine ourselves, is only the first stage in the difficult road leading to self-knowledge. “Truly

15 Jer 12:7-10 16 Prov 24:30

Examination of Conscience 239

there is nothing more difficult, nor more laborious, nor more costly.”17 It matters little – unless it is the case of serious faults – to know the number of times we have fallen during the day, or the number of imperfections on any given day, if we do not know the root of those faults. Just as to cure a rash you do not treat the spots one by one, but you seek the cause and concentrate the proper medicine there. Only by getting at the root will we cure the tree. And this is precisely what makes the examination of conscience more difficult, because it demands a serious effort, a continuous vigilance to discover our soul’s deep inclinations and dispositions. Custos, quid de nocte? Custos, quid de nocte? (Is 21:11). Sentry, be alert! We must be on the watch. We must heed that cry. We must be on guard duty, my children. We have to be alert.18 We cannot be satisfied with the first thing we see. We must penetrate the initial darkness, sharpen our sight, go deeper every day so as to know ourselves. Where is my heart? What are my intentions? What is it that habitually occupies my faculties and my senses? And here is where we have to be cautioned once more against pride that perhaps tries to distract us and hide what is really important.
