We have also to be on guard against worrying about trivia, against a legalistic and superficial formalism that leads to want to register everything, down to the smallest detail. What is important is to reach the roots. A meticulous enumeration is usually the fruit of hidden pride, of a pathological search for self-perfection which tires and asphyxiates the soul, killing the freedom of spirit of the children of God, and it ends up by leading to a lax conscience because it is unbearable in either the long or the short run. Besides, this sickly meticulousness lets what is really important escape. That was the reproach Jesus made to the

17 St Nilus, Epistolae 3, 314 18 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 13 240 Living in Christ

scribes and Pharisees: You tithe mint and dill and cummin, and have neglected the weightier matters of the law, justice and mercy and faith; these you ought to have done, without neglecting the others. You blind guides, straining out a gnat and swallowing a camel!19 Many of the little falls of the day have been erased with an act of the contrary virtue. To root out a habit demands a repetition of contrary acts. What it is important to know are the habits, the dominating keynotes of our day. Insignificant details would only manage to discourage us and make our struggle ineffective by spreading our attention over too many points. The small details should not overwhelm us, but rather should serve as pointers for the diagnosis. But they also lead a sensitive heart, a person truly in love, to sorrow. Prayer, a thorough examination on a day of recollection, the guidelines we receive in spiritual direction, will help us discover the fundamental dispositions of our soul. And “when you know yourself, you will be able to know God and turn your spirit away from creatures as you should.”20

In the presence of God Sincerity. Courage. Humility. Spirit of examination. These are the dispositions of will we need. They are the preparation, the ante-chamber, for examination. It belongs to the intellect, then, to determine what good and what bad elements there have been in our day. And this reflection should begin by asking God for help. You, O Lord, know me. You see me and test me – my heart is with you.21 Nothing escapes the sight of our Father God. Nobody knows us – ourselves least of all – better that God: sharper than any two-edged

19 Mt 23:23-24 20 St Nilus, Epistolae 3, 314 21 Jer 12:3

Examination of Conscience 241

sword, piercing to the division of soul and spirit, of joints and marrow, and discerning the thoughts and intentions of the heart.22 What may be hidden to us is plain in God’s sight. We need his help to know ourselves and to be able to make use of suitable remedies. Above all we need to keep God present in our examination because we do not go there to list our faults as simple deficiencies of a human plan or as technical errors. The examination is not a form of introspection. It is not done out of psychological curiosity, or out of desire for self-perfection, or out of stubbornness to accomplish something we had decided. We go to the examination to be sorry for our sins, to see the faults that indicate our lack of love. We are less concerned about not matching up to our ideal and more about the possible offence against God. The examination of conscience is a dialogue between the repentant soul and God. “We will never succeed in knowing ourselves unless we seek to know God: let us think of his greatness and then come back to our own baseness; by looking at his purity we shall see our foulness; by meditating on his humility, we shall see how far we are from being humble.”23

How to do the examination The general examination is a suit made to measure for each individual. There are many practical ways of doing the examination of conscience. To choose one or another will always depend on personal circumstances. One can’t give fixed rules. An examination that works well for one person might not work well for another. And even for a given individual it only works well for a time and not afterwards. This depends on each person’s situation. Each one should arrange it with his spiritual director.24

22 Heb 4:12 23 St Teresa, Interior Castle 1,2,9 24 From our Father, Crónica, November 1962, p. 13 242 Living in Christ

In the Confidence we receive this tailor-made suit. With its help we should seek the method that is best adapted to our personal situation: the points that we ought to see in greater detail, the time that should be dedicated to it. It’s not usually necessary to spend a lot of time at it. Nevertheless, although the way of doing it will be a very personal thing, we are never dispensed from examining ourselves. Every day “directing our attention to our conscience, let us ask it to give account of our words, actions and thoughts,”25 and omissions. In our dealings with God, in our interaction with those close to us, in our apostolate, in our work, in the obligations of our state… With the responsibility that the divine election for sanctity and apostolate confers upon us, we must ask ourselves: What have you personally contributed so as not to lose that intimacy with Jesus Christ, and so that your brothers may not lose it either? What have you been thinking about? About yourself, or about others? About yourself, your own things, your silly little things, your wretchedness, your flashes of pride, your things of sensuality? About what do you spend your time thinking? Examine your conscience.26 We need to have a position from which to survey, so that, as by a searchlight, it reveals in our actions, in our omissions, in our feelings, thoughts and words the fundamental dispositions of our heart. We must examine our whole day as a doctor who, suspecting an illness, examines specific organs seeking symptoms, appraising the signs that are perhaps minuscule, but which determine the entire diagnosis. We shall discover our faults of deed or omission regarding charity, temperance, fortitude, humility… when we go out to look for them. If we are not on the watch – and this applies fully to all of us – they will escape our notice. When we are in God’s presence all day, when a spirit of examination has kept our soul alert, the general examination is easy. Then, a glance is

25 St John Chrysostom, Sermo 4 26 From our Father, Meditation Let it be seen that it is You!, 1 April 1962

Examination of Conscience 243

enough. But it is not always like that. There are moments when God is preparing us for a new conversion that will be a new step in our dedication, for breaking with something tying us down. And we will have to make up our minds to go deeper. At times the examination requires more effort: we do not see anything, it is difficult to be specific, we have the general impression that things are not going well. It is the moment to use the means that everyone uses. At times it seems that you are going backwards and you have to go back to little tricks you used at the beginning… Book-keeping, numbering the defects or the acts of a specific virtue…27 At other times – because of tiredness, or sickness, or because of a temptation or pride – scruples may come and there will be an obsession with examining ourselves, a sickly anxiety. Then, with the advice of the Director and the priest, it is usually best to simplify the examination. The Father has told us: At times I have recommended an examination of just three questions: What have I done badly? What have I done well? What could I have done better? This works well for scrupulous persons. But I can’t act like a doctor who takes out of his pocket prescriptions which he has prepared beforehand. In some cases a very simple examination is enough, whereas in others it’s better to go into greater detail. The best thing is to ask the Director.28

The most important thing The knowledge of what we have done well moves us to give thanks. And consciousness of our offences moves us to sincere sorrow for out faults with a firm resolution never to sin again: No more, Jesus, no more. Because our examination is a requirement of love, we should feel sorrow in the depths of our heart when we see so many failures to love

27 From our Father, Crónica, November 1962, p. 15 28 From our Father, Crónica, November 1962, p. 15 244 Living in Christ

throughout the day. Sorrow is perhaps the most important part of our examination. “Place all your faults before your eyes. Place yourself in front of yourself, as in front of another person; and then weep over yourself.”29 If the examination does not lead to sorrow, it is useless. That is why our Father advised us: Always end your examination with an act of Love – of Love-sorrow: for yourself, for all the sins of mankind. And consider the fatherly care of God in removing the obstacles in your way lest you should stumble.30 Sorrow of love. Reparation. A sign that our desires for sanctity are true, that we are ready to do God’s will. That is why, when our resolutions are not borne out in deeds, when time and again we fall into the same fault, we should admit that our sorrow is not complete. There may still be indecision in our dedication, little ties that keep us from reaching God. Because whoever receives a blow that hurts him instinctively avoids the occasion of getting hit again. So too in our interior life. If in spite of our examination we fall again and do not improve over a period of time, we have reason to wonder about a kind of contrition we have that does not yield resolutions borne out in deeds. To be sure, we see our debts, but our love lacks strength. It is good that you acknowledge your debts; but don’t forget how they are paid: with tears… and with deeds.31 We should normally draw only one resolution – two at the most. It should be specific; often it will simply renew previous resolutions; and this is the crown of the examination. It is love put into practice; and since love means deeds, not sweet words, a sincere resolution is for God the most convincing and pleasing demonstration that we are sorry for having offended him, that we are resolved not to fall again. My son,

29 St Bernard, Meditat. piissimae 5 30 The Way, 246 31 The Way, 242

Examination of Conscience 245

you should think over your life and ask forgiveness. At the sight of your poor life, ask forgiveness and make the firm, specific and welldetermined resolution to improve in this and in that; in what is difficult for you, in what habitually you do not do as you should, and you know it.32

Effectiveness of the examination of conscience A fruit of the examination is the knowledge of one’s faults, which helps to appreciate better the value of virtue. “The remembrance of our sins moderates our thoughts and leads to humility, by which we attract God’s mercy.”33 The examination of conscience improves the tactics of our struggle too. We will know how to direct our energies to the right place, to the weak spots in our interior citadel. That supernatural way of conduct is a true military tactic. You carry on the war – the daily struggles of your interior life – far from the main walls of your fortress. And the enemy meets you there: in your small mortifications, in your customary prayer, your methodical work, your plan of life: and with difficulty will he come close to the easily-scaled battlements of your castle. And if he does come, he comes exhausted.34 The examination of conscience is a weapon that will lead us to final victory, to holiness with apostolic effectiveness, because God does not abandon the person who humbles himself, nor does he deny his grace to one who makes an effort to please him. From the first day that you set your mind to understand and humbled yourself before your God, your words have been heard.35 Examination, contrition, resolutions: knowledge, love, service. This 32 From our Father, Crónica, November 1962, p. 16 33 St John Chrysostom, Sermo 4 34 The Way, 307 35 Dan 10:12 246 Living in Christ

is a summary of the life of a Christian, born to know, love and serve God in this world and to enjoy him in the next. This was the life of Christ our Saviour. O righteous Father, the world has not known you, but I have known you.36 I seek not my own will but the will of him who sent me.37 The examination of conscience is a great means to conform our actions to Christ’s, to unite our life to his, and identify ourselves with him. Our Lady, Morning Star for voyagers, will give us the light we need each day in our examination to reach our port, if we ask her confidently.

36 Jn 17:25 37 Jn 5:30

## THE PARTICULAR EXAMINATION {#cuaderno.3.25}

Each day, my dear children, should witness our eagerness to fulfil the divine mission which God, through his mercy, has entrusted to us.1 The particular examination is a sign of our daily eagerness to carry out what God asks of us. It is the effective struggle on a specific point where we do battle with the enemy, so that the enemy does not give us battle where it would go badly for us.2

The importance of the particular examination The particular examination is a consequence of our will to struggle to be a saint realistically and effectively. It is a strategy to conquer our defects. The general examination implies defence. The particular, attack. The first is your armour. The second, your sword.3 It helps us advance, overcome obstacles, and to make progress continually. The short but frequent examination of a specific point keeps our fighting spirit alive throughout the day. It does not let us become slack. And that persistence is the best antidote against negligence and the ravages of lukewarmness. “Let no one attribute his going astray to a sudden fall, but to having followed bad advice or having fallen away from virtue little by little,

1 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 1 2 From our Father, Crónica, April 1968, p. 7 3 The Way, 238 248 Living in Christ

through prolonged mental laziness. That is the way bad habits begin to gain ground, imperceptibly, and an extremely grave situation arises. We read in Proverbs: Pride goes before destruction, and a haughty spirit before a fall (Prov 16:18). The same thing happens as with a house: it falls down one fine day simply because of an old defect in the foundation or because of the prolonged negligence of its inhabitants. Little drips penetrate imperceptibly, corroding the supports of the roof. And thanks to continued neglect, the gaps widen and the deterioration spreads. Then rain and storms come through in torrents.”4 The particular examination, as well as preventing this danger, assures the effectiveness of our effort. St Paul said: I do not run aimlessly, I do not box as one beating the air.5 Our struggle must include tactics to avoid dispersion, to concentrate efforts on a specific front. “Many people frequently propose a multitude of things to themselves, but nevertheless do very few of them, for with the same ease with which they formulate a resolution, they afterwards abandon it. We should, in the first place, propose something practical for ourselves, and then, stick firmly to that resolution.”6 We are like little children, who cannot manage many tasks at once, or projects that are overly ambitious. We should go along little by little, “like young children in school. They first learn the form of the letters. Afterwards, they begin to distinguish the curves, and thus, step by step, they end up learning to read. Let us do likewise. Dividing virtue into parts, let us first learn, for example, not to speak badly, not to swear, not to curse. Then, passing to another letter, let us learn not to envy anyone, not to be slaves of the body, not to let ourselves be carried away by gluttony, not to be cruel, not to be lazy. Afterwards, passing

4 Cassian, Collat., 6,17 5 1 Cor 9:26 6 Richard of St Victor, De erud. hom. inter 2,5

The Particular Examination 249

from there to spiritual letters, let us study continence, mortification of the senses, chastity, justice, scorn of vainglory. Let us try to be modest and contrite of heart. Combining some virtues with others, let us write them in our soul. We should practise this in our own home, with our friends, with our wife, with our children.”7

Matter for the particular examination It is not enough that we struggle with constancy, little by little, and on specific fronts; we need also to centre our efforts on a crucial point. Ask for light. Keep on asking, until the root is laid bare and you can get at it with your battle-axe, the particular examination,8 our Father told us. A particular examination aimed at the wrong target would lose much of its effectiveness. “For since not everyone is attacked in the same way, it becomes necessary for each one of us to struggle taking into consideration the kind of strife with which he is harassed. For some, the first front will be a vice that occupies third place according to the common way of numbering them. For others, it will be in the fourth or fifth place, and so on, according to the importance each vice has in us. In the same way that attack presupposes tactics, we too must establish an order of combat which, through its advantages, will lead us to triumph, to victory, which will purify our heart and lead us to full perfection.”9 There is often one defect that tends to prevail over the rest and shows itself continually: in our way of thinking, of judging, in our preferences, in our feelings. “When we see ourselves especially dominated by one defect, we should take arms against that enemy only, and try to combat him before the others… for as long as we have not

7 St John Chrysostom, In Matthaeum homiliae 11,8 8 The Way, 240 9 Cassian, Collat., 5,27 250 Living in Christ

overcome him, we will waste the fruits of victory we have gained over the rest.”10 We should see the struggle to uproot defects positively. The spirit of the Work is always an affirmation: it is joyful, supernatural, sporting.11 “Nothing is more foreign to us than negation; it is contrary to sound psychology. The Father has always taught us to do things out of Love, for positive reasons and with a positive spirit.”12 The best way to achieve this is to direct our particular examination toward a positive objective: the practice of a virtue, the attainment of some clear-cut goal. Besides, to try to cultivate a virtue is the surest guarantee of not falling into the opposite defect. In this way the struggle becomes attractive, and above all, more effective. This is because “the tendency of the soul toward the good is stronger than that directed toward avoiding evil.”13 The nature of the struggle our Lord wants of us makes us understand that “man ought not to try – it is impossible anyway – to root out sin by his own strength. To struggle, to return to the fray, to fall, to be wounded, are all within his possibilities. But to remove sin belongs to God alone.”14 This is something the particular examination makes us perceive vividly. Seeing ourselves beaten time and time again in a resolution as specific as is the matter of our particular examination, leads us to recognize clearly that alone we can do nothing. Victory cannot come as the fruit of our willpower; it is the fruit of God’s grace for which we must ask humbly. We should begin by having recourse to God so that he makes us see the point in which we ought to struggle. Search me, O God, and know my heart! Try me and know my thoughts! And see if there be any

10 St John Climacus, Scala parad. 15 11 From our Father, Instruction, 8 December 1941, note 58 12 Don Alvaro, note 58 to our Father’s Instruction, 8 December 1941 13 St Thomas, S. Th., I-II, q.29, a.3 c 14 Pseudo Macarius, Hom 3,3

The Particular Examination 251

wicked way in me, and lead me in the way everlasting!15 We must examine ourselves deeply and courageously to know ourselves interiorly. You have already heard it said that the best business venture in the world would be to buy men for what they are really worth and sell them for what they think they are worth. Sincerity is difficult. Pride does violence to the memory, it clouds it. The deed is shaded over or embellished, and one finds a justification to cloak with goodness the evil committed, which one is not willing to rectify.16 If we are sincere with ourselves in the general examination in order to be so later in spiritual direction, our Lord will give us light to pinpoint the area of struggle successfully.

Perseverance in our struggle It is essential that the particular examination be something alive, practical, yielding tangible results. It is very personal: it varies according to each one’s temperament, interior dispositions and situation. There are those who tend to dispersion in their activity and have difficulty in finding the moments and opportunities to practise a particular virtue. For others, it is easy to find specific opportunities to live very general and wide indications. Some need to keep a very strict and exact account so as not to grow lax. For others, such rigidity may cause interior complication, and make them not feel free with the glorious freedom of the children of God,17 or create unnecessary problems for themselves. A reason for changing the matter of the particular examination sometimes may be its negative and counter-productive results, if such a struggle leads to anxiety, or increases the tendency to think of oneself

15 Ps 138:23 16 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 36 17 Rom 8:21 252 Living in Christ

or to fight with the strength of one’s own forces, rather than abandoning oneself totally to God. At other times prolonged perseverance on the same point of struggle, with little positive result, may be what makes us finally feel that “it is impossible to attain victory over any passion, without first having understood that our personal efforts and work cannot bring victory to us… Experience and innumerable testimonies of Scripture persuade us with astounding clarity that our human strength, if it is not founded on the help that only God can give, will not be able to overcome such powerful enemies, and that it is to him we must attribute the honour of each day’s victories.”18 Weariness, in a point of struggle which does not produce the desired result in spite of our effort, is not in itself a sufficient reason to change the particular examination. In some cases, behind that desire for change there may be hidden an unwholesome desire of avoiding defeat, of finding an opportunity to show one’s own worth. On the other hand, with the passing years, the different manifestations of evil tend to be seen more and more clearly rooted in a single fundamental defect, against which we will have to struggle in one form or another all our lives, perhaps never completely rooting it out. We were often told by our Father that sanctity does not consist in being entirely free from defects, but rather in always struggling against them without slackening. When we experience any weakness in ourselves or in others, we shouldn’t be surprised. Let us remember all those who, in spite of their undeniable failings, persevered and carried the word of God to all nations and became saints, and let us resolve to keep on fighting. What counts is perseverance.19 The struggle is only apparently ineffective, because it is precisely in that apparent ineffectiveness where we learn humility, the foundation of all the other virtues.

18 Cassian, Collat., 5, 42, 15 19 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 48

The Particular Examination 253

We all have faults, even though we may have been struggling for years and years to overcome them. When our ascetical struggle yields discouragement, it is because we are proud. We must be humble, with the will to be faithful. It is true that servi inutiles sumus (Lk 17:10), but with these useless servants our Lord will do very great things in the world if we contribute something ourselves, that is, the effort of lifting our hand to grasp the one which God, with his grace, offers us from heaven.20

Confidence in God The need to depend on God will lead us to accompany our effort with a special petition for help. Our Lord tells us: I counsel you to buy from me gold refined by fire, that you may be rich, and white garments to clothe you and to keep the shame of your nakedness from being seen.21 Even though we may have exhausted all the human means to achieve a particular goal, even though the struggle and personal effort may not be enough to reach it, because it is a question of a gratuitous gift from God, prayer will continue to be the best means to go ahead on the road marked out by our particular examination. If you feel crestfallen when you experience, perhaps in a particularly acute way, your own wretchedness, it is the moment to abandon yourself completely, with docility, into God’s hands. There is a story about a beggar who one day went out to meet Alexander the Great, asking for alms. Alexander stopped and commanded that he be made lord over five cities. The poor man, confused and bewildered, exclaimed: I did not ask for so much! Alexander replied: You have asked in accord with what you are; I give in accord with what I am.22

20 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 24 21 Rev 3:18 22 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 25 254 Living in Christ

Forceful struggle, even though essential, is not the most important part of the particular examination. It should be, above all, a special occasion for examination of conscience, and thus for contrition. An examination of conscience that did not bring us to have sorrow, sincere repentance and the desire to make reparation, would not bring us closer to God. If our resolutions and plans for improvement are not to be mere human efforts condemned to sterility, they should yield sorrow: a lovesorrow for having offended God. The particular examination ought to bring us to ask God for pardon many times each day: as often as we have not known how to give what our Lord asks of us because of our weakness or poor dedication. And it should move us to an act of thanksgiving each time we come out victorious. If we struggle with humility and supernatural outlook, we will be cheerful and full of hope. We will be convinced that the ascetical struggle, as the Apostle teaches, is not something negative and therefore hateful. Rather, it is joyful affirmation; it is a sport.23 And then we will not give in to tiredness, we will not let ourselves be overcome by discouragement. The good sportsman does not struggle to achieve one single victory, and that at the first attempt. He prepares himself and trains for a long time. Confidently and serenely, he tries once and again, tenaciously, until he overcomes the obstacle.24 In this way, when we have finally achieved a total or partial victory over a specific defect, we will have achieved real progress on our way to God. The spirit of Opus Dei is not like an isolated thread; rather it is a woven fabric made up of virtues knitted together, joined by charity. It is a complete fabric that covers our life from head to foot. When there is one ripe fruit on a tree, there are normally many others that are ripe if the tree was pruned on time. The taste, size and

23 From our Father, Letter 28 March 1955, 24 24 From our Father, Letter 28 March 1955, 24

The Particular Examination 255

ripeness of one announce the ripeness of the rest.25 In any case, even when we are not able to overcome in a particular point of struggle, we must go on, persevering, cheerful, rectifying a little each day, as the ships on the high seas do to reach port. The saints were like us. They had good will and the sincerity to rectify in their interior life, in their struggle: with victories and with defeats, which at times are victories. They sought intimacy with God, which is hope, which is faith, which is Love. Our God is happy with our struggle, which is a sure sign that we have interior life.26

25 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 84 26 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 49

## PIETY OF CHILDREN, DOCTRINE OF THEOLOGIANS {#cuaderno.3.26}

Our life as children of God in Opus Dei is of total love. It is a life of piety: filial love for our Father God, because we are incorporated into his only-begotten Son through the Holy Spirit. And because you are sons, God has sent the Spirit of his Son into our hearts, crying: ‘Abba, Father!’1

The virtue of piety St Thomas said that “both the gift of piety and the virtue of piety exist to honour God. But the gift does so in a divine manner and with a divine measure, for it gives honour to God not because we owe it to him but because he is worthy to receive it. And this is the same manner in which God gives honour to himself.”2 A life of piety leads us to worship God in a “most holy and divine way.”3 Piety is fundamentally a task of the heart. It is an affectionate tenderness that comes from knowing we are his children, from understanding that our Father, and very much our Father, is God who is both near us and in heaven,4 following the words of Psalm 2: Filius meus es tu: ego

1 Gal 4:6 2 St Thomas, In III Sent., d.34, q.3, a.2, q.1.1 ad 1 3 St Isidore of Pelusium, Epist. 1, 3, ep.163 4 The Way, 267

Piety of children, doctrine of theologians 257

hodie genui te;5 you are my son, this day I have begotten you. A life of piety is the origin, the source, of acts of love of God. It is a habitual disposition, a stable love. While the mind attends to each moment’s small duties, the heart is fixed on God, receiving the gentle force of grace which makes it tend towards the Father, in the Son and through the Holy Spirit. Piety gives our souls the supernatural instinct that our Father wants us to have, and what St Ignatius of Antioch felt in his heart as he walked to his martyrdom: “living water that rippled and whispered within: Come to the Father.”6 We must be devout as children because children are simple and readily devout. Piety is a work of the heart, rather than of difficult reasoning and wearisome attention. And before God we are very small children. Besides being a child, you are a child of God. Don’t forget it.7 It is just that we should want to approach God as babbling children, like the prophet Jeremiah: Ah, Lord God! Behold, I do not know how to speak, for I am only a youth.8 We come to God’s presence not knowing what to ask for or how to pray as we ought.9 But it is the same Spirit of adoption which helps us in our weakness and cries out from our heart with sighs too deep for words. And he who searches the hearts of men knows what is the mind of the Spirit.10 As if of necessity a father always has great affection for his little child. He knows what it needs; he knows its inclinations. With a loving providence he foresees dangers, leading it by the hand when the child takes its first halting steps. And the child, who hardly knows how to reason yet, feels protected and safe, and abandons itself in its father’s

5 Ps 2:7 6 St Ignatius of Antioch, Epist. ad Rom. 7, 2 7 The Way, 860 8 Jer 1:6 9 Rom 8:26 10 Ibid, 26-27 258 Living in Christ

strong arms. Piety brings about this abandonment. Piety fortifies hope, the security of arriving at a good end, and gives peace and simple joy in this life. It is of value in every way, as it holds promise for the present life and also for the life to come.11 That is why piety is basically a task of the heart. And our Father urged us to have a deep and sincere life of piety when he asked us: let us place Christ in our hearts.

Doctrine and love for God Piety is sincere when it is the result of love, when it is the form, manifestation and overflow of filial love. Piety is false when it is merely external and affected. It is also false when it feeds upon little emotions, sought in themselves, reducing God to a mere means. True piety is not a question of sugar-sweet feelings, but comes from the heart, a heart that lives by love and is strong and at the same time refined. To have a refined love, we must use our heart. To have a strong love, a firm piety, we need knowledge, the doctrine of theologians. Our founder wanted us to have the piety of children and the doctrine of theologians.12 St Gregory expresses the same idea: “Knowledge is useless without piety; and piety is of little use without knowledge.”13 They are not opposed, although “at times knowledge and all that implies greatness is an occasion for man to trust in himself and thus not to give himself completely to God… But if man perfectly submits to God his knowledge and any other perfection he has, for this very reason his devotion increases.”14 Devotion, a characteristic fruit of piety, arises from a filial love for God (and for everything that in some way is related to God). It consists 11 1 Tim 4:8 12 From our Father, Crónica, April 1959, p. 8 13 St Gregory the Great, Moralia 1, 32 14 St Thomas, S. Th., II-II, q.82, a.3 ad. 3

Piety of children, doctrine of theologians 259

essentially in prompt and cheerful giving of oneself to all that refers to God’s service. From this spirit of devotion come devotions, small acts of love willingly offered to our Father God. These devotions are stronger the more directly their object refers to God. Hence our devotion to the Blessed Trinity; our devotion to the Eucharist and to Christ’s Humanity; to our Lady, to St Joseph, to the Archangels and Angels, to the Apostles, to the rest of the saints… in the order that charity requires. The truly supernatural Christian, who has had to exercise great fortitude to become a child through filial love, is the one who can give devotions their true meaning. It is he who can place them in the centre of his heart and his will, giving them the necessary vitality so that they flow from a true, strong and deep piety which is pleasing to God. In this way, without sentimentalities or banalities, a person with real piety transforms his devotions into channels of love; channels that have a two-fold movement of ebb and flow. For just as the human mind, due to its weakness, has to be led as it were by the hand to the knowledge of divine things through earthly realities, so too our love needs assistance. The pious contemplation of things that appeal to our senses and that lead to God, helps our piety, strengthens our filial love, and keeps our heart recollected, enabling it to express docilely the sighs too deep for words15 with which the Holy Spirit cries out lovingly from the Son and says through us, as we walk life’s path: Abba, Father. Our effectiveness is then assured. In the words of our Father: the secret of our effectiveness is piety, sincere piety.16

15 Rom 8:26 16 From our Father, Crónica, April 1959, p. 9

## THE HOLY EUCHARIST {#cuaderno.3.27}

In the afternoon of the previous day Jesus had worked the miracle of the multiplication of the loaves. The crowd, that had been anxiously seeking him, finally found him on the other side of the sea of Tiberias. And a remarkable dialogue begins: You seek me, not because you saw signs, but because you ate your fill of the loaves. Do not labour for the food which perishes, but for the food which endures to eternal life, which the Son of man will give to you.1 Our Lord’s words give us a glimpse of the mystery of the Eucharist, the Sacrament of his Body and Blood. He says: the bread which I shall give for the life of the world is my flesh.2 This promise, which the Jews rejected as scandalous, would become reality on the night of Maundy Thursday, in the intimacy of the Cenacle, in front of the disciples who had believed that only he had the words of eternal life.3

The sacramental sacrifice of the Body and Blood of Christ God wanted to establish a sacrament, which would be both a sacrifice and a memorial of his Passion, where his Body and Blood would be food under the species of bread and wine. “For it is clear that, just as generation is required for corporeal life since thereby man

1 Jn 6:26-27 2 Jn 6:51 3 Jn 6:68

The Holy Eucharist 261

receives life, and growth whereby man is brought to maturity, likewise food is required for the preservation of life. Consequently, just as for the spiritual life there had to be Baptism, which is spiritual generation, and Confirmation, which is spiritual growth, so there needed to be the Eucharist, which is spiritual food.”4 Our Lord’s gift of himself to all his disciples is perpetuated in the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass. The heavenly Father invites us: Come, eat of my bread, and drink of the wine I have mixed.5 This food is the Body and the Blood of Christ, for “by the consecration of the bread and wine, the whole substance of the bread is changed into the substance of the Body of Christ our Lord and the whole substance of the wine is changed into the substance of his Blood.”6 It is natural (for otherwise the Mass would be incomplete) that the Church should have commanded that “as often as the priest immolates the Body and Blood of the Lord Jesus Christ on the altar, he ought to partake of them by communion… What kind of sacrifice would it be if the priest didn’t think he had a part in it?”7 The faithful, on the other hand, need not receive Communion, because the sacrifice has already been completed on the altar. But even so, whoever participates in the eucharistic banquet unites himself better to the homage of praise, thanksgiving, satisfaction and reparation which Christ, together with the whole Church, offers to the Father at Mass. This is why it is so recommendable to go to the banquet table, at least spiritually, whenever we attend the holy Sacrifice. Qui manducat meam carnem et bibit meum sanguinem in me manet, et ego in illo.8 He who eats my flesh and drinks my blood abides in me,

4 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.73, a.1 5 Prov 9:5 6 Council of Trent, Decr. De Eucharistia, chap 4, Dz 877 (1642) 7 Twelfth Council of Toledo, chap 5 8 Jn 6:57 262 Living in Christ

and I in him. He who receives the Sacrament in the state of grace, as well as participating in the fruits of the Mass, obtains special spiritual graces proper and specific to eucharistic Communion. He receives Christ himself, source of all graces, spiritually and physically, per quem haec omnia, Domine, semper bona creas, sanctificas, vivificas, benedicis, et praestas nobis.9 This is why the Eucharist is the greatest sacrament, the centre and the summit of the whole sacramental order. For “whereas the Eucharist contains something which is sacred absolutely, namely, Christ himself, the baptismal water contains something which is sacred in relation to something else, namely, the sanctifying power. And the same holds true for the chrism and the other sacraments.”10

Supernatural efficacy of the Eucharist The real presence of Christ – Christus passus, the Christ who suffered, says St Thomas11 – gives the Eucharist an infinite supernatural effectiveness. When we want to give ourselves to others, we can give them objects which belong to us, our knowledge, our love… But we always find a limit. In the Eucharist, God’s omnipotence surpasses all human limitations and under the form of bread and wine he gives himself to us entirely, making us “one body and one blood with him.”12 He unites us to himself, identifying us with him. “You will not convert me into you, as the food of your flesh, but you will change yourself into me.”13 Love reaches its ideal in the Eucharistic Sacrament: identification with the loved one, fusing two into one, melting together, compenetrating one another. “As when you join two pieces of wax and melt them in a flame, the two become one thing, so too, through participation in the Body of

9 Order of the Mass 10 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.73, a.1 ad.3 11 Cf Ibid a.3 ad.3 12 St Cyril of Jerusalem, Catecheses 22, 1 13 St Augustine, Confessions 7, 10

The Holy Eucharist 263

Christ and in his precious Blood, he unites himself to us and we unite ourselves to him.”14 It is a union that assimilates us more and more to Christ, and through total identification with the suffering Christ, it leads to the greatest heights of sanctity, as the Church sings: O God who, through the communion of this venerable Sacrifice, make us partakers of the supreme Godhead.15 As well as identifying us with Christ, Holy Communion is food and “this sacrament does for the spiritual life all that material food and drink do for bodily life, namely sustain, increase, restore, and delight.”16 The Eucharist sustains our energies on our long journey towards God. It makes the yoke sweet and the burden light.17 It protects us against dangers, against vacillations, which try to separate us from the way, and it quickens our pace. It gives us that by which we truly live.18 Each Communion brings a new wealth of grace, a light and an impulse which gives us charity and strength for the spiritual struggle, although at times we may not perceive it. Besides, like every other food, the Eucharist is enjoyable: May your holy mysteries inspire us with a divine fervour that may make us delight both in their reception and in their fruits.19 It is not a sensible pleasure, completely accessory, which may at times be present. It is rather the joy of having received Christ, the infinite good, the source of all true joy. He who eats my flesh and drinks my blood has eternal life, and I will raise him up at the last day.20 As the sacrament of love – “sacramentum

14 St Cyril of Alexandria, In Ioannem Ev. commentarium 10, 2 15 Eighteenth Sunday after Pentecost, Prayer over the Gifts (the reference here, and in notes 18, 19 and 26 is to the Missal in use prior to 1970) 16 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.79, a.1 17 Cf Mt 11:30 18 Sixth Sunday after Epiphany, Prayer after Communion 19 Ember Saturday of Pentecost, Prayer after Communion 20 Jn 6:54 264 Living in Christ

caritatis”21 – the Eucharist burns out the impurities of the soul. It purifies it of its venial faults. It sows in it the seed of eternal life. “For as the bread of this earth, receiving the invocation of God, is no longer ordinary bread, but the Eucharist… so too our bodies, in receiving the Eucharist are no longer corruptible, but have the hope of the resurrection.”22 Finally, in uniting us to Christ, Communion is the sacrament of unity. Because there is one bread, we who are many are one body, for we all partake of the one bread.23 This personal, intimate and operative union with the Head explains and gives the raison d’etre for the unity of all those who form part of the Mystical Body, and it strengthens that union. For without this union of each soul with Christ in the Eucharist, there is not, nor can there be, a vital union among Christians. Union is manifested in charity, in deeds of love towards our brothers in the faith, above all differences of race, nation, language, and social status. For “who can divide and separate from their natural union those who have been intimately united with Christ through that one holy body?”24

Dispositions for receiving Communion Nevertheless, the degree to which we share in these benefits of the Eucharist depends on the quality of our internal dispositions, because “the sacraments of the new law, while acting ‘ex opere operato’, also produce a greater effect in so far as the conditions in which they are received are more perfect.”25 We need habitual dispositions of soul and body, with desires for purification. We should go to sacramental confession whenever necessary, or even only advisable, in order to

21 St Thomas, S. Th., III, q.73, a.3 ad.3 22 St Ireneus, Adversus haereses 4, 18, 5 23 1 Cor 10:17 24 St Cyril of Alexandria, In Ioannem Ev. commentarium 11, 11 25 St Pius X, Dec. Sacra Tridentina Synodus, 20 December 1905

The Holy Eucharist 265

receive Christ worthily. Thus we will always treat this sacrament with sincere respect… receiving it with a great spirit of faith.26 Then comes that loving, simple, and careful preparation of a whole day lived in the presence of God, the struggle to fulfil our daily tasks as well as possible. When we make a mistake, we feel the need to go to our Lord. And we should give thanks in the way the Father told us: May our whole life be thanksgiving for having received him, and preparation for receiving him again.27 This is normal for a soul in love: in our work and family life, in everything we do, our heart should be placed in God. Consequently, when our soul is in the state of grace, and is in love with our Lord, we should never think that we are not really ready to receive Holy Communion. For while we are working, opening up other fronts in this war of peace and goodness in the world, we are preparing ourselves marvellously well.28 As the moment for receiving Communion approaches, our desire to prepare ourselves should increase. We are going to receive this holy victim, this spotless victim, the holy Bread of eternal life and the Chalice of everlasting salvation.29 And we should have greater desires to prepare ourselves in a worthy and appropriate way during the night period, filling it with spiritual communions. We also prepare ourselves in the morning prayer in our intimate conversation with our Lord in the Tabernacle, and with a disposition to take part actively in the Mass, truly striving to approach the altar with devotion. We have to receive him like some important celebrity: with decorations, with lights, with new clothes. And if you ask what cleanliness, what decorations and what lights you should have, let me tell you: cleanliness in your senses, ony by one; adornment in your faculties, one by one; and light in your

26 Fourth Sunday of Lent, Prayer after Communion 27 From our Father, Meditation, 28 May 1964 28 From our Father, Meditation, 28 May 1964 29 Order of the Mass 266 Living in Christ

whole soul.30 Together with the dispositions of the soul, there are those of the body: the fast the Church asks of us as a sign of respect and reverence; the posture and dress which help us to approach the Father’s banquet as worthy children, living a refined piety which is respect, charity and good example. And everything out of love, never letting routine set in. Have you ever thought how you would prepare to receive Jesus if you could only go to Holy Communion once in your life? When I was young and frequent Communion wasn’t very common, people used to prepare themselves very carefully before receiving. First, with a good confession. Then, with a new suit, if they could; clean from head to toe. They prepared soul and body, like people in love. Nowadays we have to be grateful to our Lord for having made it so easy for us to go to Communion. But the way to thank him is to prepare ourselves very well to receive him.31

Thanksgiving after Mass If there has been a careful preparation to receive the Eucharist, the thanksgiving will flow fervently from our soul, and we will try to make good use of those minutes during which Jesus remains within us with his Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity. My children, I see Jesus in the Eucharist as a King, as a Doctor, as a Teacher, and as a Friend. He is a King, and he wants to reign in your Christian heart, as a son of God. Tell him: Yes, that you want to serve him with all your soul… He is a Doctor, and he comes to cure your ailments, your illnesses. But you have to tell him the truth: I have this inclination, I feel these

30 From our Father, Meditation, 14 April 1960 31 From our Father, Meditation, 14 April 1960

The Holy Eucharist 267

symptoms… We shouldn’t hide anything from the divine Doctor. You have to show him your wounds, and if you notice that pride is whispering to you to hide something, say to him: Lord, you who have cured so many souls, make me see you in the Host as the divine Doctor! He is a Teacher. Tell him: teach me to love. Do you remember, when we were children, how the teacher used to take our hand, as we wrote, to help us make the letters? And when we let ourselves be guided, they turned out so well! Tell him: Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament, help me to write, for I am drawing my first letters in this divine story. May I let myself be led. He is a Friend, a Friend who gives his life, for love. My son, your life is not yours, it doesn’t belong to you, we cannot keep it for ourselves. You belong to Jesus. Feel proud to be his friend, and, as a friend, offer him confidently all you have. He understands you. He, who wept for Lazarus, understands you. Tell him: Jesus, I don’t want to make you weep.32 We should have a deep and tender gratitude for so many blessings we have received, but especially for our Lord’s visit in the Host. Let our gratitude be that of sons: There he is: King of Kings, and Lord of Lords, hidden in the Bread. To this extreme has he humbled himself through love for you.33 We don’t need to express our gratitude in words. Sometimes it can be just an attitude, a thought: What would I be if I had not gone to Communion?34 And along with gratitude, petition; because we must not miss this opportunity when we have Him, the source of all graces, so close to us. You think there is something wrong because, in your thanksgiving after

32 From our Father, Meditation, 14 April 1960 33 The Way, 538 34 Ibid, 534 268 Living in Christ

Communion, the first thing you find yourself doing, without being able to help it, is asking: Jesus, give me this: Jesus, that soul: Jesus, that undertaking… Don’t worry, and don’t try to force yourself: when the father is good and the child simple and daring, don’t you see how the little lad puts his hand into his father’s pocket, looking for sweets, before greeting him with a kiss? Well then…35 To prepare ourselves fittingly to receive our Lord in the Eucharist, and to get all the fruit possible out of Communion, let us go to Mary, Mother of God, in order to receive him with the purity, humility, and devotion with which she received him.

35 Ibid, 896

## MARY, MEDIATRIX OF GRACE {#cuaderno.3.28}

When his hour had come, Jesus said: ‘It is finished’; and he bowed his head, and gave up his spirit.1 Jesus Christ, the Incarnate God, died on a cross, abandoned by everyone, victim of love for the sins of men. Through his death he attained life for us, with the co-redemptive help of his Mother, the Blessed Virgin. And from heaven, where he now always lives to make intercession2 for us, he applies the saving grace he won with his Blood, to each soul, through the sacraments entrusted to the Church. You know that you were ransomed… not with perishable things such as silver or gold, but with the precious blood of Christ, like that of a lamb without blemish or spot.3

The maternal mediation of the Blessed Virgin Our Lord, by the mercy of his divine providence, wills that all grace, which belongs to him alone by a proper and exclusive right, be distributed to us by the hands of his Mother, the Blessed Virgin. For “if the Word truly works miracles and infuses graces by means of the humanity he assumed, if he makes use of the sacraments and his saints as instruments for the salvation of souls, why should he not be able to make use of the action of his most holy Mother in distributing the fruits

1 Jn 19:30 2 Heb 7:25 3 1 Pet 1:18-19 270 Living in Christ

of the redemption?”4 And God, who certainly could do so, willed it and did it. No one has more reason or title to be dispenser of God’s grace than Mary. Being the Mother of God, she touches the frontiers of the divinity and is the only creature possessing the fullness of grace; this gives her a special aptitude for administering God’s treasures. In dependence on her Son she co-redeemed, gaining for us, with her own merits, the help and graces necessary for our salvation. As St Thomas says: “It is only right that the person who has acquired goods be able to distribute them.”5 So closely united to Christ by the bonds of maternity and by a singular communion of spirit, Mary participates, far more than the angels and saints, in the royal power of governing and leading men to their heavenly fatherland. “She derives such radiant grandeur from her union with Christ the King that her excellence surpasses all created things. From this union with Christ is born the royal power by which Mary is able to dispense the treasures of the kingdom of the divine Redeemer. The inexhaustible efficacy of her maternal intercession before her Son and before the Father also has its origin in her union with Christ.”6 Mary’s motherly love for all men, whom she has spiritually engendered in Christ, makes her worthy of such a singular mission in the economy of grace. A mother’s role involves both giving birth and nurturing the new-born child. Mary helps us with loving solicitude from heaven, guiding us to mature manhood, to the measure of the stature of the fullness of Christ.7 For all these reasons, “it can be truly and rightly affirmed that absolutely nothing of the great treasure of grace won by Jesus Christ…

4 Pius XII, Encyclical Ad caeli Reginam, 11 October 1954 5 St Thomas, Compendium Theologiae, 241 6 Pius XII, Encyclical Ad caeli Reginam, 11 October 1954 7 Eph 4:13

Mary, Mediatrix of all graces 271

is given to us except through Mary, according to the will of God Himself. And just as no one can come to the Father except through the Son, in almost the same way, no one can come to Christ except through Mary.”8

All graces come to us through Mary On earth, God used Mary’s maternal mediation to sanctify John the Precursor in the womb of Elizabeth. Mary’s cousin exclaims: When the voice of your greeting came to my ears, the babe in my womb leaped for joy.9 And in Cana it is our Lady’s request – they have no wine10 – that moves Jesus to perform his first miracle. It was a material prodigy that bore spiritual fruit: Jesus converted six jars of water into wine, and his disciples believed in him,11 adds the Evangelist at the end of his narration. Our Lady obtains all graces and distributes them to us through her powerful intercession before her Son. How can the Trinity not listen to her who is the Mother of God, Daughter of God and Spouse of God?12 Jesus can deny nothing to his Mother, who carried him in her womb, gave birth to him, and was always with him, from Nazareth right to the humiliation and sorrow of the Cross. And for this reason our Lady has been called all-powerful in her supplication. From Mary’s maternal love spring forth the torrential waters of grace, bringing God’s gifts to all men, because for her we are her beloved sons. Her most sweet Heart rejects no one. “She is rest for those who labour, consolation for those who weep, medicine for the sick, port for those tossed about by the tempest, pardon for sinners, sweet balm for the sorrowful, help for those who pray.”13

8 Leo XIII, Encyclical Octobri mense, 22 September 1891 9 Lk 1:44 10 Jn 2:3 11 Jn 2:11 12 From our Father, Crónica, August 1965, p. 7 13 St John Damascene, Encom. in Dormit. B. Mariae V.

272 Living in Christ

All graces, great and small, have come to us through the hands of Mary. “Your favours are innumerable. No one finds salvation except through you, holy Virgin. No one is freed from evil except through you. No one can find forgiveness except through you.”14 God has granted us the sovereign grace of our vocation through her intercession. A glance from his Mother may have moved him to the point of calling you to the Work, through the immaculate hand of the Blessed Virgin, our Lady.15 Gratitude to our Lady constantly wells up in our soul for the inexhaustible shower of graces that God grants us through Mary. We also feel secure and confident, firm and serene in our struggle to spread the kingdom of God. Who are we going to rely on, if not our Mother, so powerful before her Son?

Marian piety The universal mediation of Mary is therefore part of the legacy of our faith. It is a pledge of sure hope, an incentive to love, the channel through which our life of piety flows. Mary is the advocate for our hopes and desires of sanctity and apostolate. We abandon ourselves in her hands, asking her to enlighten us, to sustain us throughout our life, so that we work aflame with love, and live a life of love for God, the Church and all souls. My children, our Father insisted, invoke the Blessed Virgin confidently with a heart-felt cry. Never forget that she has been our great protector, our great recourse since 2 October 1928, and even before that.16 Trusting in the all-powerful intercession of our Lady, we have learned to ask her for the Work and for the Father, and for the sanctity of all our brothers and sisters. Cor Mariae Dulcissimum, iter para

14 St Germanus of Constantinople, Com. in S. Mariae Zonam 15 From our Father, Meditation With the docility of clay, 3 November 1955 16 From our Father, Crónica, August 1965, p. 9

Mary, Mediatrix of all graces 273

tutum! We ask her to prepare a safe way for us. We ask her to make us faithful, loyal, sincere and, above all, to gain for us the grace never to leave her Son. Don’t you want to ask the Mother of God, who is your Mother and mine, to place us under her protection, in the Heart of Jesus Christ? 17 We also ask her to teach us how to talk to Jesus, how to call on his most sweet name lovingly, how to fall in love with her Son with the strength of a young and noble heart. We ask her: Mother, take us under your protection: sub tuum praesidium. We have grown up under the protection of your mantle, as little children grow in the arms of their mother. Always teach us in Opus Dei how to stay close to and love your Son.18 Grateful for all the gifts she has obtained for us as dispenser of heavenly graces, we promise her, with her help, to be faithful to our vocation as apostles, in spite of our personal weaknesses. Holy Mary, Mother of God and our Mother, you know so well the miseries of men, your children. Holy Mary, suppliant Power, we ask pardon for our life: for what should have been light and has been darkness, for what should have been strength and has been slackness, for what should have been on fire and has been lukewarm instead. We know our life has been of little value: help us to change, sharing, as your children, the good family likeness.19

Call on Mary Our heart overflows when we consider the largesse with which the Blessed Virgin distributes the graces bestowed on her by her Son, and we share her desire to bring all men the boundless riches Christ won for

17 From our Father, Crónica, August 1965, p. 9 18 From our Father, Crónica, August 1965, p. 9 19 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 63 274 Living in Christ

us. We ardently ask the Queen of Heaven, in our prayer and work, to continue, as Mater Ecclesiae – Mother of the Church, protecting and guiding the Church, Peter’s boat which has sailed the earth’s seas for so many centuries. We especially ask her for the Pope, il dolce Cristo in terra, the sweet Christ on earth, as he was called by St Catherine of Siena and in words our Father lovingly used: to enlighten him, to help him defend the Church with the strength of a mother and father against enemies both inside and out. We beseech her, as Regina Christianorum – Queen of Christians, to enkindle in us an ever-greater desire to bring all men to the feet of her Son. We ask her that, like the woman in the Gospel parable, she may take us into her hands and turn us into good leaven in the very heart of the world. Under the protective mantle of our heavenly Mother, our Father told us, you who are a little yeast or leaven will ferment the whole mass of mankind. Then you will feel the same anxiety that brought me to write: Omnes – everyone! May not even a single soul be lost! – cum Petro ad Iesum per Mariam.20 We especially entrust to her protection, as Regina Apostolorum – Queen of Apostles, those souls in which a vocation as an apostle can take root. We know that the calling to the Work is a most special grace. But what can a mere word or glance from our Lady not win? “Heed us, O holy Mother of Christ, for your Son honours you by not denying you anything.”21 We ask her, finally, for the world, the immense field entrusted to us by God to sanctify with our work. Mary, Regina mundi – Queen of the world, will give shape to the eager desires we carry in our heart. We are not frightened by the size of the task nor does our littleness deter us. We do not fear the devil’s attacks, since God, through our Mother, will

20 From our Father, Meditation With the docility of clay, 3 November 1955 21 Innocent III, Serm. in Nativ. B. Mariae V.

Mary, Mediatrix of all graces 275

give us the necessary graces. We know that, in her hands, we can already offer God a sanctified and redeemed world in which Christ reigns from the summit of all human activities. “Mary is always the path leading to Christ. Every encounter with her can result only in an encounter with Christ himself. For what other reason do we continually turn to Mary than to seek Christ in her arms, seeking our Redeemer in her, through her and with her?”22 We have every reason for turning to Mary always, assured that we will be heard every time. We remind her “that never was it known that anyone who fled to your protection, implored your help, and sought your intercession was left unaided. Inspired by this confidence, I fly to you, O Virgin of virgins… O Mother of the Word Incarnate, despise not my petitions.”23 She is safety. She is hope. She is the Mother of Fair Love. She is the source and the seat of wisdom. And she, the Virgin Mother, the Mediatrix of all graces, will lead us by the hand to her Son, Jesus. My children, when you are happy and when you are sad; when your faults are less apparent and when they weigh most upon you: always turn to Mary, because she will never abandon you. Sancta Maria, Regina Operis Dei, ora pro nobis.24

22 Paul VI, Encyclical Mense Maio, 29 April 1965 23 Memorare prayer 24 From our Father, Crónica, August 1965, p. 11

## PROSELYTISM {#cuaderno.3.29}

Don’t doubt it: your vocation is the greatest grace God could have given you. Thank him for it.1 The first reason that urges us to seek new vocations is the knowledge of what our divine vocation is worth. It is an instance of God’s loving predilection, which makes us happy, even on earth. Since that calling is a gift, and a gift man cannot attain by himself but must receive, we feel obliged to seek new apostles. A vocation is not the fruit of a person’s own merits or the result of a mere human decision. You did not choose me, but I chose you and appointed you that you should go and bear fruit and that your fruit should abide; so that whatever you ask the Father in my name, he may give it to you.2

The right to win vocations As a vocation is a calling it must resound in the ears of the person chosen by God. Moreover, God as a rule addresses a soul by making use of other people as instruments. Jesus insisted on this teaching time and again. In the various parables about the kingdom of Heaven – the wedding feast, the rented vineyard… – Christ shows his will to use instruments to call men to follow him. He also showed it by the way he received the first Apostles, after meeting John and Andrew, and by the

1 The Way, 913 2 Jn 15:16

Proselytism 277

different missions of his disciples. This is natural, since man by his very nature needs his fellow men to teach him and help him. Sociability, a natural condition, is taken up and raised to a supernatural level by grace. Sanctification is not a purely personal activity, with everyone relying only on his or her own resources. Our vocation did not come about by chance: it was born, or at least strengthened, with the help of another person already called by God. We are members of Christ, participating in his redemptive mission, and God makes use of us in calling souls. But because these souls are also members of Christ, what was a possibility becomes an obligation and a right. Why am I going to involve myself in the lives of others? Because, as a Christian, I am obliged! Because Christ has involved himself in your life, and in mine! 3 We have a right to interfere in the lives of others because their life is our life: because if one member is weak or even dead, the whole body suffers, Christ suffers and the healthy members suffer. “For all men are one man in Christ, and the unity of Christians constitutes only one man.”4 And St Augustine adds, as if to close the circle of the affirmation, “and this man is all men, and all men are this man, for all are one, since Christ is one.”5 The ‘you’ and the ‘I’, though separated and strangers to each other, are united in Christ. So charity is no longer the expression of an occasional or sporadic generosity, but a condition of life; because if our life is Christ, where there is disunity, there is death. There is a pressing need for proselytism.6 Time is passing, life is slipping away. Don’t you long to shout to those young men and women 3 From our Father, Get-together, 14 February 1960 4 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos 39 5 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos 127 6 To those who attribute a negative connotation to the word ‘proselytism’ one would have to say that this could never apply to the apostolate of the faithful of the Work, because it is never aggressive or violent but peace-loving and extremely respectful of people’s freedom. (Catechism of the Work, 287, 8th edition)

278 Living in Christ

all around you: Fools, leave those worldly things that shackle the heart and very often degrade it…, leave all that and come with us in search of Love?7 Making use of us as his instruments, Christ comes close to our friends, one of the people we work with or perhaps someone we meet on a journey. Will Christ pass by this person again? And it is perhaps his sanctity, and the sanctity of others who are or will be close to him, that is now at stake. Can there be any excuse for not trying to make him realise this? We look for new vocations out of a desire for God’s glory and charity for souls. “The people who work for God seek to increase their love, not their personal profit; they serve with the zeal of their charity and the desire to advance in virtue; they try to win souls for God and do all they can to take others with them to the vineyard.”8

The duty to win vocations For the same reason that we have a right to do proselytism, we also have a duty. First of all because we are Christians, and Christians who have been chosen to follow Jesus, who have the treasure of his grace and his doctrine. This is a wealth that we have on loan and which we have to return with interest. Because “he who possesses the word of wisdom and does not wish to use it for the benefit of his neighbour, is like one who puts his money in a bag and keeps it tied up. Of these it is written: Of what use is a hidden store of wisdom and a hidden treasure? (Eccli 41:17).”9 There is no love of God where there is no zeal for souls; and there is no desire for sanctity where there is no desire to spread it. It is not possible to find God if there is no concern that others should also find

7 The Way, 790 8 St Gregory the Great, Hom. in Evang. 19 9 St Gregory the Great, Hom. in Evang. 17. For Eccli 41:17, cf Sir 41:14 (RSV)

Proselytism 279

him and know him as fully as possible. Only where the doctrine of Christ has been corrupted can it be thought that one can ignore the sanctity of others without endangering one’s own. This right and this duty, which every Christian has, is accentuated in our case. For us proselytism is even more vital, since the aim of the Work is to promote the fullness of Christian life among persons of all social classes. This is why God has wanted the Work: to spread the universal call to holiness. Sanctity and proselytism are two sides of the same coin; they cannot be separated. It was God’s will that there be Christians for whom proselytism is an opportunity for growing in sanctity, so that spreading sanctity is not something added to their vocation, but an essential part of it. Hence the fundamental importance that this task has for each of us. A member of the Work without a concern to seek new vocations would show that he did not have the spirit of Opus Dei, whose aim is precisely to spread sanctity in the world. That is why each member of the Work ought to be like a burning coal who spreads fire wherever he is, or who at least raises the spiritual temperature of those around him, leading them to live an intense Christian life.10 And so proselytism is a natural consequence of the St Raphael and St Gabriel work in such a way that they are the seedbed of vocations for Opus Dei. You have no alternative but to seek new vocations, our Father told us. He made it clear that in the Work even the sick have the same obligation. No limitation (of age, position, status or any other circumstance) can dispense from this holy duty. We have to do a great deal of proselytism, and always. If we had no concern to seek new vocations it would give the impression that we were not happy. A man who is happy, who has the good, does his best to give it to others.11

10 From our Father, Crónica, May 1960, p. 9 11 From our Father, Crónica, May 1960, p. 10 280 Living in Christ

We have to do proselytism because God wants to make use of us, freely, to call others to him. The members of Opus Dei have the right and the duty to pose the vocational problem – ascende superius! (Lk 14:10) – and to provoke this crisis among all kinds of people who give signs of a possible vocation. In this way there will be no souls who can say that they have not striven for sanctity because no one has called them – quia nemo nos conduxit (Mt 20:7).12 We have to seek new vocations because, although many vocations come, thank God, we need to increase greatly in numbers. We have to proselytise because that is what we came to the Work for. Not to be concerned with proselytism would be a symptom of laziness, of sickness and perhaps of death. Zeal to win vocations. Set fire to everything around you! And ignite distant things as well, through prayer, sacrifice and the smiling fulfilment of your duty.13

The means This zeal for proselytism has to show itself in a way that is specific and practical. Every week we ask ourselves: Have we shown with deeds our zeal for winning new vocations? And so it is necessary to make our zeal for proselytism more specific, to direct it towards particular people, to aim at practical results. Sometimes it happens that our effort does not bring us the joy of seeing a new vocation: God will always see to it that the seed we scatter bears fruit in other places. But usually the number of vocations corresponds to the reality of our effort. Therefore not a single year should go by without each of us winning at least one vocation. This has to be our aim, our desire; it is the goal of our efforts, of our love for God and for souls. To make our zeal for souls yield results, we must use the necessary

12 From our Father, Crónica, May 1960, p. 10 13 Alone with God, 271

Proselytism 281

divine and human means. As the enterprise is supernatural, it follows that the fundamental means must always be supernatural. We must use divine means, asking the help of our King in every enlistment of new apostles in his army.14 We must consider slowly in our prayer the endeavour we are engaged in; we have to pray insistently for the people for whom we want to win a vocation from God. And we must ensure that others keep the same intention in mind, because prayer is the most effective means of winning new apostles.15 We have to do special mortifications for these souls, and ask others to help us. We have to seek help from the Guardian Angels of the friends with whom we are dealing. We must do apostolate following the specific, clear resolutions we have consulted in the chat. But after these more directly supernatural means, the apostle, though quite convinced that he can do nothing by himself if he does not obtain abundant grace from Heaven by prayer and sacrifice,16 has to make use of all the noble human means at his disposal. He has to exercise all his human qualities so that they also become instruments of the action of God.

Apostolic zeal Our zeal for proselytism is born from deep within, and it affects us right through. It is not bureaucratic, impersonal, something done as a mere obligation, without warmth and affection. Quite the contrary: it makes us burn with love, and is a task we find attractive. This burning love for God and souls is the atmosphere in which our proselytism prospers. You lack drive. That’s why you sway so few. You don’t seem very

14 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934, 10 15 The Way, 800 16 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934, 12 282 Living in Christ

convinced of what you gain by giving up those things of the earth for Christ. Just compare: a hundredfold and life everlasting! Would you call that a poor bargain?17 Insipid proselytism, without enthusiasm or joyful persistence, is doomed to failure before it begins. We must have drive, so that souls will truly catch the flames of a conversation enkindled with the fire of God’s glory. One of the immediate consequences of this drive is to provoke an opportunity to speak with someone, instead of waiting for it to present itself. Whatever our personal position may be, whatever our age, wherever we are, we must always hear our Father urging us on: Tell me: there, where you are… mightn’t there be one… or two, who could understand us well?18 All kinds of people can fit in Opus Dei, all with the same divine vocation. The Work is a divine and human family, which is similar to natural families that God has blessed abundantly with many children: some of them are taller, some shorter, some darker, some fairer. And then there are the relatives: some closer, others more distant, and there are friends of the family. My children, exactly the same thing happens in the Work: the Numeraries, Associates and Supernumeraries; the Associates and Supernumeraries of the Priestly Society of the Holy Cross, we all form one single family, all with the same identical vocation. And moreover, close to us are those relatives, the Cooperators, whom we love so much. And then, there are so many friends and colleagues who in some way or other take part in our family.19

17 The Way, 791 18 Ibid, 805 19 From our Father, Meditation, 5 March 1963

Proselytism 283

Everyone we are close to can receive the spirit of the Work, and benefit from it. Perhaps they will not immediately receive the calling from God, and will need a long period of preparation. But to all our Father addressed these words: I see you, my sons, like Peter and John in times gone by, gazing full of compassion on friends and acquaintances, who could come to fight by our side … if they were not lame. Do as Peter and John did: when you go to the prayer, keep those friends and acquaintances very much in mind, and then with your example, tell them: respice in nos! – look at us! And, if you are persevering, if you are tenacious – tenacity is indispensable for proselytism – the moment will come when you can cry out to them: In nomine Iesu Christi Nazareni, surge et ambula! (Acts 3:1-10), in the name of Jesus of Nazareth, arise and walk! 20 We need drive and enthusiasm; we also need to be persevering and constant. We must hold tenaciously to our initial enthusiasm, the last day with as much drive as the first. We must have an outlook which becomes more and more supernatural, and with at least the same human warmth. If we are proselytistic in this way, the fruit is assured. There may be a short or a long delay – more likely a short one – but it will come. God has guaranteed it by giving our vocation this aim. Therefore our Father could say: If there are no vocations, there is no love for God. Is that clear? Proselytism! We have the happy experience so often, in so many places… On the other hand, the proselytism that we do will increase the fire in our hearts and help us see the supernatural beauty of our mission more clearly. Who does not feel the joy and the need of intensifying his effort when he sees that the seed sown by himself has borne the fruit of a vocation? “Two rewards are due for each task we do: one while we

20 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934, 57-58 284 Living in Christ

are still on our journey, and one when we have reached our homeland; one which sustains us at our work, and one which is given to us as our reward at the resurrection.”21 Every vocation we obtain improves our ability to do effective proselytism, increases our love and assures our sanctity. Proselytism springs from love and is, at the same time, a cause of love. The search for fellow-apostles. It is the unmistakable sign of true zeal.22 May our Lady, Regina Apostolorum, keep our zeal always enkindled and make it divinely fruitful.

21 St Gregory the Great, Hom. in Evang. 17 22 The Way, 793

## EVERYTHING YOU ASK FOR {#cuaderno.3.30}

Pater… pro eis rogo… pro his quos dedisti mihi.1 Jesus prays to the Father for us, for those the Father has given to him. He, who is the Word, does not need to ask for anything. But he prayed at the Last Supper with the Twelve to give them a clear lesson of our need, to teach us to ask. God our Father well knows that every instant of our existence is the fruit of his largesse, that our need is absolute, that we have to receive everything and possess nothing of our own. God, who is our Father, who loves us as his children, constantly and tirelessly showers on us the treasure of his graces and gifts. But precisely because he loves us so much, he wants us to admit our dependence, our poverty. He wants us to ask, to direct our petitions to our heavenly Father. This leads us to acknowledge that we have nothing and have to receive everything as a gift, a grace to which we have no right. It also helps us understand God's delicate love in wanting us to go to him with our prayer and petition. “God wants to be asked, to be coerced, to be won over by a certain effrontery.”2 When the disciples say to Christ: Domine, doce nos orare,3 teach us to pray, Jesus gives us a simple formula with a strong accent on filial

1 Jn 17:9 2 St Gregory the Great, Ps. 6 Paenit., 2 3 Lk 11:1 286 Living in Christ

confidence, where the desires for God's glory are intertwined with petitions for our spiritual and material needs. And asking boldly, since we know how much we are loved: panem nostrum cotidianum da nobis hodie,4 give us this day our daily bread…

Prayer of petition To induce us to ask with this prayer of petition which is so necessary for us, Jesus left us all types of assurances, showing us the conditions for our prayer to be good. He gives reasons and examples to help us understand: What father among you, if his son asks for a fish, will instead of a fish give hive him a serpent; or if he asks for an egg, will give him a scorpion? If you then, who are evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will the heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to those who ask him!5 Our prayer should be trusting and serene. That same trust leads us to persevere in our petition, certain that in the end we will receive, since this is why we are asking. “I asked once, twice, thrice, ten times, twenty times and I didn’t receive anything. My brother, do not stop until you have received what you ask for. The goal of petition is to receive the gift. Stop asking only when you have received it; don’t stop even then, but keep on praying. If you do not receive what you want, ask to receive. When you have received it, give thanks for having received it.”6 Keep on asking, like the untimely friend who did not have bread, like the defenceless widow who cried out day and night to the unjust judge. Ask insistently, because Jesus encourages us not to cease: Ask, and it will be given you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you. For everyone who asks receives, and he who seeks,
