4 Lk 11:3 5 Lk 11:11-13 6 St Augustine, In dimiss. Chan. serm. 10

Everything you ask for 287

finds, and to him who knocks it will be opened.7 The prayer of a righteous man has great power in its effects.8 It can do so much because Christ directs our prayer and the Holy Spirit enkindles it in our souls, even when we don't know what we should ask for. He who grants every gift asks along with us so that it be granted to us. Is there room for greater certainty of receiving? God our Father stands at the right hand of the poor man,9 his quick largesse has no likeness on earth. He is so close to us, he awaits our petition with such love that you shall cry, and he will say: Here I am!10 God's great mercy cannot be captured even by the image of parental love on earth. “What father would listen like this to his sons and grandsons? What mother would be so ready if her little sons were to call? No one, neither father nor mother: only God.”11 We all have had the experience recounted by the Psalmist: From his temple he heard my voice, and my cry to him reached his ears.12

Unity of intentions When we pray united, our prayer is especially effective; it is heard by God with special attention and joy. Again I say to you, if two of you agree on earth about anything they ask, it will be done for them by my Father in heaven. For where two or three are gathered in my name, there I am in the midst of them.13 And Christ's prayer is the very omnipotence of God. Hence, “when you see that prayer has not been taken from you, feel safe, for neither has his mercy been taken from you.”14

7 Lk 11:9-10 8 Jas 5:16 9 Ps 109:31 10 Is 58:9 11 St Jerome, In Ev. Matthaeum comm. 55, 5 12 Ps 18:6 13 Mt 18:19-20 14 St Augustine, Enarrationes in Psalmos 65, 4 288 Living in Christ

Our united prayer of petition has been our constant recourse since the Work's beginning. Above all else, we are a large family that prays. Besides our daily Preces, we have lifted our hearts to God collectively many times when faced with difficulties, when we don't have the necessary resources, when we begin, are involved in and finish each task. Like the apostles, we are omnes pariter in eodem loco (Acts 2:1), all united together persevering in prayer, in sacrifice and in work.15 We have always won out, thanks to everyone's confident and persevering prayer. Obstacles of all sorts, getting important new endeavours under way, decisive steps of the Work of God on earth, have always been the occasion for the Father to channel our unanimous and ardent prayer towards a single intention. This is the formula our spirit offers for the success of every apostolate. The weapon of Opus Dei is not work, it is prayer. That is why we convert work into prayer and why we have a contemplative soul. We entrust everything to prayer. We turn to it to win vocations, because prayer is the most effective means for winning new apostles.16 And since the apostolate of action is the sweet-tasting fruit of prayer and sacrifice, action itself depends on prayer for its efficacy. And so do human resources. We ask for everything certain that prayer is always fruitful. Work and action have come after prayer and sacrifice, to show God our right intention in asking. The Work of God comes to fulfil the Will of God. Therefore, have the profound conviction that heaven is determined it should be accomplished.17 This conviction has led us to pray confidently, very united to our Father. And we have always ended by giving thanks,

15 From our Father, Instruction, 1 April 1934, 43 16 The Way, 800 17 From our Father, Instruction, 19 March 1934, 47

Everything you ask for 289

because God’s mercy has flowed over without measure. The path, travelled up to now by the Work, is sown with prayer: each step has been taken with prayer. And this will always be the way Opus Dei proceeds. That is why our Father wanted us to be very prayerful, channelling our unity and strength towards specific intentions so that God will receive the gentle coercion of his children's petition. So we continue to see yesterday’s petitions become today's realities. God himself placed these petitions of yesterday in our hearts when he decided the Work should come into existence. God himself placed our apostolic desires in our souls, and afterwards surpassed them, granting much more than we asked for. We joyfully unite ourselves to the channels our Father offered us for directing our petitions, and strive to ask continuously, often raising our hearts to God for help: cor unum et anima una.18 We should petition God always, without interruption, our soul in an attitude of supplication as God’s children, certain of receiving what is best. And this is the disposition that animates our entire life, gives impetus to our work and makes our way joyful, as our Father told us: What confidence must prayer give you, my children! Prayer, prayer is the system; and then, set to work calmly and cheerfully.19

18 Acts 4:32 19 Alone with God, 80
